# Mersenne exponents: a rule that lands only on them

> **In short:** The Mersenne exponents are the primes p with 2^p − 1 prime: 2, 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 19, 31, … Asked: a rule that steps from one to the next and touches nothing else. 1. Trial factors 2kq + 1 remove about half of the primes, but among the rest the share of exponents grows only like the logarithm of the factor bound: 1.8% → 4.1% below 10⁴, as the Wagstaff heuristic predicts (23.7 expected, 22 found). 2. In 110 tests, no arithmetic feature of the 51 exponents below 83,880,077 beats that heuristic; taken as plain integers, no step rule, formula, recurrence or digit pattern beats random sequences of the same growth, and the one integer congruence that hits them all (A366029) is a probable-prime test of 2^k − 1. About 2,000 more ideas from outside the known formulas (section 7), each chosen on the small exponents and tested on the large ones and on 401 exponents of 79 related families, find nothing either. 3. So each step must prove its prime: the next q whose 2^q − 1 survives trial factoring and passes Lucas–Lehmer. The page has it as transformation 142, as a list without a circle, and as a test of the known exponents alone.

**Page:** [prime.html](writeup/code/riemann/prime.html): transformation 142 "Next Mersenne exponent" (indicator "Mersenne exponents" counts the hits), and the panel "Mersenne exponents, one after another" at the bottom (Start, and "Only the known exponents"). [prime2.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime2.html) shows the exponents as plain numbers in 54 layouts (section 8 explains every pattern in the first 43; the 11 wave layouts carry their own checked notes) (square, spirals, trees, triangles, space-filling curves, rings, walks), each with a note on why its empty lines are empty, with twin primes, chains of primes in order, a line test that counts the exponents on any lattice line against the heuristic, and the forecasts of section 7.

**Code:** [code/mersenne/](writeup/code/mersenne/), rerun by [run_all.sh](writeup/code/mersenne/run_all.sh).

**Status:** the algorithm is proved (Lucas–Lehmer is a theorem; trial factors are exact). Hit rates, feature tests and timings are computed. The probability model is heuristic.

## 1. The question

132 (Golomb's formula) gives the next prime, and from 1 its chain hits the exponents 2, 3, 5, 7 and then misses 11, 23 and 29: 7 of its first 10 steps are exponents. The aim is 100%: every step a Mersenne exponent, none skipped.

## 2. A filter is not enough (computed; heuristic model)

Every factor of 2^q − 1 has the form 2kq + 1 with 2kq + 1 ≡ ±1 (mod 8) (theorem). Removing the primes with such a factor for k ≤ K ([tf.py](writeup/code/mersenne/tf.py), [rate.py](writeup/code/mersenne/rate.py), output [rate.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/rate.txt); deeper K from [touch/tftable.out](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/tftable.out)):

| K | primes < 10⁴ left | share that are exponents (22) | primes < 10⁵ left | share (28) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1,229 | 1.8% | 9,592 | 0.29% |
| 100 | 805 | 2.7% | 6,781 | 0.41% |
| 10⁴ | 596 | 3.7% | 5,211 | 0.54% |
| 10⁵ | 532 | 4.1% | 4,665 | 0.60% |
| 10⁷ | 428 | 5.1% | | |

The heuristic P(2^q − 1 prime | no factor below Q) ≈ e^γ·log₂(Q)/q predicts 23.7 exponents below 10⁴ (true 22) and 30.0 below 10⁵ (true 28). The share grows like log Q: reaching 50% needs no factor below about 2^(0.28·q), which is the work of a primality proof ([touch/bound.out](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/bound.out)).

## 3. No hidden structure (computed)

All 51 exponents, against all 4,882,467 primes below 83,880,077 (GIMPS: every exponent below it tested and verified, milestone page read on 15 November 2026). The null is P = e^γ·log₂(a·p)/p, a = 2 for p ≡ 3 and a = 6 for p ≡ 1 (mod 4). Output in [structure/results.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/structure/results.txt), from [analysis.py](writeup/code/mersenne/structure/analysis.py); an independent recheck (gmpy2 primes, plain integers, its own Poisson-binomial code) in [check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/structure/check.py) → [check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/structure/check.txt) reproduces every count, expectation and p-value.

- Calibration: 51 observed, 50.3 expected (a = 2/6), 47.6 (a = 2). By mod 4: 31 with p ≡ 1 (25.2 expected), 19 with p ≡ 3 (24.1). The data lean towards a = 6 (Bayes factor 1.9) without deciding it.
- 110 tests: p mod 3, 5, 7, 8, 12, 16, 24, 30, 60, 120; the forms 2^k ± 1 and 4^k ± 3; closeness to a power of 2; binary weight and runs; 2p + 1, p + 2, p − 2 prime; the spacing of log log p. Bonferroni threshold 4.5·10⁻⁴; the smallest p-value is 0.023, and 49% of simulated null sets have a smaller one.
- That smallest p-value belongs to a control that is a theorem: p ≡ 3 (mod 4) with 2p + 1 prime (p > 3) never gives an exponent (Euler: 2p + 1 divides 2^p − 1); 0 found, 3.74 expected. A class with no exponents at all becomes visible only once it would hold more than about 8 of them.
- The one feature found in the exponents below 10⁵ (2^k ± 1 or 4^k ± 3: 10 against 7.0) lies entirely at p ≤ 127 and has nothing to test above 10⁵.

## 4. How few tests per exponent (computed)

[touch/](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/): trial factoring to 10⁷ ([tfdeep.py](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/tfdeep.py)), P−1 run for real with gmpy2 ([pm1.py](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/pm1.py), tables [pm1table_1e4.out](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/pm1table_1e4.out), [pm1table_1e5.out](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/pm1table_1e5.out)), orderings ([order.out](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/order.out)).

- Best share of exponents among the Lucas–Lehmer tests: 22 of 350 (6.3%) below 10⁴ (trial factoring to 10⁷ plus P−1 to B1 = 10⁵), 28 of 3,931 (0.71%) below 10⁵.
- P−1 (B1 = 1000, B2 = 3000) costs more than the tests it saves below about 4·10⁴, and 0.42 and 0.30 Lucas–Lehmer tests per removal in [4·10⁴, 7·10⁴) and [7·10⁴, 10⁵).
- Ordering the candidates by their posterior probability is the ascending order (0 positions differ); ordering by the prior with the mod-4 factor finds the first 25 exponents in 895 tests instead of 946 but needs 3,557 instead of 3,392 for all 28.
- The only proofs that 2^q − 1 is composite are a factor or a full Lucas–Lehmer (or PRP) test of about q squarings. A base-2 Fermat test never rejects 2^q − 1 (theorem: 2^q ≡ 1 mod 2^q − 1 and q divides 2^q − 2). So a rule that touches only exponents would need a test nobody has; a complete search tests every prime that survives the factors.

## 5. The algorithm on the page (proved; times measured)

Next exponent after p: the next prime q with no factor 2kq + 1 for k ≤ 100,000·(q/7000)² (at least 1,000; sieved by the primes below 2^14, exact double arithmetic below 2^38), and Lucas–Lehmer s₀ = 4, s ← s² − 2 mod 2^q − 1, s_(q−2) = 0. Below q = 4,200 in BigInt; above, the squaring is an irrational-base FFT (Crandall–Fagin, as in GIMPS) in Float64Array, with the largest rounding error checked at every step (a step above 0.35 is redone at twice the length). Every zero found up to 50,000 (all of them in transformation 142 and in the list) is recomputed in exact BigInt. The readable source is [engine/best.js](writeup/code/mersenne/engine/best.js); the page holds the same functions without comments.

| q | BigInt, ns per squaring | FFT | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4,001 | 5,025 | 5,236 | 0.96 |
| 10,007 | 19,799 | 10,953 | 1.81 |
| 20,011 | 59,705 | 22,642 | 2.64 |
| 30,011 | 106,020 | 34,213 | 3.10 |

On the page (headless Chrome, one worker; a computer with more cores runs several tests at once):

- **142**: every arrow lands on an exponent; destinations up to 150,000. Only the primes inside the circle are tested; above 3,000 in Web Workers, and the page redraws when they finish. Circles of 100 numbers: from 19,900 in 4.6 s, from 44,450 in 26.7 s, from 86,200 in 94 s. The chain from 1 on a circle of 1,000: 14 of 14 steps are exponents.
- **One after another**: the same rule with no circle and no cap: 9,941 after 22.5 s, 11,213 after 32 s, 19,937 after 170 s.
- **The perfect row in Python** ([row/row.py](writeup/code/mersenne/row/row.py) → [row/row.log](writeup/code/mersenne/row/row.log); proved): every prime in order from 2, trial factors 2kp + 1 first, then Lucas–Lehmer in gmpy2. In 17,244 s on one core it proved the first 28 exponents with no gap, 2 to 86,243: 8,384 primes, 3,770 of them by a Lucas–Lehmer test and the rest by a factor. The next one, 110,503, was out of reach of the 19,000 s time limit.
- **Only the known exponents**: Lucas–Lehmer on the 38 known exponents up to 6,972,593 and on nothing else: 31 of them in 2.6 minutes. One test on one core takes 13 s at 86,243 (Node, a full test) and, from the measured time per squaring, about 1.4 minutes at 216,091, 19 at 756,839, 82 at 1,398,269, 6 hours at 2,976,221 and 2 days at 6,972,593. This touches only exponents and proves each one, but the list is its input: it cannot find a new one.

## 6. The exponents as plain numbers (computed; A366029 proved)

The same question with the exponents as integers only: 2, 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, … as a sequence, without "prime" and without 2^p − 1. Two searches, each run identically on 250 to 500 random sequences that grow the same way (null A: points spread at random in log a; null B: random primes taken with the heuristic probability), with a family-wise correction and with every rule chosen on a₁ … a₂₅ and then counted on a₂₆ … a₅₂:

- **Step rules aₙ₊₁ = f(aₙ)** ([numbers/](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/), [report.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/report.txt)): 745,984 maps, compositions to depth 3 of 1,187 primitives (x + c, cx + d, ⌊x·r⌋, powers, 2^x ± c, bit and decimal operations, "the next y > x" in a residue class, palindromic, of a given binary weight or of the form 2^k ± c); the same grammar backwards; 16,884 two-term rules. The best, "(the second number of the form 2^k ± 1 above x) − 2", reproduces 7 of the 51 steps (2→3, 3→5, 5→7, 7→13, 19→31, 31→61, 107→127; all below 128); the null medians are 6 (p = 0.48 and 0.43). No rule chosen on the first 25 terms predicts any later term. Closed forms ([formulas.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/formulas.txt)): ⌊1.45717·1.50872ⁿ⌋ hits 8 terms (p = 0.31); a formula that reproduces all 52 needs at least the 804 bits of the list itself (a Mills-type constant restates the list).
- **Relations and patterns** ([relations/](writeup/code/mersenne/relations/), all 252 statistics in [combined.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/relations/combined.txt)): linear recurrences (Berlekamp–Massey over Q: the generic order ⌈m/2⌉ for every m from 8 to 52; integer recurrences with |c| ≤ 10 run for at most 4 terms), relations a_k = c₁aᵢ + c₂aⱼ + d with small coefficients (225 at d = 0, e.g. 19937 = 5·127 + 6·3217, against a null median of 274), the binary strings (Lempel–Ziv complexity, minimal automata, common substrings), the digits in bases 2 to 36, the growth (0.531 bits per term; the gaps behave like those of a Poisson process). The smallest raw p-value is 0.004 (base 23: two equal neighbouring digits in 12 of the 45 terms with at least two digits), 0.19 after the family-wise correction, and it fails out of sample.
- **The OEIS** ([oeis.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/oeis.txt)): the longest agreement is A366029, defined on the integer k alone: k > 1 with (k − 1)^(2^k − 2) ≡ 1 (mod (2^k − 1)·k²). It is 2, 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 19, 31, 61, 89, 107, 127, then 257 instead of 521.
  - It contains every Mersenne exponent (proved): if 2^p − 1 is prime, Fermat gives (p − 1)^(2^p − 2) ≡ 1 mod 2^p − 1; mod p², (p − 1)^N ≡ 1 − N·p for even N, which is 1 because p divides N = 2^p − 2; and gcd(2^p − 1, p²) = 1.
  - The same expansion shows that the congruence is two Fermat tests (proved): 2^k ≡ 2 (mod k) and (k − 1)^(2^k − 2) ≡ 1 (mod 2^k − 1). [a366029.py](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/a366029.py) → [a366029.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/a366029.txt) checks both forms against each other for every k ≤ 4,500: they agree, and the terms are the 20 exponents up to 4,423 and 257.
  - The extras (proved): when k − 1 = 2^m the base is a power of 2, and since 2 has order k modulo 2^k − 1, (2^m)^(2^k − 2) ≡ 1 holds whenever k divides 2^k − 2. So every k = 2^m + 1 with 2^k ≡ 2 (mod k) is a term: all Fermat numbers 2^(2^n) + 1, prime or not (257, 65537, 4294967297, …), of which 3, 5 and 17 happen to be exponents.
  - So the one integer rule that lands on every exponent is a probable-prime test of 2^k − 1 under another name: k squarings of a k-bit number, the cost of Lucas–Lehmer.
  - On the page it is transformation 143 (next k > p in A366029, up to 150,000), with the source mode "every number on the circle": the chain from 1 runs 2, 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 19, 31, 61, 89, 107, 127, 257, 521, 607, … (14 of its first 15 steps land on exponents; the miss is 127 → 257). The page decides each k exactly: 2^k ≡ 2 (mod k) first; then the congruence modulo a small factor of 2^k − 1 (for composite k, modulo 2^d − 1 with d the smallest factor of k), which refutes most k; k = 2^m + 1 by the proof above; the rest by the FFT of section 5 with an exact digit multiplication by k − 1, and every term found again in BigInt. Checked in Node on the page's own functions: equal to the definition for every k from 2 to 2,000, no base-2 pseudoprime among 25 (341 … 11305) passes, the FFT path equals exact BigInt on 18 values of k from 4,201 to 11,939, and the chain from 1 to 12,000 is the 23 exponents and 257; 65537 is a term, 65539 is not.

In the integer view as well, nothing beyond the growth law: whatever lands on every exponent tests 2^k − 1.

## 7. Outside the known formulas: rounds 3 to 5 (computed; the null is heuristic)

Ideas from anywhere, judged by one fixed referee. Background: all 4,882,467 primes below B = 83,880,077 with the heuristic rate w(p) = e^γ·log₂(a·p)/p (a = 6 for p ≡ 1, a = 2 for p ≡ 3 mod 4). Every feature chooses its class and threshold on the exponents below 10⁵ (discovery), is ranked on [10⁵, 10⁷) (validation, 10 exponents, 12.9 expected), and the whole pipeline runs again on hundreds of synthetic label sets drawn from the null, which sets a family-wise bar. The range [10⁷, B) (13 exponents, 5.86 expected) was kept as a final exam: the top five features of rounds 3b and 3c were frozen in a pre-registration file (with its sha256) before that range was opened. Each round has an independent recheck script. Code and outputs in [rounds/](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/).

| round | what | ideas | best | family-wise | final exam |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3a [r3_early](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_early/) | an early sign inside the Lucas–Lehmer and PRP runs (criterion written first: [CRITERIA.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_early/CRITERIA.txt)) | 84 features × 8 depths, survivors 100 … 20,000 | χ² of residues at k/4: all 6 test exponents kept, 25.8% of the rest rejected (p 0.033) | 0.37 | fresh range 12,000 … 20,000: chance |
| 3b [r3_arith](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_arith/) | exotic arithmetic of k: bases 3–64, the neighbours' factorisations, orders and power residues, x² + ny², class numbers, elliptic curves, dynamics | 519 | CF of √k: 5/2.13 (p 0.065) | 0.89 | best 4/0.83 (k ≡ 1 mod 7), p 0.097 given the range's 13 |
| 3c [r3_cross](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_cross/) | outside number theory: zeta zeros at k, 2k ± 1 and 2^k; frac(kα) for 31 constants; cellular automata; Collatz; spirals; prime-gap context | 320 | frac(k/2π) ≤ 0.103: 4/1.34 (p 0.047) | 0.75 | nothing (best p 0.26 given the 13) |
| 4a [r4_lines](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r4_lines/) | lines on the square (columns for widths 2 … 10,000; any slope for 2 … 3,000) and on the Ulam spiral | — | w = 1099 columns; w = 12 lines (20 of 51 exponents ≡ 5 mod 12) | 0.79, 0.45, 0.28 | holdout: nothing replicates (0.19 after 4 tests) |
| 4b [r4_index](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r4_index/) | the positions as numbers: π(a_n), its gaps, the index among p ≡ 1 / 3 (mod 4), among trial-factoring survivors, within the bit length | 9 sequences, 270 statistics | b_k = 3b_(k−2) − b_(k−1) ± 2 in the mod-4 interleave (p 0.060) | 0.84 | no later term predicted |
| 5 [r5_transfer](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r5_transfer/) | more data: (bⁿ ∓ 1)/(b ∓ 1) for 79 bases b ≤ 50, every odd prime n up to 10,000 bits: 401 PRP exponents (OEIS agrees for all 79 families); the six near-misses of round 3 frozen and tested in 39 discovery and 40 holdout families | 6 + 1,131 | k ≡ 1 mod 7: rate 0.99 (90% interval 0.76–1.27) | none passes both halves | — |
| 6a [r6_learn](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r6_learn/) | a learned combination: 1,119 features of n and b (residues, digits, characters, the near-misses) in L2 and L1 logistic models and boosted trees, with the sieve-conditioned null as offset, cross-validated by leaving whole families out; then refitted on all 79 families and applied to the Mersenne exponents | 1,119 × 24 models | best −3.3 bits out of family (every model loses bits) | 0.85 | Mersenne: mean percentile and gain at chance (family-wise 0.87, 0.94); the 52nd at percentile 30–48 |
| 6b [r6_forecast](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r6_forecast/) | the exponents as points on the number line: the heuristic as a forecast of the next one, scored on all 50 verified steps | — | 46 of 50 steps inside their 90% interval (45.75 expected); largest surprise 127 → 521 (u = 0.986) | the largest of 50 steps is that large with probability 0.52 | — |

- The most persistent near-miss, k ≡ 1 (mod 7) (13 of 51 against 7.4 expected; 4 of the 13 in the final range against 0.8), shows no effect at all in the 79 other families, where the holdout alone could detect a rate of 1.4 to 1.6. The 52nd exponent, 136,279,841 (beyond the verified bound, so used by no round), is ≡ 5 (mod 7).
- Inside a Lucas–Lehmer run nothing is recognisable before s_(k−3) ≡ ±2^((k+1)/2) (proved; checked on every exponent from 3 to 19,937). For a prime 2^k − 1, s_i is the trace of an element of order 2^(k−i); only orders up to 8 have closed-form traces, and going back one step costs a square root mod 2^k − 1, itself about k squarings. The Jacobi symbols (s_i ± 2 | 2^k − 1) are the same for prime and composite 2^k − 1 (from i = 1 on; checked on 1,184 checkpoints).
- The final range [10⁷, B) holds 13 exponents against 5.86 expected (round 1: p 0.015 for that range alone, 0.12 after its 8 ranges), so the fair final-exam test is conditional on the 13; both rounds 3b and 3c report it that way.
- The forecast (round 6b; heuristic): with w as above, the expected number of exponents between x and y is e^γ·[log₂(y/x) + log₂√12 · ln(ln y / ln x)] (the integral over the primes, within 1% of the exact prime sums from 10³ on; checked up to 10⁹). From no. 51, 82,589,933, the median forecast for the next exponent was 106.3 million (50%: 91.7–136.9 million); no. 52, 136,279,841, came at u = 0.75. GIMPS has tested every exponent below 142,869,329 at least once (milestones page, 15 November 2026), so no. 53 is forecast at a median of 184.0 million (50%: 158.7–237.1 million; 90%: 145.6–427.2 million). The 50 PIT values are uniform (KS p 0.36; recomputed independently: 46 of 50 inside the 90% interval, the step sizes sum to 50.56 against 50). prime2.html shows these forecasts.
- Power: with 10 validation exponents only strong effects show (a class holding nearly all exponents and at most a fifth of the null weight); round 5's 401 exponents raise the power to effects of about 1.5 times the rate.

## 8. Patterns in the pictures (computed; Bateman–Horn and the Hardy–Littlewood constants are conjectures, Dirichlet's theorem is proved)

prime2.html draws 1 … 500,000 in 43 layouts. Every layout was photographed (whole and zoomed four times, the exponent links off, the brightest line drawn by the page's own line test) and its exact dots read from the page ([patterns/extract.js](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/extract.js), [shots2.js](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/shots2.js)); the report with all screenshots is the page prime2-patterns.html beside prime2.html, built by [gen_report.py](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/gen_report.py) from [texts.json](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/texts.json).

- Grid layouts (23; [lines.py](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/lines.py), [lines.log](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/lines.log), [lines_out/](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/lines_out/)): every lattice line in 24 directions cut into stretches where the numbers follow one polynomial; 1.6 million stretches of 30 or more numbers. The prime count of every stretch is predicted by Bateman–Horn, C(f)·Σ 1/ln f(t) with C(f) from the densities of the small primes dividing f: in every layout a random 150 stretches differ from the prediction by 0.0 ± 0.5–0.7 standard deviations, and at most 1 in 150 by more than 3. The brightest lines are Euler's m² + m + 41 (spirals, shells, Klauber, between squares: 394 primes against 393 predicted), 2s² − 199 (tree, Floyd, diagonals, zigzag, diamond: no prime up to 31 divides it, constant 7.32 above Euler's 6.65), 3s² + 3s + 23 (hexagonal spiral) and Dirichlet's columns (rows: constant 708/φ(708) = 3.05; bands of 512: 510/φ(510) = 3.98); the dark lines are polynomials that factor or that a small prime always divides (constant 0). The six space-filling and bit-order layouts have no polynomial stretch of 30 numbers at all.
- Round layouts (12; [round/](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/round/)): the arms are remainder classes (polar: 20 of 44 and 280 of 710 arms hold primes; wheel: 232 spokes; sunflower: classes mod Fibonacci numbers), with arm strengths |μ(q)|/φ(q) as measured to about 0.01; Sacks' streaks are quadratics, 15,388 primes on 242 curves against 15,431 predicted.
- Walks and rows (7; [walks/](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/walks/)): the Mertens walk stays far tighter than a random walk and is reproduced by 150 zeta zeros (correlation 0.99); the Liouville walk touches 0 only at 2, 4, 6, 10, 16, 26, 40, 96, 586 and stays below up to 10⁶ (rechecked); the prime race mod 4 is below zero only at 26,861 and 26,862 up to 500,000 (rechecked); the turtle closes 255 rectangles (five primes with gaps a, b, a, b; recounted) against 362 ± 21 for shuffled gaps, because patterns impossible mod 3 or 5 never occur and the allowed ones are about three times as common (the Hardy–Littlewood constants); the row layouts match Legendre's sieve counts exactly.
- Cosine rows with period 12 ([patterns/cos12/analyse.py](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/analyse.py) → [analyse.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/analyse.txt)): every prime from 5 sits up (p ≡ ±1 mod 12, 3 a square mod p) or down (p ≡ ±5). The gap to the next prime decides the lane exactly (gaps ≡ 2, 10, 12 mod 12 keep it, 4, 6, 8 switch it), so consecutive primes switch lanes 54.6% of the time up to 500,000 (53.7% up to 10⁷), and repeat their remainder mod 12 only 57% as often as independence gives (66% up to 10⁷; Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan, 2016). Twin pairs always share a lane, up and down equally often (2,318 and 2,246). The down lane leads slightly (Chebyshev's bias; the up lane first leads at 61,463). The exponents lean down: 16 of the 49 from 5 to 82,589,933 are up, about 23 expected (P = 0.023 one-sided; both halves lean the same way, and no. 52 is down), but this is one split among the many residue groupings tested since round 1 (all classes mod 12 together: 0.095), so it is not significant; future exponents are a clean test (about half up if the heuristic holds).
- The cosine rows zoomed out ([patterns/cos12/bigdata.py](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/bigdata.py) → [bigdata.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/bigdata.txt), [race.svg](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/race.svg), [rails.svg](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/rails.svg); pictures by [bigpic2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/bigpic2.py); every number rechecked by [bigcheck.py](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/bigcheck.py) → [bigcheck.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/cos12/bigcheck.txt), 27 checks): at under one pixel per number the picture is an even speckle; with a row width that is a multiple of 12 (240, numbers to 100,000) the waves line up into stripes: 64 of the 240 columns hold primes, each one lane only, the colour pattern repeating every 60 columns, the stripes equally full (137 to 164 primes, mean 149.8). Twin pairs run on 24 double tracks (12 per lane); all 1,222 from (11, 13) to 100,000 are on them. Width 277 (≡ 1 mod 12) tilts the stripes into diagonals, width 100 (≡ 4) changes each column's lane in a cycle of three rows. To 10⁷ the down lane leads, 332,352 primes against 332,225, by ½ Li(√x) on average: every prime square p² (p ≥ 5) is ≡ 1 (mod 12), and the primes in class 1 fall short by about half their number (Chebyshev's bias; Rubinstein–Sarnak 1994). Measured, the lead averaged over ln x from 10³ to 10⁷ is 0.986 times ½ Li(√x) (1.26 times √x / ln x); the up lane is ahead in 102 stretches (first at 61,463, longest 1,172,807 to 1,179,403, last ending at 2,233,787), after 1,476 of the 664,577 primes. Of the 50 exponents from 5 to 136,279,841, 16 are up.
- The exponents: no layout puts them on a line, arm, spoke or ring beyond random sets of the same growth (the 23 grid layouts p ≥ 0.07, family-wise far above). The lowest values have plain causes: binary blocks (p 0.066) put 3, 7, 31, 127 on one row because they are Mersenne numbers 2^b − 1 themselves; the polar layout's angle cluster of the 31 exponents below 500,000 (p 0.003 there, 0.12 over the 12 round layouts) is round 3's near-miss "cos k large", rejected by round 5 (rate 0.99 in 79 families), and the 21 exponents above 500,000 show none of it (Rayleigh 0.18 against 4.65).

## 9. Sixteen new strategies (computed; the theorems cited are proved, Bateman–Horn, Hardy–Littlewood and Artin's conjecture are conjectures, the Chebyshev-bias sizes follow Rubinstein and Sarnak, who assume GRH and LI)

prime2.html gained 16 layouts built on number theory (70 in all). Each note computes its numbers live for the chosen parameter and prints the exceptions to its own rule ([strategies/page/](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/page/): [ls.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/page/ls.js), [lsnotes.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/page/lsnotes.js), inserted by [patchS.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/page/patchS.py); every layout run in headless Chrome by [test.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/page/test.js), 0 script errors; the page's own code swept over its parameters by [sweep.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/page/sweep.js) → [sweep.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/page/sweep.txt)). Every number below was recomputed independently in Python ([strategies/check/](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/), and the reports [forms/RESULTS.md](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/forms/RESULTS.md), [sieve/RESULTS.md](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/sieve/RESULTS.md), [race/RESULTS.md](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/race/RESULTS.md), each with a second script per result). The report page prime2-patterns.html has a card per layout with screenshots.

- **Lucas–Lehmer residues** (odd primes up to 3,000): residue 0 exactly at the 16 exponents 3 to 2,281, the other 413 normalised residues uniform (Kolmogorov–Smirnov 0.033, 5% critical value 0.067); the signs of s_(p−3) = ±2^((p+1)/2), + + − + − − + + − − + − − − + −, agree with OEIS A123271 ([check/llres.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/llres.py) → [llres.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/llres.txt)).
- **Sums a² + P·b²** (P = 1 to 100): the share of primes is Chebotarev's 1/(2h(−4P)) (h agrees with PARI for all 100); remainders mod 4P decide exactly for the 36 convenient numbers below 100 (Euler, A000926); Gauss's rules for P = 27 and 64 and the cubic rules for 14, 11 and 23 hold for every prime up to 500,000. Measured here: the share is below 1/(2h) for all 100 values of P, up to 500,000 and up to 10⁷, by about ½(h + a − 1)·Li(√N)/Li(N) (a = forms equal to their own inverse): the prime squares p² = p² + P·0² all fall in the principal class, so the primes there fall short, as in Chebyshev's race (the Chebotarev form of Chebyshev's bias, studied by Ng in 2000; the numbers are ours). Up to 10⁷ the mean shortfall over the 100 values is 0.00214 against 0.00212 predicted, correlation over P 0.76 ([check/qf_bias.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/qf_bias.py) → [qf_bias.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/qf_bias.txt)).
- **a² + ab + b²**: exactly 3 and the 20,733 primes ≡ 1 (mod 3) up to 500,000.
- **Mersenne factor sieve** (row k: the smallest factor 2kp + 1 of 2^p − 1): survivors 34,372, 31,733, 29,401 and 27,308 of 41,538 primes for K = 16, 64, 256, 1,000; 0 exceptions to the mod-4 law (no k ≡ 2 mod 4; k ≡ 1 needs p ≡ 3, k ≡ 3 needs p ≡ 1) among 17,199 factors; row 1 is exactly Euler's rule (2,189 primes); each candidate q = 2kp + 1 ≡ ±1 (mod 8) divides with chance about 1/k (17,318 factors expected, 17,199 found).
- **Order of 2 mod q** (the sieve from the factor's side): 2,178 of the 41,537 odd primes up to 500,000 have a prime order of 2, i.e. divide a Mersenne number with a prime exponent; their indices (q − 1)/ord are ≡ 0, 2 or 6 (mod 8), never odd or ≡ 4, as the mod-4 law requires ([check/ord2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/ord2.py)).
- **Fermat quotients**: the only Wieferich primes up to 500,000 are 1,093 and 3,511 (none other below 6.7·10¹⁵, Dorais and Klyve 2011), with orders of 2 equal to 364 and 1,755, not prime; since p² | 2^r − 1 (r prime) forces p to be Wieferich with order r, no Mersenne number with a prime exponent has a square factor p² with p below 6.7·10¹⁵. The other quotients are uniform (Kolmogorov–Smirnov 0.0026, 5% critical value 0.0067) ([check/wief_cunn.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/wief_cunn.py) → [wief_cunn.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/wief_cunn.txt)).
- **Cunningham chains** p → 2p + 1: 4,324 Sophie Germain primes up to 500,000 against 4,367 by Hardy–Littlewood; chains of six start at 89, 63,419, 127,139 and 405,269. Euler's rule keeps every exponent ≡ 3 (mod 4) above 3 out of the Sophie Germain rows (a theorem; 0 exceptions); the exponents in them are 2, 3, 5, 89, 9,689 and 21,701, and 89 starts the first chain of six.
- **Gap towers**: champion 6 (7,474), 4,565 twin pairs, record 114 after 492,113. In windows of length ln n (10⁵ to 5·10⁵) the prime count is not yet Poisson(1) as Gallagher's theorem gives in the limit (29.8% empty windows, not 36.8%); the Hardy–Littlewood pattern constants predict the histogram (9,474 / 14,172 / 6,878 / 1,249 / 74 / 1 against 9,495 / 14,180 / 6,835 / 1,258 / 78 / 2).
- **Polygon spirals** (P sides): corner j of ring m is P·m(m − 1)/2 + j·m + 1; the brightest rays are often the centred polygonal numbers, 3m² + 3m + 1 (P = 6, the cuban primes, 3.3 times the naive count) and 5m² + 5m + 1 (P = 10, 4.4 times). Over the 7,864 lines of P = 3 to 40 where Bateman–Horn applies, 191,999 primes above 1,000 against 192,781 predicted (0.996), with a spread of 0.6 standard deviations instead of 1 (the small primes hit a line in an exact rhythm); the brightest line is 19m² + 19m − 1 (P = 38): 90 primes among 161 numbers; the line one step before each corner is dark (it factors).
- **Digit sums** (base B): rows whose sum shares a factor with B − 1 hold only the prime factors of B − 1 (casting out nines): base 10, 16 of 49 rows.
- **Race of squares mod P** (a walk: |R|·π_non-squares − |N|·π_squares): the prime squares all land on squares, so the walk rises like ½·|N|·Li(√n). Up to 10⁷ the walk averages 0.970 times that over the moduli 3 to 40 (0.82 to 1.12). How one-sided a race is depends on the number of real characters mod P against the noise from the zeros of their L-functions (rank correlation 0.87), not on P dividing 24 alone: P = 4 goes negative, 16 moduli that do not divide 24 never do up to 10⁷, and P = 19 and 38 are below zero after a third of the primes because L(s, χ₋₁₉) has a zero at height 1.5161 (rechecked with mpmath).
- **Goldbach's comet**: no exception up to 500,000; the fewest ways above 1,000 is 16, at 1,112; multiples of 3 have 1.999 times the ways and multiples of 5 1.333, Hardy and Littlewood's factors 2 and 4/3 ([check/gold_dick.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/gold_dick.py), by direct counting, not the page's FFT).
- **Largest prime factor**: the share with no prime factor above √n is 26.8% up to 10⁶ and 27.2% at 10⁷ (Dickman's ρ(2) = 30.7%); above the cube root 4.7% at 500,000 and 4.4% at 10⁷ (ρ(3) = 4.9%): slow and not monotone.
- **Least primitive root**: 2 for 15,528 of the 41,537 odd primes (37.38%; Artin's constant 37.40%), 3 for 22.66%, the largest 69 (p = 110,881); squares never, 2 never for p ≡ ±1 (mod 8).
- **π(n) − li(n)**: below zero from 3 to 10⁷; averaged over ln n it is 0.999 (to 500,000) and 0.997 (to 10⁷) times Riemann's R(n) − li(n), against 1.22 and 1.18 for −½li(√n) alone ([check/pili.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/pili.py)).
- **Totient**: φ(n)/n depends only on the prime divisors, so the numbers fall on horizontal lines.
- **The exponents**: nothing beyond chance in any of the other 15. Least primitive root 2 for 15 of the 51 from 3 to 136,279,841, where their residues mod 8 give 15.7 (p = 0.80); the gaps after them add up to 36.4 in units of ln p against 32.7 ± 3.8; six of the 30 odd ones up to 500,000 have a prime order of 2 (3, 7, 31, 127, 89, 23,209) against 3.9 for weighted random sets (p = 0.16, [check/ord2_exps.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/ord2_exps.py)); on the polygon spirals the cuban ray holds 7, 19, 61 and 127 (2.6 expected), and the most striking of 817 corner rays, 19m² + 15m + 1 (P = 38), holds 107, 19,937 and 44,497 where 0.17 are expected: p = 0.018 among those rays, but untestable, since the heuristic puts only 0.002 more exponents on it up to 10⁹ ([check/poly_exps.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/poly_exps.py), [poly38_oos.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/check/poly38_oos.py)). With 70 layouts and the earlier rounds, one ray at that level is expected.

## 10. Theories of our own (each derived first and tested after; nothing fitted; the label of each says what is proved)

Eleven statements we derived ourselves, each with a prediction fixed before the data were counted. Five rest on the sieve (the densities of the small primes, Hardy–Littlewood style); six do not (prime squares, group orders, zeros). Code and outputs in [theories/](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/) ([run.sh](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/run.sh) runs them all; the primes to 10⁹ come from [sieve1e9.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/sieve1e9.py), whose twin count equals the known 3,424,506 less the 4 pairs below 24). An independent recheck with its own scripts ([theories/check/](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/check/), [RESULTS.md](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/check/RESULTS.md)) agrees on every number it was given. A literature search ([theories/LIT.md](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/LIT.md)) says what is known; "not found" there means not found, not new.

Without the sieve:

- **9. Chebyshev's bias in Goldbach's comet** (the identity is proved, the rest heuristic; literature: averages of Goldbach counts in progressions with terms from L-function zeros, Rüppel 2012, Bhowmik–Halupczok–Matsumoto–Suzuki 2019, but no prime-square term found). Every prime square s² (s ≥ 5) is ≡ 1 (mod 12). Count that class with half a point added at each prime square (the count with no bias for single primes, as in section 8); then exactly Δ(n) = Δ_Π(n) − S(n), where Δ compares the ordered representations n = p + q through class 1 with those avoiding it (n ≡ 0: (1, 11) against (5, 7); n ≡ 2: (1, 1) against (7, 7); n ≡ 6: (1, 5) against (7, 11)) and S(n) = #{primes s ≥ 5: n − s² prime}. The theory: Δ_Π has no bias, so the strands of the comet that use class 1 lie S(n) lower. Up to 4·10⁶ ([gold.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/gold.py) → [gold.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/gold.txt), FFT checked by direct counts), over the four octaves from 2.5·10⁵: mean Δ −43.3, −43.0 and −42.8 against −S = −40.0, −39.8 and −39.9, so the squares carry about 93% of the bias; the rest swings with the scale (for n ≡ 0: +9, −20, +8, −11; the three classes move together, the race of class 1 itself), and n ≡ 10 ((5, 5) against (11, 11), no class 1) averages +0.2. Within an octave Δ_Π/G and S/G are uncorrelated (|r| ≤ 0.08). Again mod 8, where every odd square is ≡ 1 (s ≥ 3; [gold8.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/gold8.py) → [gold8.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/gold8.txt), to 2·10⁶): mean Δ −23.2, −23.1, −23.0, −21.3 for n ≡ 0, 2, 4, 6 against −S = −21.1, −21.0, −21.0, −21.1. The rest is the prime race (9b, heuristic, no parameter): to first order Δ(n) ≈ w·Σ ±(π_a(n) + π_b(n)) over the pair types (a, b), with π_a(n) the primes up to n in class a; the prime squares are the part of that race known exactly (−½ per square in class 1, i.e. −Q(n)/2, Q(n) = #{prime s ≥ 5: s² ≤ n}), and in Goldbach they give exactly −S(n), so w = S(n)/Q(n) and Δ(n) ≈ −S(n)·D(n)/(Q(n)/2), D(n) the lead of the other classes over class 1 among the primes up to n. In 40 bins of log n from 2.5·10⁵ to 4·10⁶ ([goldrace.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldrace.py) → [goldrace12.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldrace12.txt), [goldrace8.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldrace8.txt)) the binned mean Δ and the prediction correlate 0.993, 0.993, 0.989, 0.990 for n ≡ 0, 2, 6, 10 (mod 12) and 0.986 to 0.989 for the four classes mod 8; the mean error falls from 10–19 (squares alone) to 2–4 (mod 12) and from 7–12 to 1.6–2.3 (mod 8), against a sampling error of a bin of 1 to 1.5. The control n ≡ 10 (mod 12), with no squares, is predicted as well (its w taken from n − 2). So the comet's class strands are the prime race of the same moduli, scaled by the squares. And the race comes from the zeros (9c, heuristic, assumes GRH): in characters the four races are 2Σχ₁₂(p), Σ(χ₋₄ + χ₁₂)(p), 2Σχ₋₄(p) and Σ(χ₋₄ − χ₁₂)(p), and the explicit formula gives Σ_(p≤n) χ(p) ≈ −Σ_ρ Li(n^ρ) − ½·(prime squares), so for n ≡ 0 the strands are Δ(n) ≈ −S(n)·(1 + 2Z₁₂(n)/Q(n)) with Z₁₂(n) = Σ_ρ Li(n^ρ) over the zeros of L(s, χ₁₂) (similarly for the other classes). With the 289 zeros of L(s, χ₋₄) and 359 of L(s, χ₁₂) below height 400 (PARI's lfunzeros, [zeros_m4.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/zeros_m4.txt), [zeros_12.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/zeros_12.txt)) and no prime count in the race, the binned correlation is 0.993, 0.993, 0.990, 0.990 and the mean error 4.3, 2.8, 2.5, 2.3 ([goldzeros.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldzeros.py) → [goldzeros.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldzeros.txt)): the strands of Goldbach's comet follow the zeros of two L-functions. On a range 250 times longer ([goldbig.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldbig.py): each bin's sum of Δ is an exact count of class pairs (p, q) with p + q in the bin, by binary search in the sorted primes, so no comet is built; it reproduces the FFT run to 4·10⁶, [goldbig_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldbig_check.txt)), 60 bins of log n from 10⁶ to 10⁹ ([goldbig_1e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldbig_1e9.txt)): correlation with the race 0.997, 0.997, 0.997, 0.995 for n ≡ 0, 2, 6, 10; with the zeros alone 0.997, 0.998, 0.997, 0.995; with the squares alone 0.80 to 0.85 (0.37 to 0.51 inside a single decade); mean error 10.7, 7.8, 7.5, 4.9 against 70.9, 51.6, 48.2, 35.1 for the squares alone, while the binned gap swings between about −15 and −750. Averaged over the range the squares carry the whole bias (mean Δ −183, −178, −173 against −S = −177). The measured swings are slightly larger than predicted: the slope of measured on predicted is 1.033, 1.020, 1.017, and 1.111 for the square-free class n ≡ 10, whose scale is borrowed from n − 2. For single n the gap is noisy: at n = 100,000,008 there are 437,634 ways through class 1 and 437,800 through (5, 7) and (7, 5), gap −166 against −216 predicted, and its neighbours n ≡ 0 swing from −1,378 to +630; averaged over 1,000 neighbours the gap is −166.9 ± 28.7 against −176.2 predicted and −207.2 for the squares alone ([goldex.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldex.py) → [goldex.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/goldex.txt)). The report page prime2-patterns.html shows this and the other theories with charts.
- **6. A Chebyshev bias for twin primes** (heuristic; not decidable by counting; literature: the race is tabulated to 10¹⁰ with lead changes and no explanation, Sahoo, arXiv:2111.09053). If Σ Λ(n)Λ(n + 2) has no bias between the lanes, as ψ has for single primes, the up twins (12i − 1, 12i + 1) lag the down twins (12i + 5, 12i + 7) by half the number of primes p with p² − 2 prime ((p² − 2, p²) is an up pair of prime powers; p² + 2 is a multiple of 3). To 10⁹ ([ord2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/ord2.py) → [ord2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/ord2.txt)): 651 such p, so +326 predicted; the down twins lead by 956 (1,712,729 against 1,711,773), but the race swings by about √(count) ≈ 1,850, and the down twins are ahead only 48% of the time on a log scale. If twin races swing like random walks, the bias shrinks against the swings like 1/ln x, so no count will decide it.
- **7. How many primes have a prime order of 2** (heuristic; literature: the sequence is OEIS A122094, bounds of order x/(log x)² by Pomerance, no formula found). These are the prime factors of Mersenne numbers with a prime exponent: q = 2kl + 1 with l prime. For fixed k, Hardy–Littlewood counts the primes l with 2kl + 1 prime (2C₂ times the product of (r − 1)/(r − 2) over odd r | k); q ≡ ±1 (mod 8) holds for every l when 4 | k, for half of them when k is odd, never when k ≡ 2 (mod 4); then the order is l with chance 1/k. Summed: N(x) ≈ Σ_k 2C₂·m(k)g(k)/k·∫₃^(x/2k) dt/(ln t·ln 2kt), asymptotically A·x/ln²x with A = C₂·Σ m(k)g(k)/k² = 0.5382 ([t7form.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/t7form.py) → [t7form.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/t7form.txt)). No prime enters the formula; it gives 3,841.6 up to 10⁶ and 26,690 up to 10⁷, and the count is 3,845 and 26,560 (ratio 1.001 and 0.995; 0.93 to 0.97 from 10³ to 10⁵).
- **10. One Mersenne factor in five is ≡ 1 (mod 3)** (the rule is proved, the limit heuristic; literature: not found, implicit in Shanks and Kravitz 1967). For a factor q = 2kl + 1 of 2^l − 1 with l > 3 prime: if 3 ∤ k then 2kl + 1 ≡ 0 or 2 (mod 3), so a prime q is ≡ 1 (mod 3) exactly when 3 | k (0 exceptions among 8,861 factors). With the weights of theory 7, writing k = 3^a·k′ (m and g do not change, except the factor 2 for 3 | k) the sum over a gives 1 + 2(1/9 + 1/81 + …) = 1 + 1/4, of which 1/4 has a ≥ 1, so the share tends to exactly 1/5, slowly: the formula gives 0.2202 at 10⁶ and 0.2162 at 10⁷, measured 0.2216 and 0.2156 (all primes: 1/2).
- **8. Euler's rule one row down** (proved; known: O. Latinne's conjecture, proved by Z.-W. Sun in 2008, in probabilistic form already in Shanks and Kravitz 1967; OEIS A188130). For p ≡ 1 (mod 4) and q = 6p + 1 prime: q | 2^p − 1 exactly when q = x² + 27y² (q ≡ 7 mod 8 makes 2 a square; Gauss: 2 is a cube mod q iff q = x² + 27y²; so 2 is a sixth power and 2^p = 2^((q−1)/6) ≡ 1). Row 3 of the factor sieve is the P = 27 line of the a² + P·b² layout. Checked with x and y found directly for all 11,198 such p up to 1,666,666: 3,706 divide (0.331; Chebotarev 1/3), exactly those of the form.
- **11. The race of the zeros** (heuristic, assumes GRH and LI; literature: the substance is Bays, Ford, Hudson and Rubinstein 2001, who give the mod-19 bias 0.8042). For a prime P the race of non-residue against residue primes is, by the explicit formula, E(x) = (ln x/√x)(π_N(x) − π_R(x)) ≈ b + Σ_ρ x^(iγ)/ρ over the zeros of L(s, χ_P), where b = 1 comes from the prime squares (all residues). The total variance of the zero terms needs no zero at all: V = Σ 1/|ρ|² = 2[Re L′/L(1, χ) + ½ ln(P/π) + ½ψ((1 + a)/2)] (Hadamard product; a = 0 or 1 for even or odd χ). Our one-zero law: the lowest zero γ₁ is a wave of amplitude 2/|ρ₁| and the other zeros are noise of variance V − 2/|ρ₁|², so the share of log-time with E < 0 is P(b + A cos θ + G < 0). From L-values only (Hurwitz zeta, [zrace.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/zrace.py) → [zrace.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/zrace.txt)): the lowest zeros for P = 3 … 47 run from 8.04 (P = 3) down to 1.5161 (P = 19), 1.79 (29) and 0.836 (43, like 19 a class number 1); for P = 19 the law gives 0.195, the full computation of Bays et al. over 3,184 zeros 0.1958. Measured on the odd primes up to 10⁸ (log-time from 10³; counting 2 as well moves the walk one step, and the shares by up to 0.02): the predicted shares follow the measured ones across the 14 moduli (correlation 0.97 to 0.98; the largest are 43, 19 and 29, measured 0.204, 0.137 and 0.120), but our first version overestimated them by 0.036 to 0.046 on average. At finite x the squares give b(x) = (ln x/√x)·½Li(√x) = 1 + 2/ln x + …, which is 1.47 at 10³ and 1.15 at 10⁸, not 1; with b(x) (no parameter, but added after that first comparison) the mean error is 0.011 (Gauss over all zeros) and 0.012 (one zero with its actual phase), e.g. P = 43: 0.211 and 0.178 against 0.204; P = 17 stays the worst (0.021 against 0.004).

With the sieve:

- **1. Lanes for Mersenne exponents** (heuristic; literature: Wagstaff 1983 has a = 2/6 by p mod 4 and replaces the k-dependent factor by 1; no refinement by p mod 3 or counts mod 12 found). Which small k can give a factor 2kp + 1 depends on p mod 12: the mod-8 law fixes k mod 4 by p mod 4, and 3 divides 2kp + 1 for one class of k mod 3 set by p mod 3. Euler's k = 1 is possible only for p ≡ 11 (mod 12) (proved). Multiplying 1 − P(2kp + 1 prime)/k over k ≤ 10⁴ (the densities of 3 to 13 averaged over p mod 5 … 13) gives the lanes p ≡ 1, 5, 7, 11 the weights 1.049, 1.007, 1.065, 0.879 at p = 10⁶ (relative to their mean; at 10⁸: 1.036, 1.005, 1.048, 0.911); the ratio of p ≡ 1 to p ≡ 3 (mod 4) comes out 1.058 against Wagstaff's log 6p / log 2p = 1.076. The mechanism, tested on all 54,359 primes in [10⁵, 8·10⁵): predicted share with no factor for k ≤ 100 0.795, 0.761, 0.809, 0.657, measured 0.799, 0.766, 0.812, 0.656 ([mlane.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/mlane.py) → [mlane.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/mlane.txt)). The exponents from 5 to B: 11, 20, 13, 5 in the four lanes, predicted 11.9, 13.2, 14.0, 9.2 (the mod-4 null 11.8, 13.4, 11.9, 11.3): 1.4 bits better than the null (P = 0.06 under the null), mostly from the Euler lane 11; the 20 in lane 5 (round 4a) stay unexplained. It accounts for part of the down-lean of section 8 (16 up against 21.1 predicted, 23.1 under the null).
- **2. A local factor for every exponent** (heuristic; literature: the per-k densities are Shanks and Kravitz 1967, no per-exponent version found). The same product with p's own residues mod 3, 5, 7, 11 and 13 instead of the lane average: a prior for each prime from its residues alone. In ten deciles of the prediction the trial-factoring survival of the 54,359 primes matches within 0.015 (0.506 against 0.506 in the lowest, 0.866 against 0.869 in the highest), 2,186 bits better than a constant (lanes: 775). On the 51 exponents, normalised inside each class mod 4: 2.5 bits better than the mod-4 null; the summed log₂ weight at the exponents is +1.20, against −3.97 ± 2.69 for sets drawn from the null (P = 0.025) and +0.98 ± 2.05 if the theory holds. Out of sample, no. 52 (136,279,841) has the low factor 0.932 (5.5th percentile of its neighbours, [t2oos.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/t2oos.py) → [t2oos.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/t2oos.txt)). A Bayes factor of about 6 for the local factor: small, and not a rule that finds exponents.
- **3. The bytes of the wheel 24** (heuristic: Hardy–Littlewood; literature: the inclusion–exclusion over the constants is Brent 1974, no such byte table found). Byte j is the primality of 24j − 1, 24j + 1, …, 24j + 19 (four lane words of section 8). The k-tuple constants with inclusion–exclusion give the expected count of each of the 256 values with nothing fitted; the 27 values that would need a multiple of 5 or 7 to be prime get 0 and never occur. Up to 10⁹ (41,666,666 bytes): χ² = 174 on 228 degrees of freedom, the largest deviation 2.6 standard deviations (to 10⁷: 196 on 223). The counts scatter less than independent draws (z = −2.5): the sieve makes prime patterns more regular than chance ([hlbytes.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/hlbytes.py) → [hlbytes.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/hlbytes.txt)).
- **4. The two rarest bytes** (heuristic). 223 and 251 are the two prime septuplet patterns (p, p + 2, p + 8, p + 12, p + 14, p + 18, p + 20 and its mirror) with p = 24j − 1; mirror images share one constant (C₇ = 323.83 as 7 forms in j): 1.08 each expected to 10⁷ and 13.22 to 10⁹. Found 5 and 0 to 10⁷ (together 5 against 2.2), 14 and 14 to 10⁹. Byte 251 first occurs at j = 1,875,108: the septuplet 45,002,591 … 45,002,611.
- **5. What can be known about the lane words** (heuristic; literature: entropies of prime sequences studied, Lacasa et al. 2018, none from Hardy–Littlewood found). Entropy per byte to 10⁹: 4.9189 bits predicted, 4.9188 measured; between neighbouring bytes 0.0321 predicted, 0.0322 measured (after the sampling bias). For the learning run (train to 10⁶, test 10⁶ … 2·10⁶ and 9·10⁶ … 10⁷): a predictor with no training — a slot with a factor ≤ 47 is composite, a survivor is prime with Mertens' 1/(ln n·Π_(r≤47)(1 − 1/r)) — scores 0.832 and 0.826 bits per word, better than the best trained model (0.836, 0.839); and the previous 8 words hold 0.048 and 0.033 bits about the next (18 positions), so no model of the history can go below 1.439 and 1.356 bits; the trained ones reached 1.458 and 1.387 ([info.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/info.py) → [info.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/info.txt)). The models learned the sieve because there is nothing else to learn.

What this adds for the exponents: theories 1 and 2 are the first effects in this study that go the predicted way with the predicted size, and they are effects of the small factors, which a search removes anyway; they change the odds of a prime by a few per cent, not the work of finding one. Chebyshev's class-1 lag does not reach the exponents ([expo12.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/expo12.py) → [expo12.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/expo12.txt)): among all primes up to 136,279,841 class 1 mod 12 is behind the mean of the four classes by 498 of 1,928,410 (0.03%), caused by prime squares, which are never exponents, and far too small to show in 50 exponents. The exponents above 10⁷ fall 4, 6, 2, 2 into the classes 1, 5, 7, 11 (theory 1: 3.6, 3.5, 3.7, 3.2), above 10⁶ 4, 10, 3, 2 (4.9, 4.8, 5.0, 4.3). For the next exponents theory 1 forecasts the shares 25.9%, 25.1%, 26.2% and 22.8% (lane weights at 10⁸); class 11 is the rarest because only there can 2p + 1 be a factor.

## 11. Ten layouts that leave the dots behind (computed; the empty cells and words are theorems, the counts are Hardy–Littlewood, the variance law is Montgomery and Soundararajan's conjecture, the covariance law is our derivation from it)

The 70 layouts above place n and colour it prime or not, so they show only where primes are dense. prime2.html gained ten layouts (80 in all), and then two more without any sieve (82), for what such a picture cannot show: words of consecutive primes, two primes at once, the evenness of the primes over long windows, and the zeros ([lx.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/lx.js), inserted by [patchT.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/patchT.py); every layout run in headless Chrome, 0 script errors). Every number below was recomputed in Python ([check/](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/) → [logs/](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/)); the report page prime2-patterns.html has a card per layout ([mk_texts_ten.py](writeup/code/mersenne/patterns/mk_texts_ten.py)).

- **Chaos game of the gaps mod 6**: gaps ≡ 2 and ≡ 4 take turns (p mod 6 switches), so of the 3⁸ = 6,561 words of 8 gap classes exactly 2·2⁸ − 1 = 511 can occur, and all 511 do up to 500,000 (0 exceptions): a dust of dimension 1 where random gaps draw Sierpinski's triangle. After a gap ≡ 2 the next is ≡ 4 in 6,949 of 12,224 cases ([a_analysis.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/a_analysis.txt)).
- **Chaos game of the remainders mod 12**: a prime repeats the remainder before it 14.3% of the time; step shares from 0.56 (1 → 1) to 1.46 (5 → 7, 11 → 1); 59% of the steps 5 → 7 are twin gaps, 72% of 1 → 1 gaps of 12; mirror pairs agree (3,428 and 3,486).
- **Gap before against gap after**: the cells with both gaps ≡ 2 or both ≡ 4 (mod 6) are empty (two in nine); equal gaps occur 1,668 times, all multiples of 6, against 3,841 for shuffled gaps; mirror symmetry χ² = 113 on 138 cell pairs.
- **The opening recurs**: primes whose next L gaps copy the gaps after 11: 36,971 / 3,738 / 723 / 80 / 18 / 2 / 1 for L = 0 … 6; the nested constellations against Hardy–Littlewood: 4,562 (4,622), 825 (856), 101 (109), 21 (24.5), 3 (4.2), 1 (1.6); at most 15 primes (11 to 67) can recur.
- **Goldbach's first partner** (even n by the least prime p with n − p prime): multiples of 3 need 4.79 tries, the others 6.35; a number ≡ 1 (mod 3) never has a partner ≡ 1, one ≡ 2 never a partner ≡ 2; worst case 413,572 = 389 + 413,183 (OEIS A025018). The gap rule of FINDINGS.txt (sieve, then a coin) carries over with nothing fitted: try p fails if n − p has a factor up to 47 and otherwise succeeds with chance 1/(ln(n − p)·∏(1 − 1/q)); for 4,000 random even n above 100,000 the chance of more than 1, 5, 10, 20, 40 tries is 0.840, 0.387, 0.160, 0.033, 0.0018 against 0.839, 0.385, 0.157, 0.033, 0.0018 found.
- **Mirror** (n against its digits reversed in base B): a reversal keeps the remainder mod B − 1 and, up to sign, mod B + 1, so the reversal of a prime is (B/φ(B))·∏ q/(q − 1) over q | B² − 1 times as likely to be prime: 4.125 in base 10 (reversible primes with 1 to 6 digits 4, 9, 43, 204, 1,499, 9,538; predicted 6, 12, 44, 235, 1,463, 9,843) and 3 in base 2. A prime with a short period in base B ties n to its reversal too while the digits are few: the reversal of a 20-bit prime is a multiple of 7 for 11.0%, of 5 for 17.9%, of 31 for 2.5% (even shares 14.3, 20.0, 3.2%), and with the measured shares the count for 15 / 18 / 20 bits is 568 / 2,874 / 9,175 against 589 / 2,814 / 9,210 found (479 / 2,651 / 8,552 without) ([p6_mirror.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/p6_mirror.txt)).
- **Survivors of the sieve up to P**: with P = 7 and width 48 every column is one remainder mod 210 and the multiples of 11, 13, 17 are every 11th, 13th, 17th dot (0 exceptions); two primes in a row occur 0.957 of the coin's share at P = 7 and 1.000 at P = 197 = N^0.403.
- **Scales** (primes left of n minus primes right of n, windows H = 2^k): the mean square in units of a coin falls from 0.93 (H = 1) to 0.18 (H = 16,384), 0.055 per doubling against ln 2/ln n = 0.057. The long rows are the zeta zeros: rebuilt from the first 160 zeros alone they correlate 0.940 (H = 16,384), 0.904, 0.841 ([a_analysis2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/a_analysis2.txt)). The decoy "no factor up to 47, then a coin" stays at 0.58 from H = 2,048 on (10 to 30 million) while the primes fall from 0.46 to 0.21 at H = 131,072 ([p4.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/p4.txt)): the one picture that tells the primes from the decoy.
- **Atlas** (a column per polynomial m² + m + A, sorted): ln(primes) against ln(√|D|/h(D)) correlation 0.982 over 1,249 columns; Bateman–Horn 99,892 against 99,345 found, correlation 0.997, and the counts differ from it by 0.39 standard deviations of a random count, not 1.
- **Spectrum** (the walk ψ(x) − x, or of a character mod P, as waves in ln x): 13 peaks on the 13 zeta zeros below 60 with heights within 0.02 of 1/|ρ|; mod 12 the peaks 3.81, 6.68, 8.90, 11.21 on the zeros 3.80, 6.69, 8.89, 11.19 of L(s, χ₁₂); mod 19 the low zero 1.5161 at 1.50.
- **Classes repel by their conductors** (heuristic; derived first, tested after; literature not searched). For the primes of class a mod q, weight ln p, in a window of H numbers near x: Cov(a, b) = (H/φ(q))·(ln(x/H) + 1 − γ − ln 2π)·[a = b] + (H/φ(q)²)·T(b/a), T(r) = Σ_(χ ≠ χ₀) χ(r)·ln q_χ (q_χ the conductor), from independent zeros of different L-functions and the Montgomery–Soundararajan variance per character. Mod 12: T(5) = −2.20, T(7) = −2.77, T(11) = 0; measured on 10 to 30 million with H = 256 / 1,024 / 4,096: −2.26 / −2.13 / −2.09, −2.79 / −2.94 / −2.91, 0.03 / 0.01 / 0.05. Mod 8 (−1.39, −2.77, −1.39): −1.41 / −1.47 / −1.47, −2.91 / −2.96 / −2.59, −1.35 / −1.45 / −1.40. Mod 5 (−1.61): −1.70 / −1.69 / −1.70. Variances mod 12: 44.24 / 38.58 / 33.12 against 44.37 / 38.83 / 33.28 ([p7_classcov.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/p7_classcov.txt)).
- **Primes on a line, even above and odd below** (no sieve: the parity of the number of prime factors): above minus below is exactly π(N) + L(N) − 1 (24,996,195 − 22,002,670 = 2,993,525 at 5·10⁷, L = −7,608). The number after a prime leans odd: Σ λ(p + 1) = −289, −1,372, −1,877 at 10⁶, 10⁷, 5·10⁷, below zero 97% of the way from 10⁵ on (log scale). Identity: p + 1 = k·m² with k square-free gives λ(p + 1) = μ(k), so Σ λ(p + 1) = Σ_k μ(k)·#{primes k·m² − 1}; in units of √x/ln x the walk averages −5.2 and the k up to 300 give −4.4. For p − 1: −159, −568, −3,373 (negative 82% of the way) although the first term (primes m² + 1) is positive: open; literature not searched ([nosieve2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/nosieve2.py) → [nosieve2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/nosieve2.txt)).
- **Rows by the class number** (no division: h(−4n) counted form by form; row = the power of 2 in it): the primes halve from row to row, 50.1, 25.0, 12.5, 6.2, 3.1, 1.7, 0.8, 0.4, 0.2% of the 78,497 odd primes up to 10⁶ (PARI); row 0 = p ≡ 3 (mod 4), row 1 = p ≡ 5 (mod 8), deeper = p ≡ 1 (mod 8), row 3 or deeper = x² + 32y² (9,766 of 19,552; Barrucand and Cohn 1969), all with 0 exceptions; a square-free odd number with r prime factors reaches no higher than row r − 1 (genera). The 51 odd exponents: 19, 12, 9, 6, 2, 1, 2 in rows 0 to 6, as their remainders mod 8 give. Also tested and ordinary: the Lehmer signs of the 51 odd exponents (26 plus, 25 minus, 33 runs where 26.5 ± 3.5 are expected; for p ≡ 1 (mod 8) 7 plus and 13 minus, for p ≡ 5 (mod 8) 9 and 3, p = 0.07 for that split alone, one of several), and Mersenne against Wagstaff exponents (9 in common, 3 to 127, where 5.9 are expected if independent) ([nosieve1.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/nosieve1.py) → [nosieve1.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/nosieve1.txt)).
- **The exponents**: 13 of the 52 have a prime reversal in base 10 (14.1 expected) and 16 in base 2 (20.4 expected); nothing beyond chance.
- **What it shows**: seven of the ten are pure sieve (remainder rules plus coin flips reproduce them), the scales and the spectrum are not, and the atlas sits between (sieve, but steadier than coin flips). The open part of the primes is their evenness over long windows, and that is the zeros.

## 12. Fifteen more layouts: no sieve, and puzzle tricks (computed; the tables are probable primes; the lean rule is a Bateman–Horn heuristic; identities are theorems)

The page [prime2.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime2.html) now has 117 layouts. The sources of the 47 added since section 10 are in [lx.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/lx.js); [patchT.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/patchT.py) inserts them into the 70-layout page.

**Five layouts without a sieve** (`lean`, `sqwalk`, `curve`, `famplus`, `famminus`).

- **The lean next to a prime is computed (heuristic), which closes the open point of section 11.** Let S(k) be the sum of λ(p + k) over the primes p ≤ x, with λ = +1 for an even and −1 for an odd number of prime factors. Write p + k = j·m² with j square-free; then λ(p + k) = μ(j), so S(k) = Σ_j μ(j)·#{m : j·m² − k is a prime ≤ x} (identity). Bateman–Horn gives each count: C(j, k)·Σ_m 1/ln(j·m² − k), with C(j, k) the product over primes q of (1 − w/q)/(1 − 1/q), w the number of roots of j·m² ≡ k (mod q). Summed over j ≤ J this is the expectation B(k).
  - Up to 5·10⁷ with 1,500 distances and J = 3,000: slope of S on B 1.02 ± 0.04, correlation 0.62; what is left has a spread of 1,543, coin flips would give 1,732.
  - By what k shares with 30 (found / expected): multiples of 30: −4,046 / −3,147; multiples of 6 without 5: −2,013 / −1,885; nothing shared: −251 / 76.
  - p + 1: found −1,878, expected −1,455. p − 1: found −3,374, expected between −12 and 337 depending on J (two coin-flip spreads away, one of 240 distances).
  - The cutoff J matters a little: at 5·10⁵ the slope is 1.17, 1.16, 1.07, 1.08, 1.05 for J = 300, 1,000, 3,000, 10,000, 30,000.
  - Tested later at a size not looked at before, 10⁸ with 600 distances ([lean_cutoff_big.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/lean_cutoff_big.py)): slope 1.01 ± 0.06, correlation 0.63, what is left 2,141 against 2,400 for coin flips; multiples of 30: −5,187 found, −4,125 expected; p + 1: −2,341 found, −1,958 expected.
  - First run, both signs, |k| ≤ 120, primes q < 20,000 in the constants: slope 0.94 ± 0.09, z = 10.3.
  - Literature: cancellation of λ on shifted primes is proved on average (Lichtman 2020); we found no statement of this lower-order term in a quick search.
- **Square walk.** For a prime Q, step up at n when n is a square mod Q, down when not. For Q ≡ 3 (mod 4) the height at Q/2 is (2 − (2/Q))·h(−Q) and the sum of all heights is Q·h(−Q); for Q ≡ 1 (mod 4) the height at Q/2 is 0 and at Q/4 it is h(−4Q)/2 (Dirichlet). Checked with Euler's criterion against PARI for all 1,123 + 1,114 primes between 100 and 20,000: 0 exceptions. Q = 216,091: h = 85, middle 255, sum 18,367,735.
- **Heights from a curve.** a = p + 1 − #E(F_p) for six curves and the 9,591 odd primes up to 10⁵ (direct count for p < 200 and PARI for all: 0 mismatches). The sum of a·ln p/p ends at 0.35, −0.70, −1.75, −2.63 times ln x for the curves of conductor 11, 37, 389, 5077 (rank 0, 1, 2, 3); ½ − rank is expected. Known theory, shown as a picture.
- **Family trees.** Send every odd prime q to the largest prime factor of q + 1 and repeat: the last prime before 2 is a Mersenne prime, so every odd prime has one Mersenne founder (with q − 1: one Fermat founder). The shares hold still: founder 3 has 68.73, 69.10, 69.11, 69.06% and founder 7 has 24.43, 23.61, 23.86, 24.05% of the primes up to 10⁴, 10⁵, 10⁶, 10⁷; with q − 1 the founders 3, 5, 17, 257 have 52.94, 37.94, 8.59, 0.52% at 10⁷. No formula is known to us. The 37 odd exponents up to 10⁷ by founder: 24, 8, 4, 1 (25.6, 8.9, 2.1, 0.5 expected).

**Ten layouts from puzzle tricks** (`expclass`, `per10`, `per2`, `pow2`, `riesel`, `primorial`, `glue`, `stairs`, `shield`, `lucky`).

- **The exponents by remainder.** A factor of 2^p − 1 is 2kp + 1 ≡ ±1 (mod 8), so the remainder of p decides which k can occur. For primes p up to 5·10⁵, the share with no factor 2kp + 1, k ≤ 200, by p mod 12: 77.4% (7), 75.7% (1), 72.6% (5), 61.2% (11). For p ≡ 11 (mod 12) the first possible factor is 2p + 1; for p ≡ 7 (mod 12), 2p + 1 and 8p + 1 are multiples of 3 and the first possible factor is 10p + 1. Mod 60 the best class is 49 (first possible factor 22p + 1; 82.3%) and the worst 11 (53.8%). Wagstaff's rule only separates p mod 4. The 41 exponents above 120 lie as 9, 18, 10, 4 in the classes 1, 5, 7, 11 (mod 12).
- **A scan of all remainders finds nothing more.** Over the moduli 3 to 120 the strongest single class of the 41 exponents has z = 5.91 (5 exponents ≡ 61 mod 95); 27% of 300 decoy sets (random primes of the same sizes) have a class at least as strong. Gaps before and after an exponent: 1.03 and 1.06 times ln p (decoys: 0.85 to 1.22).
- **Around the powers of two.** Primes 2^n ± d for n ≤ 400 and odd d ≤ 299: 3,868 (gmpy2 recount: the same). The row 2^n − 1 holds the exponents 2 to 127 and 2^n + 1 holds n = 1, 2, 4, 8, 16. The row 2^n − 7 holds only n = 39 (8 of the 60 classes of n mod 60 survive 3, 5, 7, 11, 13), 2^n + 7 holds 20, and one row of the 300 is empty: 2^n − 221. Sieve to 2,000, then a coin with chance e^γ·ln 2,000/ln x: 3,849 expected; correlation of expected and found per row 0.94.
- **k·2^n ± 1** for n ≤ 400, odd k ≤ 299: 3,475 primes (3,467 expected, correlation 0.93); the only empty row is 47·2^n + 1.
- **Around the primorials** p# ± d for the first 100 primes, odd d ≤ 499: 1,482 primes (1,504 expected, 0.96); p# + 1 is prime for p = 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 31, 379 and p# − 1 for p = 3, 5, 11, 13, 41, 89, 317, 337.
- **Blocks to glue.** Runs of k primes n, n + W, …: for W = 210 up to 5·10⁵ there are 5,031, 1,655, 516, 158, 49, 17, 4, 1 runs of at least 3, …, 10 (sieve then coin: 5,139, 1,726, 559, 173, 50, 13, 3.0, 0.5); the longest starts at 199.
- **Rising gaps.** Runs of k rising gaps up to 5·10⁵: 6,050, 1,311, 215, 32, 2 for k = 2 to 6; the same gaps shuffled inside blocks of 1,000: 4,944, 864, 120, 17, 2. The first run of six is 128,981 with gaps 2, 4, …, 14.
- **Shielded primes** (conjecture 111): 5,640 of 41,538 primes stay (13.58%), 10,590 of 78,498 (13.49%); 1/e² = 13.53%.
- **Lucky numbers.** 38,015 up to 5·10⁵; 4,877 primes are lucky where independent sets among the odd numbers would share 6,316. The first strike leaves only numbers ≡ 1 or 3 (mod 6), so every lucky prime after 3 is ≡ 1 (mod 6); inside that class 4,712 are expected.
- **Period of 1/p even or odd.** Base 10, mod 40: always even in the classes 7, 11, 17, 19, 21, 23, 29, 33, always odd in 3, 27, 31, 39, mixed in 1, 9, 13, 37; 66.7% even. Base 2, mod 8: 3 and 5 even, 7 odd, 1 mixed; 70.9% even (17/24, Hasse).

**Ten layouts with new shapes** (`galaxy`, `globe`, `donut`, `crystal`, `dragon`, `gosper`, `fan`, `bubbles`, `curlicue`, `road`). They add geometry, not arithmetic: a galaxy, a globe and a ring whose arms, meridians and bands are the remainders mod P; a cube of side 84 seen at an angle (primes in 24 of its 84 sheets); the dragon curve (after 2 every prime lies on a piece of one slope: 20,674 pointing right, 20,863 left); the flowsnake (7 levels, 823,543 steps); a fan in which n = a × b stands at the angle of its most square rectangle, so the primes lie on the bottom edge (332,967 of the numbers up to 5·10⁵ have a at least the fourth root of n); the primes on a spiral with every gap as a bubble (4,565 twin bubbles; the largest is the gap of 114 after 492,113); a curlicue walk as a neutral thread; rows in perspective.

**Ten more shapes** (`daisy`, `mobius`, `canopy`, `disc`, `heart`, `fibsquares`, `dna`, `popcorn`, `terdragon`, `nautilus`). A daisy and a Möbius band by remainder mod P; a tree whose branches follow the binary digits; rows cut to a disc and to a heart; the golden spiral of Fibonacci squares with sides 1 to 610; a double helix with 6k − 1 on one strand and 6k + 1 on the other (4,564 rungs with both ends prime, 32,408 with one, 46,361 with none up to 5·10⁵); every fraction between 0 and 1 once, by Stern's sequence (largest a + b 10,946; every odd n above 1 lies right of 1/2); the terdragon; a nautilus with one chamber per power of two. One count needed explaining: on the terdragon the primes lie 11,022 / 19,813 / 10,703 on the three directions. The direction of piece n is the number of digits 1 of n − 1 in base 3, mod 3 (0 exceptions up to 5·10⁵); for odd n that number is even, and among 12 digits the even counts fall 23.8 / 47.8 / 28.4% on the three directions.

**Tested and ordinary:** the period of the continued fraction of √n (primes 0.82·√n on average, composites 0.32·√n; x² − ny² = −1 solvable for 68.3% of the eligible composites up to 2·10⁵, limit 58.1%); numbers p + 2^k (8 of the 32 odd exponents up to 10⁶ are not of that form, 4.7 expected, binomial tail 0.09); 2 is a primitive root for 15 of the 51 odd exponents (19.1 expected); Collatz stopping times of primes against composites coprime to 6 (difference −0.11 ± 0.25 between 10⁵ and 10⁶ with size removed).

## 13. Backtest of the layouts and a search that improves itself (computed; the coin model's limit near squares is an exact count, its explanation is a heuristic)

Every layout is now scored, and the scores steer the next layouts. The page has 171 layouts, every note on it ends with the layout's own score, the same score stands in a strip under the picture, and a panel at the bottom of the page gives the findings and a sortable table of the 94 scored layouts; the scripts are in [backtest/](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/).

**The scorer** ([score.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/score.js), run in the page itself). For each of the 24 nearest neighbour directions in a picture it takes all pairs of numbers that sit one such step apart and measures how much "this one is prime" tells about "that one is prime" (mutual information). The sum, in thousandths of a bit, is the *structure* of the layout. The same is done for eight decoy sets: the sieve up to N^0.4027 with a coin above it (our gap model), with the coin's rate matched to the primes in blocks of 4,096 numbers. *Beyond* is the root mean square, over the directions, of the z-score between the pair ratio of the primes and that of the decoys; about 0.5 means the decoys explain the picture. Calibrated with a ninth decoy set scored as if it were the primes ([gen_null.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen_null.txt), 80 maps in two ranges): mean 0.50, 95 % of the values below 0.73, maximum 0.85.

**The scoreboard** ([scores_all.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/scores_all.txt), N = 500,000).
- Of the 78 layouts that place n by a formula and can be scored, none shows more than the decoys: the structure of the primes is 0.999 of the decoys' in the median (0.94 to 1.05), *beyond* has median 0.50 and maximum 1.39.
- Most structure: wheels within wheels 1,165; galaxy with 30 arms 662; the tuned map 565; Möbius band 455; wheel 429; daisy 377; pentaflake 334; rows of width 210 275; plain rows 198; Ulam spiral 201; Hilbert 179; Sacks 29; dragon 24; curlicue 0.7.
- The exponents: the mean distance from an exponent to the nearest other exponent, against 200 decoy sets of primes of similar rank, has z between −2.5 and 1.9 over the layouts (mean 0.5, sd 0.9). No layout gathers them.
- 51 layouts place or colour by more than n (gaps, factors, orders) and cannot be compared this way; 17 have too few neighbouring dots.

**The search** ([gensearch.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gensearch.js); [gen1.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen1.txt), [gen2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen2.txt), [gen3.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen3.txt)). 537 generated maps in three rounds (rows, power spirals, turns by a fixed angle, between-squares maps, sheared cubes), each scored on n ≤ 500,000 and again on 500,000 < n ≤ 10⁶.
- Structure carries over (correlation 0.994 between the two ranges) and improved from 411 to 565 and then stayed: a cube of side 30 with each layer shifted by (−2, 1), now the layout `tuned`. Its decoys score 562.
- *Beyond* does not carry over: the 15 best maps of round 1 average 0.62 in-sample and 0.56 on the unseen range, where all maps average 0.55; in round 3 the correlation between the two ranges is 0.00.

- A fifth round of 845 maps repeats this family by family ([gen5_summary.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen5_summary.txt)). The 10 best in-sample of each family, in-sample / on the unseen range / family average: rows 0.44 / 0.44 / 0.44; power spirals 0.59 / 0.60 / 0.62; turns by a fixed angle 0.61 / 0.64 / 0.62; sheared cubes 0.61 / 0.61 / 0.57. Only the 60 maps built on squares or triangular numbers keep their lead: 0.74 / 0.79 against a family average of 0.58, and they are 8 of the 15 best of all maps. Scored against the improved model described below (factor ratios up to 6 struck), the lead is gone ([gen7_summary.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen7_summary.txt)): that family's 10 best give 0.24 / 0.49 against a family average of 0.46, none of the 15 best maps is built on squares, and the highest value of all 845 maps on the unseen range is 0.78.

**One thing did carry over, and it is a limit of the coin model.** The between-squares maps (n = r² + x at column x of row r) score about 1.0 in both ranges. A count to 2·10⁷ ([quad_probe.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_probe.py), [quad_surv.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_surv.py)) shows why.
- Take pairs n, n + h with h = 2r + 2k, r = ⌊√n⌋. For the primes the pair ratio is flat in k: 2.001 to 2.009 for k = −4 … 4 (noise 0.005).
- For the survivors of the sieve to 871 = N^0.4027 it is not: it deviates by +0.26, −0.44, +0.34, +0.10, −0.81, +0.78, +0.25, −0.53, +0.04 % for k = −4 … 4 (an exact count), although the product of the local factors is flat to 0.01 %. A coin cannot repair that, so the model is off by up to 0.8 %: z = +4.2 at h = 2r, −2.8 at 2r + 2, +3.5 at 4r + 2, −2.6 at 4r + 6 (16 decoys), and the same signs in both halves of the range. For fixed h (6,000, 6,002, 6,006, 8,190) |z| ≤ 0.94.
- By sieve depth the deviations are at most 0.02 % (to 13), 0.28 % (to 100), 0.81 % (to 871), 0.39 % (to 2,000) and within noise for the full sieve. So the composites with two large factors, which the model places by coin, are not placed at random relative to the squares.
- The cause is a small group of them ([quad_fermat.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_fermat.py)). Every such composite is a difference of two squares t² − s²; 8,348 of the 349,465 have t = r + 1, that is n = (r + 1 − s)(r + 1 + s). Taking only those out cuts the deviation from 0.47 % to 0.17 % (root mean square over k; the primes alone give 0.16 %). Their partner n + 2r + 2k shares an algebraic factor with them for some k and none for others.
- The scope ([quad_scope.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_scope.py); survivors against primes, root mean square over k = −3 … 3): distances 2√n 0.52 % against 0.17 %, 4√n 0.54 / 0.18, 2√(2n) 0.53 / 0.21, √(2n) 0.42 / 0.08, 2√(3n) 0.40 / 0.21; no effect for 3√n (0.18 / 0.23), for distances tied to the cube root (0.30 / 0.34) or to √n·ln n (0.11 / 0.09). A fourth search round over maps n = k·r^e + x agrees ([gen4.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen4.txt)): only e = 2 with k = 1, 2, 4 and the triangular maps reach 0.8 to 1.3 on unseen numbers; cubes and the powers 1.5 and 2.5 stay at 0.3 to 0.65. The layout `fermat` draws the survivors between the squares with these composites in green.
- This is Hardy–Littlewood holding for the primes and the sieve-then-coin model failing by 1 % for distances tied to √n. It is the reason the layouts built on squares (Legendre, Klauber, the triangle, the diagonals, the clock) have the highest *beyond* on the scoreboard, 1.0 to 1.4.

**The scores are stable, and so is the list of exceptions** ([stab.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/stab.txt); boards for [N = 500,000](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/board_5e5.txt) and [N = 10⁶](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/board_1e6.txt), 91 layouts scored at both sizes). Structure keeps its order (rank correlation 0.96). *Beyond* keeps part of it (0.70), and the ten layouts highest at both sizes are all built on squares or triangular numbers: the diagonals, Floyd's triangle, the tree, Sierpinski, the diamond, the clock, Klauber, the hexagon spiral, Legendre and Sacks (1.0 to 1.6; all others near 0.5). The clustering of the exponents does not repeat (correlation 0.25; mean z 0.52 and 0.10): noise. With more data the square effect stays: up to 10⁸, sieve to 1,666, the primes are flat to 0.07 % (noise 0.13 %) and the survivors deviate by +0.24, −0.31, +0.32, +0.03, −0.51, +0.42, +0.07, −0.35, +0.09 % for k = −4 … 4, the same signs as at 2·10⁷ ([quad_big.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_big.py)).

**Nothing else fails** ([relation_scan.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/relation_scan.py), odd n from 10⁶ to 2·10⁷, 12 decoy sets). For 25 ways to pick a partner m of n the share of pairs with both prime was compared with sieve-and-coin: fixed distances (2, 6, 30, 2,310), 2n ± 1, 4n + 1, 6n + 1, distances tied to n^(1/4), n^(1/3), n^(2/3) and ln² n, the digits and the bits reversed, single bits flipped, distances made from the digits of n, a partner near n·φ, and N − n (Goldbach pairs). All have |z| ≤ 1.6 except the two tied to √n: n + 2⌊√n⌋ with z = +4.1 and n + 2⌊√n⌋ + 2 with z = −2.9.

**The model, improved and tested again** ([model_versions.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/model_versions.txt), N = 10⁶; `score.js` takes the version as an argument). Version 1 is the sieve and the coin. Version 2 also strikes, before the coin, the products of two large primes that are nearly equal (q·c with (q + c)/2 within 1 of √n: 6.3 % of the leftover composites). Version 3 also strikes those whose factors are close to the ratios 2, 3, 3/2 and 4 (a·q + b·c within 2 of √(4ab·n); 35.5 % in all, [model_v3_count.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/model_v3_count.py)). *Beyond* for the nine square-based layouts, versions 1 / 2 / 3: Legendre 1.13 / 0.51 / 0.56, Klauber 1.12 / 0.59 / 0.63, Sacks 0.95 / 0.66 / 0.46, diagonals 1.57 / 1.52 / 0.68, Floyd 1.55 / 1.49 / 0.66, tree 1.54 / 1.50 / 0.69, diamond 1.33 / 1.28 / 0.50, hexagon 1.18 / 1.03 / 0.67, clock 1.09 / 1.08 / 0.80; controls stay where they were (rows 0.35 / 0.34 / 0.23, Hilbert 0.35 / 0.34 / 0.41). So equal factors explain the layouts built on squares, and the ratio 2 the ones built on triangular numbers; with version 3 no layout of the page exceeds its decoys: over the 92 that can be scored at N = 10⁶ *beyond* has median 0.47 and maximum 0.80 ([board_1e6_v3.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/board_1e6_v3.txt)). On the final page of 171 layouts, 94 can be scored at N = 500,000: against sieve-and-coin *beyond* has median 0.50 and maximum 1.39, with 8 layouts above 1.0; against version 3 the median is 0.39, the maximum 0.81 and none is above 1.0 ([board_final_summary.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/board_final_summary.txt)); both numbers stand in each note. The layouts `struck` and `pairs` show the struck composites, the second as pairs of primes with the five ratios as bands. Counted directly on pairs up to 2·10⁷ ([model_v3_pairs.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/model_v3_pairs.py); root mean square deviation over k, sieve only / version 2 / version 3 / primes): distance 2√n 0.52 / 0.19 / 0.17 / 0.17 %; 4√n 0.54 / 0.18 / 0.16 / 0.18; 2√(2n) 0.53 / 0.53 / 0.24 / 0.21; 2√(3n) 0.40 / 0.40 / 0.18 / 0.21; 2√(6n) 0.28 / 0.29 / 0.18 / 0.17. The distance 2√(5n) stays at 0.54 / 0.53 / 0.51 against 0.20 for the primes: the ratio 5 is not in version 3, so each ratio needs its own strike, as the cause predicts. There version 3 strikes 50,454 of the 349,465 leftover composites. The prediction was then tested ([model_v4_pairs.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/model_v4_pairs.py)): with the ratios up to 6 struck as well (5, 5/2, 5/3, 4/3, 5/4, 6, 6/5), the distance 2√(5n) falls from 0.54 to 0.17 %. Against that model none of 60 maps n = k·r² + x (k ≤ 40) and n = k·r(r + 1)/2 + x (k ≤ 20) exceeds 0.75 in both ranges, where 3 did against sieve-and-coin ([gen6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gen6.txt)).
