The effect shrinks slowly with size ([quad_decay.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_decay.py)): at k = 0 the survivors of the sieve to N^0.4027 are off by −2.04, −1.45, −0.89, −0.64, −0.51 % for N = 10⁶, 3·10⁶, 10⁷, 3·10⁷, 10⁸, about like N^−0.3; at the size of the page it is 2 %.

**An older number explained on the way.** Section 11 found that prime counts along the polynomials m² + m + A scatter with 0.39 of the spread of a Poisson count. That is the coin of the gap model: at its horizon x^0.4027 a survivor is prime with rate ρ ≈ 0.85 to 0.87, a count of coin flips with that rate has √(1 − ρ) = 0.36 to 0.39 of the Poisson spread, and the measured variance is 1.07, 1.07 and 1.11 times the coin's (A < 2,500, 10,000, 20,000; [poly_coin.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/poly_coin.py)). With a shallower sieve the counts scatter far more than the coin (2.0 to 2.4 times at x^0.3, 4.9 to 9.0 times at x^0.2), because each polynomial then keeps its own small primes.

**Backtest of rules for the exponents** ([exp_backtest.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/exp_backtest.py)). The 40 exponents above 500 were split in two halves of 20.
- Mined rules: over all moduli 3 to 500, the 10 classes that hold most of the first half hold 22 of them where 0.5 is expected by chance; of the second half they hold 0 (0.5 expected). The best 200: 438 against 23.9 in the first half, 33 against 23.9 in the second.
- The factor rule of section 12 (a class weighs as much as the share of its primes p whose 2^p − 1 has no small factor): log-likelihood gain +0.55 on the first half and +0.31 on the second with the classes mod 12 (all 40: +0.86, reached by 7 % of decoy sets), mod 4: +0.13 and +0.34 (all: +0.46, 1.3 %), mod 60: +0.57 and +0.31 (all: +0.87, 5.7 %). It is positive on both halves for every modulus; mined rules are not. With weights from all primes up to 5·10⁵ and k ≤ 2,000 ([exp_class_fine.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/exp_class_fine.py)) the gain on the first / last 20 exponents is +0.12 / +0.32 (mod 4), +0.49 / +0.23 (mod 12), +0.55 / +0.22 (mod 60), +0.79 / +0.22 (mod 420): finer classes help on the half they resemble and neither help nor hurt on the other.

## 14. A model with random remainder classes, tested at every scale (computed; the model is Banks, Ford and Tao's, the variance law is Montgomery and Soundararajan's conjecture, the bias in the race is Rubinstein and Sarnak's, the factor rule is Wagstaff's heuristic)

The coin model of section 13 fails near squares. This section replaces the coin and tests the result against the primes from the shortest distances to the longest ([ladder.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/ladder.py) → [ladder_2e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/ladder_2e7.txt), N = 2·10⁷, statistics on 10⁷ to 2·10⁷, five draws per model).

**The model.** A number n survives when, for every prime q up to z(n), it avoids one remainder class mod q. For q ≤ 31 the class is 0 (the true sieve). For larger q the class is drawn by lot, once per q. A prime q takes part from n = exp(1/∏(1 − 1/q′)) on, the product over the primes q′ < q, so that the density is 1/ln n; this puts z(n) near n^0.56 (12,487 at 2·10⁷). The count up to 2·10⁷ is 1,270,680 ± 291 against 1,270,607 primes, with no calibration. This is the model of Banks, Ford and Tao with the true classes kept for the small primes.

**What it matches.**

| Test | Primes | Class model | Coin model |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pairs n, n + 2 | 48,427 | 48,438 ± 131 | 48,445 ± 194 |
| Pairs n, n + 6 | 96,611 | 97,026 ± 100 | 97,111 ± 149 |
| Triples n, n + 2, n + 6 | 6,343 | 6,375 ± 70 | 6,392 ± 59 |
| Quadruples n, n + 2, n + 6, n + 8 | 569 | 549 ± 18 | 569 ± 26 |
| Distances 2⌊√n⌋ + 2k, rms deviation over k | 0.18 % | 0.23 ± 0.05 % | 0.49 ± 0.05 % |
| Scatter of the count in windows of 1,024 (share of a random count) | 0.490 | 0.496 ± 0.009 | 0.555 ± 0.006 |
| The same, windows of 8,192 | 0.346 | 0.383 ± 0.024 | 0.564 ± 0.008 |
| The same, windows of 65,536 | 0.238 | 0.276 ± 0.018 | 0.479 ± 0.026 |
| Largest gap up to 2·10⁷ | 180 | 184 ± 17 | 186 ± 18 |
| Gaps of 100 or more | 651 | 648 ± 24 | 684 ± 27 |
| Next member has the same remainder mod 3 | 0.4333 | 0.4339 ± 0.0005 | 0.4337 ± 0.0005 |

- The ± is the spread over the five draws; the counts of the primes scatter as well (the square root of 96,611 is 311), so the pairs, triples and quadruples agree within twice that scatter.
- The class model passes the square test without the striking of section 13: a class drawn by lot has no place relative to the squares.
- The scatter in windows follows (ln(n/H) + 1 − γ − ln 2π)/ln n (0.495, 0.369 and 0.243 for the three window lengths). A coin cannot go below its own noise and levels off near 0.5; a sieve that strikes whole classes keeps falling with the primes.
- With the classes drawn by lot for every prime, 2 and 3 included, the results are the same (column "class z0=1" of the log). With the true sieve up to 871 the density comes out 2 % too low (Buchstab), so that variant is not used.

**Where it differs.**

- The race mod 4 ([race_draws.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/race_draws.py) → [race_draws.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/race_draws.txt); [race_x.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/race_x.py) → [race_x.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/race_x.txt)). Up to 2·10⁷ the primes ≡ 3 lead 99.8 % of the time and end 265 ahead. Forty draws of the model lead 49 % ± 28 % of the time and end at −18 ± 321. The race of the model is fair. Its swings have the size of those of the primes (√n/ln n = 266) and are far below the 1,127 of a coin toss per member. One of six further draws led 99.9 % of the time, so a single draw cannot be told from the primes by this test. The bias of the primes comes from the squares of primes (Rubinstein and Sarnak), and a model of lots has no squares.
- Goldbach ([goldbach.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/goldbach.py) → [goldbach.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/goldbach.txt)). For the even n from 10⁶ to 2·10⁶ the number of sums of two members scatters around its Hardy–Littlewood value with 0.27 of the variance of a random count for the primes, 0.30 to 0.33 in four draws of the class model (each with its own singular series, the lots included) and 0.37 for the coin model.

**The factor rule for 2^p − 1, measured** ([mers_law.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/mers_law.py) → [mers_law.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/mers_law.txt)). For the 5,889 primes p from 1,000 to 60,000 the smallest factor 2kp + 1 below 2³⁰ was found by trial. The share of p with no factor below B is 0.947 to 0.972 times ln(a·p)/ln B for B = 2¹⁸ to 2³⁰, with a = 2 for p ≡ 3 and a = 6 for p ≡ 1 (mod 4); the ratio does not move with B. No factor below 2³⁰: 55.9 %, 53.6 %, 57.5 % and 42.1 % for p ≡ 1, 5, 7, 11 (mod 12). The 13 exponents in the range are among the 3,076 primes without a factor.

**On the page.** Ten layouts: the primes and the model side by side in rows and in Ulam spirals, a quiz (which half is real), three walks of count minus expected, the scatter by window length, the race mod 4, Goldbach's comet for both, the gaps of both, the diffraction of both (peaks 1/φ(q)² at the fractions with square-free denominator q, the same for both), and every prime at the height of the first factor of 2^p − 1, with the exponents in the band of primes that give up no factor.

## 15. Binary layers, and primes found by counting only (the floor is proved; the fold is an identity; the pair counts follow Hardy and Littlewood's conjecture; the rest is computed)

No sieve and no known prime test is used in this section: the numbers are written in binary and taken apart layer by layer, and primes are picked out by adding and counting.

**The fold (identity).** Cut the binary digits of n in pieces of w bits and add the pieces; repeat until one piece is left. Because 2^w ≡ 1 (mod 2^w − 1), the result is n mod (2^w − 1), found without dividing. A prime q shows in the fold (its multiples fill fixed columns) exactly when q divides 2^w − 1.

**The floor (proved).** The depth of an odd prime q is the smallest w with q | 2^w − 1 (the order of 2 mod q, the period of 1/q in binary). From q | 2^w − 1 follows q ≤ 2^w − 1, so depth ≥ log₂(q + 1): a prime of b bits needs at least b layers. Equality holds exactly when q = 2^w − 1. The Mersenne primes are the primes on this floor, and the Mersenne exponents are their depths.

- Checked ([check6.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/check6.py) → [check6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/check6.txt)) for the 13,009 odd primes up to 140,000: the depth is at least log₂(q + 1) for all, with equality for 3, 7, 31, 127, 8,191 and 131,071. The depth is q − 1 for 4,859 of them, 37.35 % (Artin's conjecture: 37.4 %).
- The primes that first show at w layers, for every w up to 200 ([depth2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/depth2.py) → [depth200.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/depth200.txt), [depth_rows_200.json](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/depth_rows_200.json); PARI): 420 primes; only the rows 1 and 6 are empty (Bang's theorem). The rows that hold the single prime 2^w − 1 are 2, 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 19, 31, 61, 89, 107 and 127. In the other rows of a prime w the w bits are shared, for example 4.5 + 6.5 for w = 11, 7.4 + 75.6 for w = 83 and 12.9 + 184.1 for w = 197.

**What a fold tells about primality** ([fold1.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/fold1.py) → [fold1.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/fold1.txt); odd numbers of 21 bits, 14.04 % prime, full knowledge 0.585 bits).

| Layers w | 2^w − 1 | Information (bits) |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | 3 | 0.090 |
| 3 | 7 | 0.034 |
| 4 | 3 · 5 | 0.142 |
| 5 | 31 | 0.007 |
| 6 | 3² · 7 | 0.126 |
| 7 | 127 | 0.002 |
| 8 | 3 · 5 · 17 | 0.156 |
| 12 | 3² · 5 · 7 · 13 | 0.194 |
| 13 | 8,191 | 0.000 |

The information equals what the prime factors of 2^w − 1 give and nothing more (the log lists both). A number of layers that is a Mersenne exponent shows one prime only, and from 13 on that prime is too large to tell anything.

**Layers one at a time, in pairs, in sets** ([bits1.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/bits1.py) → [bits1.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/bits1.txt), [bits2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/bits2.py) → [bits2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/bits2.txt)).

- One layer: each of the bits 1 to 19 of the 73,586 primes of 21 bits is 1 for 49.4 % to 50.3 % of them.
- Two layers: the largest information between two bit layers is 0.00006 bits.
- Ones in the even layers against ones in the odd layers: a number is divisible by 3 exactly when the two counts differ by a multiple of 3 (2 ≡ −1 mod 3), so every third diagonal of that grid has no prime but 3.
- Sets of layers: for each of the 1,048,575 sets, split the odd numbers below 2²¹ by the parity of their ones in the set; bias = (share of primes in the even half − share in the odd half)/(2 × overall share), noise 0.0023. The top layer alone: 0.054 (the primes thin out). All layers 1 to 20: 0.034 (primes more often have an odd number of ones). Sets that repeat every four layers, such as 110011001100100010000: 0.039. The 1,000 strongest sets hold 5.3 % of the total, and their biases are reproduced with correlation 0.956 by the numbers without a factor 3, 5, 7, 11 or 13. The parities see the small primes through the layers and nothing else.

**Primes found by counting only** ([add1.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/add1.py) → [add1.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/add1.txt)). Input: the list of primes; operations: adding and counting.

- Rarest distances. For an odd n count the pairs of primes that lie 2n apart. Every odd prime factor p of n makes that distance (p − 1)/(p − 2) times more common (Hardy and Littlewood), so the count is lowest when n has no small factor. With the primes up to 10⁶ and odd n below 50,000, taking in each block of 2,048 odd numbers as many of the lowest counts as the block has primes gives 93.5 % primes (4,724 of 5,050; 20.5 % of these n are prime). With the primes up to 500,000 and n up to 10,000: 98.4 % (1,208 of 1,228). Measured steps for the smallest factor 3, 5, 7, 11: 2.11, 1.37, 1.21, 1.12 (rule: 2, 1.33, 1.2, 1.11; the surplus is the other factors of n).
- Prime plus twice a prime. The number of ways n = p + 2q behaves the same: 67.1 % for the odd n between 500,000 and 10⁶ (14.8 % are prime); steps 2.11, 1.36, 1.22, 1.12.
- Three primes work the other way round: the highest counts of n = p + q + r find 44.6 %.
- These counts see the small factors of n, as the sieve does, by another road; they do not see more.

**On the page.** Seven layouts: the primes by the number of layers in which they first show, with the exponents on the diagonal; the depth of every prime up to 140,000 with the floor; the fold in P layers; even against odd layers; the parity bias of every set of layers; the rarest distances; prime plus twice a prime.

## 16. Without carries, rotations, runs and other bases (the splitting rule is classical; the carry lemma and the rotation and run statements are proved; the link between the two kinds of division is measured)

**Multiplying without carries.** Add the shifted copies of a long multiplication so that two ones in a column cancel (exclusive or). Bit patterns then multiply as polynomials over the field of two elements.

- The strip of p ones (the number 2^p − 1) splits, without carries, into (p − 1)/d patterns of d + 1 bits, where d is the order of 2 mod p, the binary depth of p itself; it stays whole exactly when d = p − 1 (classical: the factors of x^p − 1). Computed for the exponents up to 127 ([lay2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/lay2.py) → [lay2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/lay2.txt)): 127 = 11 ⊗ 13; 131,071 = 313 ⊗ 471; 2,147,483,647 = 37 ⊗ 41 ⊗ 47 ⊗ 55 ⊗ 59 ⊗ 61; 2^89 − 1 is 8 patterns of 12 bits; 2^127 − 1 is the 18 patterns of 8 bits that cannot be split. The strips for p = 3, 5, 13, 19, 61, 107 stay whole.
- The carry lemma (proved). If a · b is a strip of p ones with p prime and a, b > 1, the multiplication carries. Without carries every column of the stack holds at most one 1, so the numbers of ones multiply: ones(a) · ones(b) = p. Then one factor has a single 1 and is a power of 2, which an odd number above 1 is not.
- Of the 51 odd exponents, 15 give a strip that stays whole (2 is a primitive root). Primes with the same remainders mod 8 give 15.7 ± 2.0 (the share is 0.748 for the remainders 3 and 5, measured below 2·10⁶, and 0 for 1 and 7). No link.
- Prime with carries against unsplittable without carries ([two.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/two.py) → [two.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/two.txt)). Below 2²² there are 295,947 primes, 210,871 unsplittable patterns and 31,937 that are both. An unsplittable pattern ends in 1 and has an odd number of ones, so the comparison is made inside those numbers and length by length: the overlap is 1.026, 1.024 and 1.001 times the independent value for 20, 21 and 22 bits (4,517, 7,546 and 14,165 numbers). Pooled over all lengths the same data would show a false link of 5 %, because short numbers hold more of both kinds.

**Where the two kinds of division do agree** ([link.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/link.py) → [link.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/link.txt)). For all numbers below 2^L: how often "2^w − 1 divides n" and "the strip of w ones divides n without carries" hold together, as a multiple of chance.

| w | 2^w − 1 | L = 16 | L = 20 | L = 24 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 3 | 1.20 | 1.11 | 1.06 |
| 3 | 7 | 1.73 | 1.43 | 1.30 |
| 4 | 15 | 3.31 | 2.43 | 1.91 |
| 5 | 31 | 6.17 | 4.65 | 3.05 |
| 6 | 63 | 12.5 | 8.13 | 6.59 |
| 7 | 127 | 36.9 | 17.0 | 11.0 |
| 8 | 255 | 128 | 42.9 | 25.5 |

Mismatched pairs give chance: 3 with the strip 111: 1.002; 7 with the strip 11: 1.001; 7 with the strip 11111: 1.000; 31 with the strip 111: 1.000 (9, which divides 2⁶ − 1, with the strip 111: 1.10). For w = 2 this is Newman's phenomenon (multiples of 3 prefer an even number of ones); the table shows it for every width and growing fast with it. One cause: when the shifted copies of the strip do not collide, the product is the same number with and without carries.

**Rotations, runs, bases** ([lay2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/lay2.py)).

- Rotations (proved). Write a prime in binary and rotate the digits as a ring. If the pattern has a 0, some rotation ends in 0 and is even. So the primes all of whose rotations are prime are exactly the Mersenne primes. Below 2²⁰: 3, 7, 31, 127, 8,191, 131,071, 524,287; the best of the others with 20 bits is 786,431 (12 of 20); on average 19.2 % of the rotations of a prime are prime.
- Runs. A prime above 2 has an odd number of runs of equal bits; one run is a Mersenne prime. Below 2²⁰: 7, 225, 2,684, 12,557, 26,339, 25,870, 11,821, 2,361, 157 and 3 primes with 1, 3, …, 19 runs.
- Bases. The numbers 1 + b + … + b^(w−1) with w ≥ 3 below 10¹³: 3,186,384 values, and only 31 (bases 2 and 5) and 8,191 (bases 2 and 90) occur twice (Goormaghtigh's conjecture says there is no other).

**On the page.** Six layouts: the stack that builds 2^P − 1 without carries next to the stack with carries; the multiples of 2^P − 1 with the numbers the strip divides in green; prime, unsplittable, or both; the rotations; the runs; all ones in base b.

## 17. The first 30, 100 or 250 numbers as gears (a page to watch; the rule is elementary, the history is literature)

[prime3.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime3.html) shows the numbers 1 to 30 (or 1 to 100, or 1 to 250) as gears that turn when Start is pressed. Gear k has k teeth and every tick moves every gear one tooth, so gear k is home every k ticks: at tick t the gears at home are the divisors of t, and t is prime when only gear 1 and gear t are home. With only the prime gears the machine is a sieve that is right up to tick 960 (below 31²). D. H. Lehmer built such a sieve from bicycle chains in 1926 and from gear wheels in 1932.

Ten machines show the same rule: wheels of one size that move one tooth per tick; a train of meshing gears; rings around one centre; chains on one shaft; pistons on a camshaft; drums that show the remainders; one dial on which wheel k turns k times as fast (after a/b of a turn the hands lie on b rays and the multiples of b are home); gears that roll inside a ring of 30 and draw a curve with 30/gcd(k, 30) petals that closes after lcm(k, 30) ticks; a tape on which the gears print the divisors; and gears of true size on a rack. Choices: a slider and a number box for how many numbers (2 to 5,000; above 250 every gear is drawn as a small square, and the gear train, the rack, the drums and the rolling gears show the first 250); colour for the primes; colour for the gears whose number is a Mersenne exponent (2, 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 19, 31, 61, 89, 107, 127); pointers and lamps; numbers; sound. Changing a dropdown sets the tick counter and the records back to zero.

Nine more machines change the rule of motion (each fact below was checked by a separate short script for all wheels up to 250).

- Doubling wheels: tooth x goes to tooth 2x. Wheel k is home when k divides 2^t − 1, so at a prime tick p with no wheel home 2^p − 1 has no factor up to the largest wheel: trial factoring as a machine. Even wheels never come home.
- Fibonacci wheels: wheel k stands on tooth F(t); the wheels at home are the divisors of F(t), and every wheel up to 250 comes home.
- Prime clock: the wheels move only on prime ticks; home are the divisors of π(t).
- Accelerating wheels: the step grows by one tooth per tick; wheel k is home when k divides t(t + 1)/2, an odd prime wheel p at the ticks p − 1 and p.
- Square jumps: every wheel stands on tooth t²; the teeth it can reach are the squares, (p + 1)/2 of them on an odd prime wheel, 6 on wheel 15 and 24 on wheel 105.
- Back and forth: the machine reverses after every prime tick, so the wheels have moved D(t) = the prime gaps added and subtracted in turn; D(t) = 0 at 134 ticks up to 100,000, first at 36 and 38.
- Slipping wheels: wheel k stands still when the tick shares a factor with k; a prime wheel p is first home at tick p + 1.
- Wheels on wheels: an arm with a joint for every number, piece k turning once every k ticks; the tip draws a curve.
- Lucas–Lehmer wheels: the wheel for the odd prime p has 2^p − 1 teeth, starts on tooth 4 and jumps from s to s² − 2; it stands on tooth 0 at tick p − 2 exactly for p = 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 19, 31, 61, 89, 107, 127 among the primes up to 127.

Machine 20 is a pattern machine that can be aimed. Every wheel stands on tooth a(t), counted around the wheel, so wheel k is home when k divides a(t); a tick with no wheel but wheel 1 home means that a(t) has no factor up to the largest gear N, and while every part of a(t) is below N² each part is prime (proved as for the accelerating wheels). The choices: t (primes), t(t + 2) (twin primes; checked by script: with 30, 100 and 250 gears the quiet ticks below N² are exactly the twin pairs above N, 30, 197 and 814 of them), t(t + 4), t(t + 6), t(2t + 1) (Sophie Germain), t(t + 2)(t + 6), t(t + 2)(t + 6)(t + 8), t² + 1, t² + t + 41 (Euler) and 2^t − 1 (Mersenne candidates). For 2^t − 1 a quiet tick is only a candidate, and the page says so: with 249 wheels and 2,520 ticks there are 462 candidates (359 at prime ticks, 103 at composite ticks, which can never give a prime), the wheels rule out five prime ticks (11, 23, 29, 37, 83), and 13 candidates are Mersenne primes, decided by a Lucas–Lehmer test that the page runs itself (counts recomputed by a separate script). A lamp shows each find, a strip keeps the last ones, and finds beyond the exact range are tested and shown in amber when they are not prime. The statistics now stand above the gears, and the box also counts how many gears whose number is a twin prime are home.

The box under the machine also lists every prime gear with the first tick at which it was home, as gear (tick): on the doubling wheels that tick is the order of 2 (3 (2), 7 (3), 5 (4), 17 (8), 11 (10), …), on the Fibonacci wheels the first Fibonacci number the prime divides (2 (3), 3 (4), 5 (5), 13 (7), 7 (8), …).

The box also counts the twin primes among the ticks: the pairs so far, how many prime ticks in a row have a prime two away, and the longest such row (up to tick 100,000: 1,224 pairs, 2,447 twin primes, longest row 8, from 9,419 to 9,463; recomputed by a separate script). And it lists the quiet ticks; on the accelerating wheels each one is written out as its pair of primes. The pair rule is proved: a tick t is quiet when t(t + 1)/2 has no factor up to the largest gear N, so for t + 1 < N² the two coprime factors (t/2 and t + 1, or t and (t + 1)/2) are both prime; checked for N = 5, 10, 30, 60, 100 and 250.

A box under the machine keeps one record: the tick at which the most prime gears were home while no other gear was home (gear 1, which is always home, left out), with the earlier records and the tick of the next one. A set of prime gears can be home alone only when no product of two of them is a gear, so 2 and 3 drop out as soon as there are three: the records are 2 (gear 2), 33 = 3 · 11, 385 = 5 · 7 · 11, 5,005 = 5 · 7 · 11 · 13, 85,085, 1,616,615, 37,182,145 and 1,078,282,205 = 5 · 7 · 11 · 13 · 17 · 19 · 23 · 29, and eight is the most (recomputed by a separate search over all sets of prime gears; the first five by running through the ticks to 100,000). A second line keeps the same record for the Mersenne-exponent gears alone: with 30 gears 2, 34 = 2 · 17, 455 = 5 · 7 · 13, 7,735 = 5 · 7 · 13 · 17 and 146,965 = 5 · 7 · 13 · 17 · 19 (five is the most); with 100 gears 2, 119 = 7 · 17, 2,261 and 70,091 = 7 · 17 · 19 · 31 (at most six); with 250 gears 2, 254 = 2 · 127 and 10,013 = 17 · 19 · 31 (at most seven). All recomputed by a separate script up to tick 200,000. With 100 gears the records are 2, 106 = 2 · 53, 2,261 = 7 · 17 · 19 and 46,189 = 11 · 13 · 17 · 19; with 250 gears 2, 253 = 11 · 23, 7,337 = 11 · 23 · 29 and 215,441 = 17 · 19 · 23 · 29, and at most 47 prime gears can be home alone (17 and all larger primes up to 241). On the dial with 30 hands the box keeps the ray that holds the most prime hands and no other hand: 5, 11, 17, 23, 29 after 1/6 of a turn, and no other fraction of a turn does better.

The page was then simplified (17 November 2026): every machine is one strategy, the two wheel choices became machines of their own (21, only prime wheels; 22, wheels that fit 2^p − 1), and under every machine the same three results are kept: how many prime gears, Mersenne-exponent gears and twin-prime gears are home now, the most at once with its tick, and the records of the run. The earlier lines of the box (only primes home, first tick home, quiet ticks) are no longer shown; the figures quoted above for them stand as computed.

**Which machine has the best results, and the list cut to eleven** ([best.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/best.py) → [best.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/best.txt), [best2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/best2.py); 250 wheels, 100,000 ticks; most prime wheels / Mersenne-exponent wheels / twin-prime wheels home at once, of 53 / 12 / 33). Back and forth 53 / 12 / 33 (at tick 36 every wheel is back at its start); Fibonacci 26 / 11 / 19; doubling 27 / 10 / 21; the pattern machine set to quadruplets 13 / 9 / 11 and to twin primes 9 / 8 / 8; accelerating 9 / 8 / 8; slipping 11 / 6 / 8; the plain wheels and the square jumps 6 / 6 / 5; the prime clock 5 / 5 / 4; the wheels that fit 4 / 1 / 2; the dial and the Lucas–Lehmer wheels 1 / 1 / 1. On this measure the ten weakest were taken off the page: the dial, the prime clock, the Lucas–Lehmer wheels and seven of the looks of the plain wheels (rings, chains, pistons, drums, rolling gears, rack, arm), which all share the result of machine 1. The prime wheels and the wheels that fit were combined into one machine, the Mersenne finder: prime wheels decide whether the tick p is prime, then the wheels with 2kp + 1 teeth try to divide 2^p − 1, and the Lucas–Lehmer test decides what is left. The measure counts wheels at home; for finding primes it is the other way round, a tick with no wheel home is the find.

## 18. All gear machines run and compared (computed; every figure recomputed by a tick-by-tick simulation)

Each rule of motion of section 17 was run for 100,000 ticks with 30, 100 and 250 gears ([machines.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/machines.py) → [machines.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/machines.txt)). A quiet tick is a tick at which no gear other than gear 1 is home; 9.6 % of all ticks are prime.

| Machine | Quiet ticks that are prime (30 / 100 / 250 gears) | Prime ticks that are quiet (250) | Most prime gears home alone (30 / 250 gears) | First tick prime gear p is home |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wheels, one tooth per tick | 60.6 / 80.9 / 98.4 % | 99.4 % | 5 (tick 85,085) / 3 (7,337) | p |
| Square jumps | 60.6 / 80.9 / 98.4 % | 99.4 % | 4 (17,017) / 3 (7,429) | p |
| Doubling wheels | 31.6 / 41.3 / 51.9 % | 99.9 % | 3 (110) / 6 (6,545) | the order of 2 mod p |
| Fibonacci wheels | 28.0 / 33.8 / 38.1 % | 99.9 % | 4 (70) / 7 (20,482) | the first Fibonacci number p divides |
| Accelerating wheels | 30.3 / 41.5 / 49.4 % | 6.9 % | 6 (7,105) / 6 (46,138) | p − 1 (3 for p = 2) |
| Back and forth | 19.7 / 19.3 / 19.0 % | 15.1 % | 3 (32,869) / 2 (8,773) | 2, 5, 11, 17 for 2, 3, 5, 7; then 36 |
| Prime clock | 9.7 / 9.7 / 9.6 % | 11.8 % | 4 (48,661) / 3 (74,377) | the p-th prime |
| Slipping wheels | 9.4 / 9.4 / 9.0 % | 4.3 % | 6 (2,492) / 8 (51,331) | p + 1 |
| One dial | 18.0 / 36.0 % / no quiet tick | – | – | 60, 40, 24 for 2, 3, 5; then 120 |

- Four machines sieve the primes: the plain wheels and the square jumps alike (a quiet tick has no factor up to the largest gear; the prime ticks that are not quiet are the primes up to 250 themselves), the doubling and the Fibonacci wheels at about half strength, because there a product of two large primes is quiet too.
- The accelerating wheels find pairs. With 250 gears there are 894 quiet ticks below 250² = 62,500, and every one is either t = 2p with p and 2p + 1 prime (440) or a prime t with (t + 1)/2 prime (454): the tick is quiet when t(t + 1)/2 has no factor up to 250 ([check_pairs.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/check_pairs.py)).
- The prime clock and the slipping wheels say nothing about the tick. The back-and-forth machine gives twice the base rate only because its distance is odd exactly at the odd ticks. The dial's quiet ticks are those that share little with 120, a sieve by 2, 3 and 5.
- On the plain wheels more gears make it harder for prime gears to be home alone (5, 4, 3 with 30, 100, 250 gears within 100,000 ticks); on the slipping, Fibonacci and doubling wheels it gets easier (8, 7, 6 with 250 gears).
- The doubling wheels as a sieve for Mersenne numbers: a wheel q can divide 2^p − 1 only when q ≥ 2p + 1, so 250 wheels reach the 30 prime ticks up to 124. They rule out five (11 by 23 and 89, 23 by 47, 29 by 233, 37 by 223, 83 by 167); 11 are Mersenne exponents; 14 pass although 2^p − 1 is not prime.
- The Mersenne-exponent gears come home neither sooner nor later than other prime gears of their size: first tick divided by p, median 0.67 against 0.50 on the doubling wheels (permutation test p = 0.53) and 0.96 against 0.51 on the Fibonacci wheels (p = 0.21). On the other machines the first tick is a fixed rule of p.

The page shows this table in a panel under the machines ([gen_panel.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/gen_panel.py) writes it from [machines.json](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/machines.json)).

**The exponent record with other gears allowed** ([exp_logic.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/exp_logic.py) → [exp_logic.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/exp_logic.txt), 100,000 ticks; the page counts the same way and agrees). The box now counts the Mersenne-exponent gears that are home while only prime gears are home, and in a second line with any gears home. Allowing other primes changes the record only on the doubling wheels (2 → 3 with 100 and 250 gears) and on the accelerating wheels (3 → 4 with 250 gears); on the plain wheels it cannot change, because the smallest tick for a set of exponents is their own product. With any gears allowed the plain wheels reach 6 exponents at tick 46,410 = 2 · 3 · 5 · 7 · 13 · 17 (all 7 of 30 gears at 881,790), the doubling wheels 10 of 12 and the Fibonacci wheels 11 of 12 at tick 27,720, and the back-and-forth machine all 12 at tick 36.

**A strategy that beats more wheels: wheels that fit the question** ([strategy.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/strategy.py) → [strategy.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/strategy.txt); the page counts the same and agrees: 264 of the prime ticks up to 5,000). The pattern machine has a choice of wheel sets. A wheel with a composite number of teeth never decides anything, so with the first N primes as wheels instead of the numbers 1 to N the machine stays exact up to the square of the N-th prime: 12,769 instead of 900 for 30 wheels, 2,505,889 instead of 62,500 for 250, 2.36·10⁹ instead of 2.5·10⁷ for 5,000. For 2^p − 1 a factor must be of the form 2kp + 1, leave remainder 1 or 7 mod 8 and be prime, so the fitting wheels at a prime tick p are those with 2kp + 1 teeth, k = 1 to N; 85 % of them can be skipped unseen. Of the 669 prime ticks up to 5,000, 250 plain wheels rule out 5, the first 250 primes 22, and 250 fitting wheels 264 (39.5 %); with 5,000 wheels the three figures are 52, 160 and 334 (49.9 %). No Mersenne exponent is ruled out. This is the trial-factoring step of the real search; what is left goes to the Lucas–Lehmer test.

## 19. A second backtest of the gear machines: what was new (computed; the Fibonacci bound and the cyclic numbers are known theorems, the trust curve is Buchstab's function)

All machines were run again over every number of gears from 2 to 250 and over wheels up to 5,000 ([bt1.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt1.py) → [bt1.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt1.txt), [bt2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt2.py) → [bt2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt2.txt)). Kept:

- **Dial.** With 157 to 186 hands, at tick 4 (1/30 of a turn), one ray holds 7, 37, 67, 97, 127, 157 and no other hand: six primes alone, the most for any number of hands up to 250. It stops at 187 = 11 · 17.
- **How far a machine can be trusted.** The share of quiet ticks that are real finds depends only on u = ln t / ln N, not on the number of gears N: at u = 2.5 it is 0.72 to 0.75, at u = 3 it is 0.60 to 0.61 for N = 30, 60, 100, 250 (Buchstab's function gives 0.712 and 0.591). For twin pairs at u = 3: 0.34 to 0.37.
- **Fibonacci wheels.** No wheel up to 5,000 needs more than twice its size in ticks to come home, and exactly twice only the wheels 6, 30, 150, 750, 3,750 (6 · 5^j; a known theorem). The fastest are the Fibonacci numbers: wheel 4,181 at tick 19, wheel 2,584 at tick 18.
- **Slipping wheels.** Wheel k repeats after k²/gcd(k, φ(k)) ticks, so the wheels that need the full k rounds are exactly the numbers with gcd(k, φ(k)) = 1: every prime, and 15, 33, 35, 51, 65, 69, 77, … (the orders for which every group is cyclic).
- **Accelerating wheels.** The slowest wheels are the powers of two: wheel 2^a comes home first at tick 2^(a+1) − 1, a Mersenne number (wheel 4 at 7, 16 at 31, 64 at 127, 4,096 at 8,191). Every other wheel up to 5,000 is home within k ticks.

Dropped by the backtest:

- The distance of the back-and-forth machine seemed to drift (2.4 spreads at 2·10⁶); at 10⁸ it is 0.45 spreads, and the mean gap at odd and even places differs by 0.006 ± 0.012 ([bt4.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt4.py) → [bt4.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt4.txt)).
- The 44 Mersenne exponents above 40 against 17 properties the machines look for (twin, p ± 4, p ± 6, 2p ± 1, (p ± 1)/2, 4p ± 1, 6p ± 1, a triplet, remainders mod 4 and 8), compared with the 300 nearest primes and with those of them whose 2^q − 1 has no factor 2kq + 1 for k ≤ 300: no z-score beyond 2.1, and the largest is the known tilt towards p ≡ 1 (mod 4) ([bt3.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt3.py) → [bt3.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt3.txt)).

## 20. One wheel decides: a wheel the number does not fit (that every prime comes home is proved; "no mistake below 2^64" is computed and rests on the published list of base-2 pseudoprimes; that there is never a mistake is open: for the ring it is Agrawal's conjecture with x + 1 in place of x − 1, the same statement when the number of cells is even; for the two cells it is a Frobenius test, which a start of norm 2 settles below 2^64, given the list, and nothing settles above; the chain of wheels and the test with a receipt are literature and their runs are computed; the four machines that find primes before their tick are literature with computed runs, and the rule for the rounds of machine 23 is a heuristic)

Sections 17 to 19 judged a machine by how many prime wheels are home together. A harder demand is that one wheel decides alone: wheel n must be home after n turns exactly when n is prime. The gear rules were put in a contest on that demand ([tour.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour.c), [psp2.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/psp2.c), [summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/summary.py) → [tournament.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tournament.txt)).

**The machines.** Each wheel n carries a few cells that hold remainders after division by n.

- Doubling wheel: one cell, doubled every turn; home when it shows its start again, 2^n ≡ 2 (mod n).
- Fibonacci wheel: two cells (a, b) → (b, a + b) from (0, 1); home when n divides F(n − 1) for n ≡ ±1 (mod 5) and F(n + 1) for n ≡ ±2 (mod 5); multiples of 5 are turned away.
- Perrin's wheel: three cells (a, b, c) → (b, c, a + b) from (3, 0, 2); home when n divides the first cell after n turns.
- Two-cell wheel with step c: cells (a, b) → (a + c·b, a + b) from (1, 0), which is multiplication by 1 + √c; home when the cells read (1, −1), that is (1 + √c)^n ≡ 1 − √c (mod n).
- Pascal's wheel with r cells: a ring with a 1 in cell 0; a turn replaces every cell by itself plus the cell before it. After t turns the ring holds the coefficients of (x + 1)^t mod (n, x^r − 1). Home means a 1 in cell 0, a 1 in cell n mod r and nothing else: (x + 1)^n ≡ x^n + 1 (mod n, x^r − 1).

**Every prime comes home (proved).** For a prime n every binomial coefficient C(n, k) with 0 < k < n is divisible by n, because n divides n! and not k!(n − k)!. So (x + 1)^n ≡ x^n + 1 (mod n) before the ring is folded, and folding keeps it. The same binomials give (1 + √c)^n ≡ 1 + c^((n − 1)/2) √c (mod n), and c^((n − 1)/2) ≡ −1 when c is not a square mod the prime n (Euler's criterion). The other three wheels are the classical statements 2^n ≡ 2, n | F(n − (5/n)) and n | P(n) for primes. So a wheel that is not home proves that n is not prime; the only possible mistake is a number that is not prime and comes home.

**Two lemmas that make the search finite (proved).**

1. Sum of the cells. A turn doubles the sum of the cells, so after n turns the sum is 2^n; the home position has sum 2. A number that comes home on any Pascal ring therefore satisfies 2^n ≡ 2 (mod n): it fools the doubling wheel first. The odd numbers that do are known below 2^64: 118,968,378 of them (Feitsma and Galway; the 20,516 smallest agree with our own sieve, [list_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/list_check.py) → [list_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/list_check.txt)).
2. Folding. If d divides r, folding the ring of r cells onto d cells (cell k onto cell k mod d) turns one home position into the other. A mistake on r cells is a mistake on every ring of d cells, d | r.

3. The sign. Agrawal's congruence is (x − 1)^n ≡ x^n − 1 (mod n, x^r − 1); Pascal's wheel is (x + 1)^n ≡ x^n + 1. For odd n they are the same statement on a ring with an even number of cells: replace x by −x. On an odd number of cells r they differ, and the link is: n is home with x − 1 on 2r cells exactly when it is home with x − 1 on r cells and with x + 1 on r cells. Proof: x^(2r) − 1 = (x^r − 1)(x^r + 1), and the two factors differ by 2, a unit mod n; so the ring of 2r cells is the product of the rings mod x^r − 1 and mod x^r + 1, and x → −x carries the second onto the first and x − 1 onto −(x + 1). Checked on every odd number below 10^6 that is not prime, for r = 3, 5, 7, 9, 11 ([agrawal_sign.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/agrawal_sign.py) → [agrawal_sign.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/agrawal_sign.txt)). Lemma 1 has no counterpart for x − 1 on an odd ring, where the sum of the cells is 0 after the first turn: of the 13 numbers below 10^6 that are home with x − 1 on 5 cells, 9 are not base-2 pseudoprimes. So the list reaches Pascal's form on every ring, and Agrawal's form on even rings only.
4. Folding by symmetry. Let r be prime and H a group of residues mod r. Renumbering the cells k → hk (x → x^h) carries the ring onto itself and a home position onto a home position, so a number that is home satisfies (x^h + 1)^n ≡ x^(hn) + 1 for every h. Multiply over h in H: u^n ≡ u(x^n) for u = ∏ (x^h + 1). This u does not change under H, so apart from cell 0 it has one value on each of the e = (r − 1)/|H| classes of H, and so has each of its powers: the test runs on a wheel of e cells (the sums of x^k over a class, taken mod 1 + x + … + x^(r−1)). A mistake on r cells is a mistake on every folded wheel. Exact facts for every prime r below 100 and every prime power e dividing (r − 1)/2, 49 folds ([fold_units.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_units.py) → [fold_units.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_units.txt)): mod 1 + x + … + x^(r−1) the product of all r − 1 factors x^h + 1 is 1, so u is a unit; and u itself is 1, so that the fold says nothing, when 2 lies in H (then ∏ (x^(2h) − 1) = ∏ (x^h − 1), and x^h + 1 is the quotient of the factors), and for these r in no other case. That happens for (r, e) = (17, 2), (31, 3), (41, 2), (43, 3), (73, 2), (73, 4), (89, 2), (89, 4), (97, 2).

**The contest (computed).** All odd numbers below 10^8 that are not prime, without any shortcut (44,238,545 numbers):

| Wheel | Cells | First mistake | Mistakes below 10^8 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Doubling | 1 | 341 | 2,057 |
| Fibonacci | 2 | 323 | 1,460 |
| Perrin | 3 | 271,441 | 5 |
| Pascal, 5 cells | 5 | 219,781 | 90 |

Wheels with a feedback, x^d = c_(d−1) x^(d−1) + … + c_0 with coefficients from −3 to 3 and the same test (x + 1)^n ≡ x^n + 1, were joined with the doubling wheel and run on the list below 10^10: all 6 with one cell and all 42 with two cells make mistakes, and 7 of the 294 with three cells make none there ([tournamentA.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tournamentA.py) → [tournamentA_1e10.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tournamentA_1e10.txt)). A fixed wheel fits some numbers, so these are expected to fail further on; they were dropped.

No fixed wheel is free of mistakes, and more cells alone do not help: on the list below 10^10 the rings of 2 to 40 cells make 42,120 mistakes (number and ring counted together; [ringcensus.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ringcensus.py) → [ringcensus_1e10.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ringcensus_1e10.txt)).

**Fit.** Say that n fits a ring of r cells when n² ≡ 1 (mod r). The condition is in the literature: Popovych (2009, Cryptology ePrint 2009/008) restated Agrawal's conjecture as "if (x − 1)^n ≡ x^n − 1 and (x + 2)^n ≡ x^n + 2 (mod n, x^r − 1), then n is prime or n² ≡ 1 (mod r)" (quoted from the Wikipedia article on Agrawal's conjecture; of the paper itself we read only the abstract). Our runs use one offset, x + 1, where he uses two. Of those 42,120 mistakes, 0 are numbers that do not fit their ring. On 5 cells the 628 mistakes below 10^10 split as 611 with n ≡ 1 and 17 with n ≡ −1 (mod 5), none with n ≡ ±2. Why ±1 is special (a heuristic, not a proof): for a prime factor p of n and an r-th root of unity ζ over the field with p elements, home means (ζ + 1)^n = ζ^n + 1. For n ≡ 1 this reads (ζ + 1)^(n − 1) = 1, and for n ≡ −1, because ζ^(−1) + 1 = (ζ + 1)/ζ, it reads (ζ + 1)^(n − 1) = ζ^(−1). Both are conditions on the order of one element, of the kind that Carmichael numbers meet. For another class ζ^n + 1 is not ζ + 1 times a root of unity, and the equation asks for a coincidence between two unrelated elements, unless n ≡ p^j (mod r) for every prime factor, the case in which Lenstra and Pomerance expect mistakes among very large numbers. A simpler guess, that every prime factor p of a mistake has a power p^j ≡ n (mod r), was tested and fails for 14 of the 628 mistakes on 5 cells: a factor p ≡ 1 with n ≡ −1 ([factor_classes.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/factor_classes.py) → [factor_classes.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/factor_classes.txt)).

**The two machines that were kept.** Both give n a wheel it does not fit.

Pascal's wheel. For each n take the smallest r that has no factor in common with n and that n does not fit (r is 5 or more, since every number fits 2, 3 and 4 cells), and run Pascal's wheel on r cells. Results:

- All 118,968,378 candidates below 2^64: 0 came home. Ring sizes: 5 cells 25.9 %, 7 cells 26.2 %, 9 cells 14.9 %, 11 cells 15.4 %, 13 cells 8.9 %, 16 cells 2.0 %, 17 cells 3.9 %, 19 cells 1.8 %, 23 cells 0.7 %; the largest ring needed had 47 cells.
- Separately, every candidate was put on each ring of 5, 7, 9, 11, 13 and 16 cells that it does not fit (5 cells: 30,822,826 tested; 7 cells: 46,429,065 tested; 9 cells: 46,932,566 tested; 11 cells: 67,934,752 tested; 13 cells: 76,118,129 tested; 16 cells: 41,018,314 tested): none came home. By lemma 2 a mistake on a ring r that n does not fit shows on one of the prime-power parts of r that n does not fit either, and the parts 2, 3, 4 and 8 fit every number. So this settles every ring whose other prime-power parts are among 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 16; that includes every r up to 42.
- A second program, written separately with plain 128-bit remainders ([ring2.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring2.c)), repeated the ring of 5 cells (30,822,826 tested, 0 came home) and the ring of 7 cells (46,429,065 tested, 0 came home).
- More rings with a faster program ([ring3.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring3.c); sums of products are kept unreduced and reduced once per cell). Each candidate first runs on a ring of half the size: for an odd r the cells k and −k taken together, where ((x + 1)²/x)^n ≡ (x^n + 1)²/x^n must hold, and for 32 cells the ring of 16 cells with a sign (x^16 = −1). Only what passes goes to the full ring. 17 cells: 85,355,413 tested; 19 cells: 90,377,602 tested; 23 cells: 95,590,960 tested; 25 cells, the candidates that fit 5 cells and not 25 (the others are covered by lemma 2): 54,189,558 tested; 27 cells, the candidates that fit 9 cells and not 27 (the others are covered by lemma 2): 35,782,571 tested; 32 cells, the candidates that fit 16 cells and not 32 (the others are covered by lemma 2): 27,276,052 tested. 0 passed the first step and 0 came home. The program gives the known mistakes of numbers that fit (1,468 on 20 rings below 10^10) and the same cells as plain Python on 409 rings with numbers up to 2^64 ([ring3_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring3_check.py) → [ring3_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring3_check.txt)). The second program [ring2.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring2.c) repeated these rings on a sample of 29,743 lines of the list: nothing came home, and for the prime rings it tested the same numbers ([ring2_sample.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring2_sample.txt)).
- Folded wheels first ([fold.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold.c)). For a prime r each candidate was put on the smallest folded wheel of lemma 4 that says something and for which n is not in H; only what passes goes to the full ring (a class with no such fold goes to the half-size ring first). 29 cells: 99,210,915 tested (folded onto 2 cells: 54,394,433, folded onto 7 cells: 44,816,482); 31 cells: 100,173,453 tested (folded onto 5 cells: 86,174,123, no fold applies and the half-size ring was used: 13,999,330); 37 cells: 102,117,897 tested (folded onto 2 cells: 54,930,111, folded onto 3 cells: 35,594,590, folded onto 9 cells: 11,593,196); 41 cells: 103,530,056 tested (folded onto 4 cells: 81,525,451, folded onto 5 cells: 22,004,605). Candidates that passed a folded wheel: 1 (2,465, on the fold of 37 cells onto 2, where it moves the cells with order 2; it is not home on the full ring; [fold37_show_1.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold37_show_1.out)); came home: 0. The rings of 5, 7, 11, 13 cells were run again this way, with the same numbers of candidates as before and no pass: a second method for those rings. Folding loses nothing: of the 1,044 pairs (mistake below 10^10 of a number that fits, fold) every one passes its fold; the folded wheels agree with plain Python on the full ring in 1,978 cases with numbers up to 2^64; and the whole chain, run on numbers that fit, returns exactly the 944 known mistakes on 12 rings ([fold_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_check.py) → [fold_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_check.txt)).
- Together: below 2^64 no number comes home on a ring of up to 42 cells that it does not fit (the first ring not run has 43 cells). By lemma 3 the same holds for Agrawal's own congruence on every even number of cells up to 62.
- Without lemma 1 and without the list: every odd number below 2 · 10^8 that is not prime (88,921,063 numbers) was put on its ring directly: 0 came home.
- Even numbers: the test box turns an even n above 2 away. On the page's range (2 to 5,000) no even number comes home, and the three even numbers below 3 · 10^6 with 2^n ≡ 2 (mod n) do not either ([pascal_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/pascal_check.py) → [pascal_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/pascal_check.txt)).

**Folded wheels alone (computed).** Without the doubling wheel and without the list, the folded wheels were put on every odd number below 10^7 that is not prime and does not fit the ring; each number went to the fold chosen as above ([folded_alone.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/folded_alone.py) → [folded_alone_1e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/folded_alone_1e7.txt)).

| Ring | Cells after folding | Order of n on the fold | Tested | Came home | First mistakes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 5 | 2 | 2 | 1,667,559 | 192 | 323, 377, 3827 |
| 7 | 3 | 3 | 2,413,995 | 0 | none |
| 11 | 5 | 5 | 3,104,646 | 0 | none |
| 13 | 2 | 2 | 1,975,273 | 304 | 119, 1189, 1763 |
| 13 | 3 | 3 | 1,317,048 | 0 | none |
| 17 | 4 | 2 or 4 | 3,031,046 | 22 | 399, 3827, 40299 |
| 17 | 8 | 2 | 505,164 | 0 | none |
| 19 | 3 | 3 | 2,714,710 | 0 | none |
| 19 | 9 | 3 | 904,956 | 0 | none |

All 518 mistakes are numbers whose square lies in H (518 of 518): n moves the cells of the folded wheel round with order 2. No number that moves the cells with order 3 or more came home: nothing on the folds where every tested number has such an order (10,455,355 tests on the folds onto 3, 5, 9 cells), and nothing of that kind on the others. This is the fit rule one level down: n² ≡ 1 (mod r) says that n acts on the whole ring with order at most 2. The first mistakes of the fold of 5 cells, 323 and 377, are the first mistakes of the Fibonacci wheel, for the reason given next.

**Pascal's wheel on 5 cells contains the Fibonacci wheel (proved).** For r = 5 and H = {1, 4} the fold is u = (x + 1)(x⁴ + 1) = 2 + x + x⁴, and x + x⁴ is 2 cos 72° = φ − 1 for the golden ratio φ, so u = φ + 1 = φ². A number without the factor 5 that is home on 5 cells therefore satisfies φ^(2n) ≡ φ² for n ≡ ±1 and φ^(2n) ≡ φ^(−2) for n ≡ ±2 (mod 5), in the integers extended by φ, mod n. With √5 F(k) = φ^k − (−1/φ)^k and k = n ∓ 1, which is even, √5 F(k) φ^k = φ^(2k) − 1 ≡ 0; φ is a unit and 5 has no factor in common with n, so n divides F(n − 1), respectively F(n + 1). Every mistake of the ring of 5 cells is a mistake of the Fibonacci wheel, and by lemma 1 of the doubling wheel: all 628 below 10^10 are ([fold_units.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_units.txt)). For n ≡ ±2 (mod 5) such a number is what the prize of 620 dollars asks for: a base-2 pseudoprime that divides F(n + 1). So on the numbers that do not fit it, the ring of 5 cells can only err if the prize problem has a counterexample, and the run of that ring above tested the same 30,822,826 numbers as the run of the pair.

**A three-cell wheel (that every prime comes home is proved; the rest is computed).** Folding gives a machine that is not a ring: a cubic cousin of the two-cell wheel. Take a prime r ≡ 1 (mod 3) for which 2 is not a cube mod r (7, 13, 19, 37, 61, 67, …) and let H be the cubes mod r. The folded wheel has three cells, one for each class of H. A turn multiplies by u, a fixed rule that replaces each cell by a sum of the three; for r = 7 it is multiplication by 2 + 2 cos(2π/7), a root of u³ − 5u² + 6u − 1. For n take the first such r that does not divide n and for which n is not a cube mod r; then n is home when after n turns the three cells show what they showed after the first turn, moved round by one or two places. So n acts with order 3: by the rule above this is a wheel that n does not fit. A number divisible by a wheel size tried on the way is turned away, and a cube has no wheel. Results ([three_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_check.py) → [three_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_check.txt)): alone, on every odd number below 10^8 that is not prime (44,238,545 numbers): 0 came home; 8,521,121 were turned away because a wheel size divides them, and 143, the cubes, have no wheel; on all 118,968,378 candidates below 2^64, that is together with the doubling wheel: 0 came home (13,809,649 turned away, 0 with no wheel); the largest wheel size used was 211. All 25,999 primes tried came home, and the program agrees with plain Python on the full ring for 1,896 numbers. In the terms of the literature this is a Frobenius test with a cyclic cubic polynomial.

An attempt to break it (computed; not a proof). A wheel with a unit is usually fooled by a product of primes on which the wheel has a short period: 323 = 17 · 19 fools the golden wheel that way. For a prime p that is not ±1 (mod 7) the three cells form one field mod p, and a number n with the factor p can only be home if the period d of u mod p divides n − p or n − p² (the first when n ≡ ±p, the second when n ≡ ±p² mod 7). For all 6,401 such primes below 10^5 the period is larger than the prime (the smallest periods: 13 for p = 3, 31 for p = 5, 127 for p = 19). Then no product pq of two of them, p < q, can be home: the condition at q asks that its period divides q − p, which is smaller than q. Among the 27 primes with a period below 20,000 no product of two or three meets the condition at every factor ([three_orders.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_orders.py) → [three_orders.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_orders.txt)). Primes with a period d must divide the norm of u^d − 1; PARI factors these norms for every d up to 70 and finds 2 such primes, 3 and 5 ([three_hunt.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_hunt.py) → [three_hunt.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_hunt.txt)). So the usual construction finds nothing here. A square p² is home mod p for every such prime, so it needs a separate look: the squares of the 216,815 odd primes below 3 · 10^6 were tried and 0 came home ([three_squares.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_squares.txt)). Prime factors that are ±1 (mod 7) were not treated this way; for them only the runs above speak.

The two-cell wheel. Its step is the smallest odd prime c that is not a square mod n in the sense of the Jacobi symbol, (c/n) = −1; n "fits" a step that is a square. Even numbers, numbers divisible by a step tried on the way, and numbers with no step up to 199 (a square has none) are turned away. Results:

- Alone, on every odd number below 2 · 10^10 that is not prime (9,117,793,284 numbers): 0 came home.
- On all 118,968,378 candidates below 2^64, that is together with the doubling wheel: 0 came home; 2 had no step (the squares of 1093 and 3511); the largest step needed was 131.
- Independent check in plain Python with sympy's Jacobi symbol: [tour_check2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check2.py) → [tour_check2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check2.txt).


A check of the list and of the code against a known result: the doubling wheel together with the Fibonacci wheel for n ≡ ±2 (mod 5), the pair for which a prize is offered, on 30,822,826 candidates: 0 came home.

Above 2^64 there is no list. As a spot check, numbers that fool the doubling wheel were built on purpose: 4,000 Carmichael numbers (6k + 1)(12k + 1)(18k + 1) and 6,000 numbers p·(k(p − 1) + 1), k = 2 to 6, all above 2^64 and up to 6.0 · 10^24. On Pascal's wheel 0 and 0 came home, on the two-cell wheel 0 and 0 ([beyond64.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/beyond64.py) → [beyond64.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/beyond64.txt)).

In the literature the two-cell wheel with this choice of step is Khashin's form of the Frobenius test (2013); no number that fools it is known, and Khashin shows that one below 2^60 would need a prime factor below 19 or a step above 128. Agrawal's conjecture says: if (x − 1)^n ≡ x^n − 1 (mod n, x^r − 1), then n is prime or n² ≡ 1 (mod r). Pascal's wheel is the same statement with x + 1, and by lemma 3 the two agree when the number of cells is even. The checks of Agrawal's form that we found reported: r < 100 with n < 10^10, r = 5 with n < 10^11, and a distributed search over 10^10 < n < 10^17. The runs above reach 2^64 ≈ 1.8 · 10^19 for Pascal's form on every ring up to 42 cells, and with it for Agrawal's form on every even number of cells up to 62. For Agrawal's form on an odd number of cells they say nothing new, because a number that fools it need not be on the list. All of this is computation, not proof, and it rests on the completeness of the published list.

**Prime powers (proved, then computed).** Write (x + 1)^p = x^p + 1 + p h(x). Then (x + 1)^(p²) = (x^p + 1 + p h(x))^p ≡ (x^p + 1)^p ≡ x^(p²) + 1 + p h(x^p) (mod p²). So p² comes home on r cells exactly when h(x^p), and hence h(x) (x → x^p only permutes the cells, as p does not divide r), vanishes mod (p, x^r − 1). Now h(1) = (2^p − 2)/p, which is divisible by p only for a Wieferich prime, and x(x + 1)(x² + x + 1) divides h as a polynomial. Any higher power needs a Wieferich prime too: if p^k, k ≥ 2, comes home, lemma 1 gives 2^(p^k − 1) ≡ 1 (mod p²); the order of 2 mod p² divides p(p − 1) and p^k − 1, whose greatest common divisor is p − 1, so 2^(p − 1) ≡ 1 (mod p²). For the two known Wieferich primes 1093 and 3511 the square fools the rings of 2, 3 and 4 cells, which every number fits, and no other ring up to 200 cells ([lemma_pp2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/lemma_pp2.py) → [lemma_pp2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/lemma_pp2.txt)).

**Mersenne numbers (proved).** For n = 2^p − 1 with p an odd prime, the two-cell wheel is the Lucas–Lehmer test. Here n ≡ 1 (mod 3) and n ≡ 3 (mod 4), so (3/n) = −1 and the step is 3. Home means (1 + √3)^n ≡ 1 − √3, so (1 + √3)^(n+1) ≡ (1 − √3)(1 + √3) = −2. Now (1 + √3)² = 2(2 + √3) and n + 1 = 2^p, so (1 + √3)^(n+1) = 2^(2^(p−1)) (2 + √3)^(2^(p−1)), and 2^(2^(p−1)) ≡ 2 (mod n) because 2^p ≡ 1 (mod n) and 2^(p−1) ≡ 1 (mod p). So a wheel that is home gives (2 + √3)^(2^(p−1)) ≡ −1 (mod n). That is the Lucas–Lehmer condition, and it proves that n is prime: for a prime factor q of n the element 2 + √3 then has order exactly 2^p among fewer than q² elements, so q² > 2^p > n. On Mersenne numbers machine 11 cannot make a mistake. Check: for the 429 odd primes p below 3,000 the wheel and the Lucas–Lehmer test agree, always with step 3; the wheel is home for p = 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 19, 31, 61, 89, 107, 127, 521, 607, 1279, 2203, 2281 ([duo_mersenne.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/duo_mersenne.py) → [duo_mersenne.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/duo_mersenne.txt)). Pascal's wheel agrees with Lucas–Lehmer for the 124 exponents below 700, but there is no such proof for it.

**Cost.** A turn count of n is reached in about log2 n doublings of the turn count, each a product of two rings: about r² log2 n multiplications mod n. The page tests 2^4423 − 1 (1,332 digits) in about a second.

**Proved or fast (computed; the two theorems are literature).** Timings are one run in Python with gmpy2 on 2^4423 − 1 (1,332 digits), from [duo_mersenne.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/duo_mersenne.txt); on 2^19937 − 1 (6,002 digits) Lucas–Lehmer, the two-cell wheel, the two cells with check turns, Pascal's wheel and the three-cell wheel took 0.30, 1.24, 14.21, 13.91 and 16.11 seconds. The programs are plain and not tuned; only Lucas–Lehmer and Pascal's wheel use the cheap remainder of a Mersenne number.

| Wheel | Cells | For which n | Proved | Cost for 2^4423 − 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wilson's wheel: one cell, multiplied by 1, 2, 3, … turn after turn; −1 after n − 1 turns | 1 | every n | yes | n − 1 turns, and none can be skipped |
| The proved ring test of Agrawal, Kayal and Saxena | more than 19,562,929 | every n | yes | about 19,562,929 offsets on that ring |
| Lucas's wheel: one cell, multiplied by a every turn; home after n − 1 turns and at no turn (n − 1)/q, q a prime factor of n − 1 | 1 | every n whose n − 1 can be factored | yes | cannot start: the prime factors of n − 1 = 2(2^4422 − 1) are needed |
| Two cells with check turns: the two-cell wheel from the start (a, 1), home after n turns and the round trip of n + 1 turns not finished at any turn (n + 1)/q | 2 | every n whose n + 1 can be factored, so every 2^p − 1 | yes | 0.42 s (step 3, start 1, one check turn) |
| Lucas–Lehmer | 1 | 2^p − 1 | yes | 0.02 s |
| Curve wheel (Goldwasser and Kilian): the points of a curve y² = x³ + ax + b mod n form a wheel whose number of positions m changes with a and b; one is picked whose m has a large prime factor q, and the wheel must be home after m turns and not after m/q | 1 point | every n | yes | machine 16 counts the positions one by one and stops at 3,000,000; programs that find m in a cleverer way (Atkin and Morain) reach far beyond: the largest proof we found reported has 109,297 digits |
| Two-cell wheel | 2 | every n (a square has no step) | only for 2^p − 1 | 0.06 s |
| Three-cell wheel | 3 | every n (a cube has no wheel) | no | 0.74 s (folded from 13 cells) |
| Pascal's wheel | 5 and more | every n | no | 0.33 s on 5 cells |

Wilson's wheel gave no wrong answer for n up to 20,000, Lucas's wheel none for n up to 200,000 (never undecided; largest step 69, for n = 110,881) and it proves 3 · 2^2208 + 1 (666 digits) with step 11; the two cells with check turns gave none for n up to 100,000 (never undecided; largest start 31, for n = 60,539) and prove 2^521 − 1 and 2^4423 − 1 with step 3 and start 1; the curve wheel gave none for n up to 5,000 and proved all 669 primes there ([curve_wheel.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/curve_wheel.py) → [curve_wheel.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/curve_wheel.txt)); and the proved ring test none for n up to 300 (its largest machine there: 103 cells and 79 offsets, for n = 227) ([proved_wheels.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/proved_wheels.py) → [proved_wheels.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/proved_wheels.txt)). The wheel that is proved for every n with one cell is slow, the proved ring test is far too large, and the fast proofs by one wheel need something special: the form 2^p − 1, or the factors of n − 1 or n + 1. Elliptic curves remove that need for every n, at the price of a long search for a suitable curve and a chain of proofs. One small wheel with few turns, for every n and with a proof, is what is missing.

**A chain of wheels (the three theorems are literature; the runs are computed).** Machines 14, 15 and 16 are one idea with three sources of wheel sizes, and together they give a machine that is proved, fast, and has worked for every number tried. Let n be the number and f, q primes.

1. One cell (Pocklington). Suppose a^(n−1) ≡ 1 (mod n) and gcd(a^((n−1)/f) − 1, n) = 1 for every prime f of a set, and let F be the product of the full powers of these f in n − 1. For a prime p dividing n, the order of a mod p divides n − 1 and not (n − 1)/f, so it contains the full power of f; it divides p − 1. Hence p ≡ 1 (mod F). If F² > n, every prime factor of n exceeds √n, and n is prime.
2. Two cells (the n + 1 partner; Morrison). Let c be coprime to n, u = a + √c with a² − c coprime to n, and β = u²/(a² − c), which is u divided by its conjugate and has norm 1. Suppose β^(n+1) = 1 and β^((n+1)/f) − 1 has a norm coprime to n for every prime f of a set, with F the product of their full powers in n + 1. Mod a prime p dividing n the two cells are either two copies of the remainders mod p (c is a square mod p; orders divide p − 1) or the field of p² elements, where the elements of norm 1 form a group of order p + 1. As before the order of β contains F, so F divides p − 1 or p + 1 and p ≥ F − 1. If (F − 1)² > n, n is prime.
3. A curve (Goldwasser and Kilian). Let gcd(n, 6) = 1, let E be y² = x³ + ax + b with gcd(4a³ + 27b², n) = 1, P a point of E mod n, and m = c · q with q > (n^(1/4) + 1)². Add points by the chord-and-tangent rule, where every division is by a number coprime to n. If m · P is the point at infinity and c · P is not, n is prime. Proof: mod a prime p dividing n every step of the same computation is a valid step on E over the remainders mod p, so the order of P there divides m and not c; q is prime, so q divides that order. The curve has at most (√p + 1)² points (Hasse), so (√p + 1)² ≥ q > (n^(1/4) + 1)² and p > √n.

In each case n is prime if the large prime q (or f) is, and that number is smaller: a chain, ended by trial division below 10^10.

Where the curve sizes come from (Atkin and Morain). Machine 16 counts the points of a curve, which takes about n steps. For a negative discriminant D with 4n = t² + |D| s² the curves with complex multiplication by D have n + 1 − t and n + 1 + t points (for D = −4 also n + 1 ± 2s, for D = −3 also n + 1 ± (t ± 3s)/2); t and s follow from a square root of D mod n by Cornacchia's algorithm. The j-invariants of these curves are the roots mod n of the class polynomial of D. We use the 97 fundamental discriminants with class number at most 4 ([classpoly_gen.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/classpoly_gen.py) with PARI → [classpoly.json](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/classpoly.json); [classpoly_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/classpoly_check.py) recomputes every polynomial from the j-function with mpmath: 97 checked, 0 differ). From a root j the curve is y² = x³ − 3kx + 2k with k = j/(j − 1728); for a random x0 and z = x0³ − 3k x0 + 2k the curve y² = x³ − 3k z² x + 2k z³ contains the point (z x0, z²), so no square root is needed, and z decides which of the two sizes it has. For D = −3 and −4 the curve is y² = x³ + b or y² = x³ + ax through a random point.

The search ([chain_wheel.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_wheel.py); the page's version is the same in JavaScript). (i) n must pass the doubling wheel (the strong test to base 2); the page also asks the wheel of machine 19 first, so that a composite which fools the doubling wheel is turned away at once and not after a failed search. (ii) List every size on offer: n − 1, n + 1 and the curve sizes. Divide out the primes below 10^5; keep a size when what remains is a probable prime q that is large enough. (iii) Take the candidates with the smallest q first; prove q by the same procedure, remembering the numbers that failed; then find a, c or the curve and the point, and run the check of the theorem. (iv) If nothing works, split the remaining parts with Pollard's rho (20,000 steps) and try again. The result is a list of steps. A separate program, [chain_verify.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_verify.py), which shares no code with the search, checks the conditions of the three theorems on every step.

Random primes, 30 of each size up to 60 digits and 20 above ([chain_test_big.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_test_big.py) → [chain_test_big.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_test_big.txt), [chain_test_big2.log](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_test_big2.log)); the checker accepted every chain that was built, and PARI's isprime, which is a proof, agreed on every prime up to 100 digits. Times are for plain Python.

| Digits | n − 1, n + 1 and 97 curve families | 97 curve families only | n − 1, n + 1 and 9 curve families | n − 1 and n + 1 only | Median steps (first column) | Median time, s |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 15 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 29 of 30 | 1 | 0.01 |
| 20 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 28 of 30 | 2 | 0.03 |
| 30 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 22 of 30 | 2 | 0.05 |
| 40 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 24 of 30 | 3 | 0.07 |
| 60 | 30 of 30 | 30 of 30 | 29 of 30 | 8 of 30 | 5 | 0.16 |
| 80 | 20 of 20 | not run | 20 of 20 | not run | 7 | 0.28 |
| 100 | 20 of 20 | not run | 18 of 20 | not run | 9 | 0.47 |
| 150 | 20 of 20 | not run | 10 of 20 (4 stopped after 120 s) | not run | 16 | 2.04 |
| 200 | 18 of 20 (1 stopped after 120 s) | not run | 7 of 20 (1 stopped after 120 s) | not run | 22 | 6.60 |
| 300 | 15 of 20 (2 stopped after 120 s) | not run | not run | not run | 33 | 27.68 |

On every number from 2 to 100,000, with trial division allowed only below 50, the chain proved all 9,592 primes and no other number; with curves only it proved 9,591 and found no chain for 1 prime (73127) ([chain_test.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_test.py) → [chain_test_small.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_test_small.txt)). The page's code, run under node, proved all 160 random primes of 20 to 150 digits given to it (median 906 ms at 100 digits, 2601 ms at 150) and all primes up to 100,000, and the Python checker accepted every one of its chains ([chain_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_js_check.py) → [chain_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_js_check.txt); the primes are in the files primes_N.txt).

Numbers that are not prime ([chain_adversary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_adversary.py) → [chain_adversary.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_adversary.txt)): all 358 strong pseudoprimes to base 2 below 50,000,000 enter the search, and for every one a turn failed that every prime makes or a factor appeared; of 80 composites p(2p − 1) of 20 to 60 digits that pass the doubling wheel none got a chain; of 2,928 tampered certificates (one number changed, or the top number replaced by a composite) the checker accepted 68, all for a number that is prime (a changed base can still be a valid base).

What this is not. It is not new: it is elliptic curve primality proving, with few curve families and plain arithmetic. It is not one wheel but a chain, and finding the chain is a search for which no bound is proved; with these 97 families the table shows where it starts to fail. The page stops at 150 digits for the curve steps. The method itself has no such limit: the largest proof of this kind that we found reported has 109,297 digits.

**A test with a receipt (the halving protocol and its weakness are literature; the runs are computed).** For a Mersenne number the Lucas–Lehmer test cannot be beaten by any wheel of this section; what can be saved is the second run that a result needs before it is trusted. The statement to check is y = x^(2^T) in a group: T squarings.

The protocol (Pietrzak, 2018). If T is odd, replace y by y² and T by T + 1. The tester sends the middle value μ = x^(2^(T/2)). Then y = μ^(2^(T/2)), and for every number r: (x^r · μ)^(2^(T/2)) = μ^r · y, which is again a statement of the same kind with half the turns. The number r is taken from a hash of x, y, μ and T (64 bits of SHA-256 here), so the tester cannot choose it. After k halvings the checker makes the remaining T/2^k squarings itself; in all it makes T/2^k squarings and 2k powers with exponents of 64 bits. The tester needs no second pass. With h_1, …, h_k the lengths of the halves, the i-th middle value is f(i − 1, h_i), where f(0, m) = x^(2^m) and f(j, m) = f(j − 1, m)^(r_j) · f(j − 1, m + h_j); so it keeps the residues at the 2^k − 1 positions h_i + (a sum of some of h_1, …, h_(i−1)) and builds the receipt from them.

Two groups ([receipt.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt.py)). One cell: x = 3 mod n = 2^p − 1 and T = p; for a prime n, 3^(n+1) = 3^(2^p) ≡ 9, so y = 9 is a probable-prime test (not a proof). Two cells: x = 2 + √3, which has norm 1, and T = p − 2; squaring is (a, b) → (2a² − 1, 2ab), because a² − 3b² = 1. Twice the first cell is x^(2^i) + x^(−2^i), the Lucas–Lehmer sequence 4, 14, 194, …, so the first cell is 0 after p − 2 squarings exactly when 2^p − 1 is prime (the Lucas–Lehmer theorem, p odd). This is machine 11's wheel with step 3 again, and its verdict is a proof.

Agreement ([receipt_test.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_test.py) → [receipt_test.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_test.txt)): for all 668 odd primes p up to 5,000 both forms give the verdict of the plain Lucas–Lehmer test, and every honest receipt is accepted; the exponents found are the 19 odd Mersenne exponents below 5,000. For no composite exponent below 2,000 does 2^n − 1 pass the one-cell test.

Cost ([receipt_timing.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_timing.py) → [receipt_timing.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_timing.txt); Python with gmpy2, the smaller time of two runs on a shared machine, and for the test the smallest of its four runs; for each p the receipt of 6 or 8 numbers that gave the faster check). The counts of squarings are exact; the times are one machine's.

| p | Form | Halvings | Turns of the test | Test, s | Receipt, s | Check: squarings and small powers | Check, s | Test / check |
