| 9,689 | one cell | 6 | 9,689 | 0.05 | 0.04 | 152 and 12 | 0.007 | 7 |
| 9,689 | two cells | 6 | 9,687 | 0.14 | 0.12 | 152 and 12 | 0.023 | 6 |
| 21,701 | one cell | 6 | 21,701 | 0.33 | 0.12 | 340 and 12 | 0.025 | 13 |
| 21,701 | two cells | 6 | 21,699 | 1.02 | 0.40 | 340 and 12 | 0.087 | 12 |
| 44,497 | one cell | 8 | 44,497 | 1.75 | 1.15 | 174 and 16 | 0.076 | 23 |
| 44,497 | two cells | 8 | 44,495 | 5.29 | 4.08 | 174 and 16 | 0.261 | 20 |
| 86,243 | one cell | 8 | 86,243 | 8.73 | 2.82 | 337 and 16 | 0.208 | 42 |
| 86,243 | two cells | 8 | 86,241 | 25.88 | 10.30 | 337 and 16 | 0.688 | 38 |
| 110,503 | one cell | 8 | 110,503 | 15.63 | 4.07 | 432 and 16 | 0.311 | 50 |
| 110,503 | two cells | 8 | 110,501 | 52.07 | 16.84 | 432 and 16 | 1.160 | 45 |

For small p the 2k small powers cost more than the turns that are saved, so the gain grows with p. The project that searches for Mersenne primes reports that its receipts are checked with less than 0.5% of the work of a test (literature, mersenne.org); it uses the one-cell form.

Tampering, p = 9,689, receipts of 6 numbers, each form: a wrong result was accepted in 0 of 51 tries (the true result plus 1, and 50 random values), a changed receipt number in 0 of 12, and after a single bit was flipped at a random turn of the test the receipt was accepted in 0 of 100 runs (the result was wrong in all of them).

A forged receipt (the weakness is literature: Hoffmann, Hubáček, Kamath and Pietrzak, 2023, who state it for Proth numbers and repair it; the runs are ours). Let ε be an element with ε^d = 1 for a small d, and suppose the tester knows the true y and μ. It reports y·ε and sends μ·δ. The check of the first halving, (x^r · μδ)^(2^h) = (μδ)^r · yε with h = T/2, holds exactly when δ^(2^h − r) = ε. Taking δ = ε^t, this is t·(2^h − r) ≡ 1 (mod d), where r depends on t through the hash; each t without a common factor with d can be tried, and each fits with a chance of about 1/d, because r is a hash value (heuristic). After that the new statement is true and the rest of the receipt is honest. If T is odd the first step squares y, so ε is replaced by ε² and the factor −1 disappears at once: the receipt cannot see the sign. For a cube root of 1 the heuristic gives 1 − (2/3)² = 56% and we saw 57%; for the factor of order 4 on two cells (ε² = −1, one try, chance 1/2) we saw 51%. In the table an attempt is one exponent with one label added to the hash (40 labels per exponent, to count how often the hash falls right; a real receipt has no such label, so a forger has one try per factor).

| Form | Factor ε | Prime 2^p − 1 used | Attempts | Accepted by the checker | Verdict changed from prime to not prime |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| one cell | -1 (order 2) | 9 | 360 | 360 | 360 |
| one cell | a cube root of 1 (order 3) | 9 | 360 | 205 | 205 |
| two cells | -1 (order 2) | 9 | 360 | 360 | 0 |
| two cells | the true result itself (order 4) | 9 | 360 | 185 | 185 |

On two cells the factor −1 leaves the first cell at 0, so the verdict stays; the factor of order 4 does not. So a receipt of this kind shows that the turns were made by someone who knows the true result. It does not show that the reported result is the true one.

A guard (ours; tested, not proved). Compare not the result but a power of it that removes every factor of small order: on one cell y^L against 9^L, with L = 2 times the prime factors below 2^16 of (n − 1)/2; on two cells whether y^(2^20) = 1. A result that passes this weaker comparison is sent to a full test. All 24 forged results tried were caught this way (12 of 12 on one cell, 12 of 12 on two), and of the 288 composite 2^p − 1 with prime p < 2,000 none was sent to a full test without need.

The page's code and the Python code were checked against each other ([receipt_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_js_check.py) → [receipt_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_js_check.txt)): for 26 exponents from 3 to 9,689 the page's receipt is identical to Python's and accepted by it, and the page's checker accepts 6 honest Python receipts and rejects 12 tampered ones. The page's own SHA-256 agrees with node's on 6 strings.

**A two-cell wheel that is settled alone below 2^64 (the norm lemma is literature; the run is computed and rests on the published list).** Pascal's wheel is settled alone below 2^64 by lemma 1 (the sum of its cells is the doubling wheel), but it needs 5 to 47 cells. Machine 11 has two cells and was checked below 2^64 only together with the doubling wheel. A different start gives a wheel of two cells that needs no second test.

The wheel. Let n be odd and not a perfect square. For a = 1, 2, 3, … take c = a² − 2 (from a = 2 on) and then c = a² + 2. If c and n share a factor and n does not divide c, n is not prime. Stop at the first c with Jacobi symbol (c/n) = −1. The two cells start at (a, 1), that is a + √c, and n is called prime when (a + √c)^n ≡ a − √c (mod n). The starts are 1 + √3, 2 + √2, 2 + √6, 3 + √7, 3 + √11, …

1. Every prime comes home (proved). For a prime n and (c/n) = −1 the numbers x + y√c mod n form a field with n² elements, in which the n-th power is the map √c → −√c. A suitable a exists: for a prime n > 3 the sum of the Legendre symbols of a² + 2 over all a mod n is −1, so a² + 2 is a non-residue for at least (n − 1)/2 values of a; n = 3 uses 2 + √2.
2. The norm lemma (literature: a Frobenius pseudoprime for x² − Px + Q is a Fermat pseudoprime to base Q; here P = 2a, Q = ±2). The map √c → −√c is a ring automorphism mod n. Apply it to (a + √c)^n ≡ a − √c: also (a − √c)^n ≡ a + √c. Multiply the two: (a² − c)^n ≡ a² − c (mod n). Now a² − c = 2 or −2 and n is odd, so 2^n ≡ 2 and 2^(n−1) ≡ 1 (mod n). A composite n that comes home is a base-2 pseudoprime.
3. The run ([norm2.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2.c); the list was streamed from the download and not stored, sha256 3b92a884…f8ef as before). All 118,968,378 numbers of the list were read (last 18,446,744,066,047,760,377). 106,118,387 were put on their wheel and 0 came home; 12,849,989 share a factor with a small c and 2 are perfect squares (1,194,649, 12,327,121), so they are turned away before any turn. The largest a was 27. [norm2_list_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_list_check.py) → [norm2_list_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_list_check.txt) recomputes every 4,000th line in plain Python ([norm2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2.py)): 29,742 lines, 0 differ. A second program, written separately ([norm2_second.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_second.c): plain remainders instead of Montgomery form, the power taken from the lowest bit up), read the whole list again: on a wheel 106,118,387, came home 0, and every count equals the first run.

4. Why it behaves like a pair of wheels (proved). Write a² − c = 2s with s = 1 or −1. Then (a + √c)² = (a² + c) + 2a√c = 2ε with ε = (a² − s) + a√c, and ε has norm (a² − s)² − a²c = 1: the square of the start is twice a unit. So (a + √c)^n = (a + √c) · 2^((n−1)/2) · ε^((n−1)/2), and (a − √c)/(a + √c) = (a − √c)²/(2s) = s/ε. Home is therefore the single equation 2^((n−1)/2) · ε^((n+1)/2) ≡ s (mod n): Euler's test for 2 and a Lucas test for the unit ε, tied together. For the start 1 + √3 the unit is 2 + √3, the wheel of the Lucas–Lehmer test; for 2 + √2 it is 3 + 2√2, the square of 1 + √2. The pair "doubling wheel and a Lucas test" is the idea of Baillie, Pomerance, Selfridge and Wagstaff; here both turn on one shaft, and the first is not run separately.

So, given that the published list is complete: for every odd n below 2^64 this wheel alone says prime exactly when n is prime. The same lemma covers machine 11 when its step is 3, because 1 + √3 has norm −2; for the steps 5, 7, 11, … the norm is −4, −6, −10, … and the list does not apply.

Checks that do not use the list. The lemma was tested where it is not empty ([norm2_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_check.py) → [norm2_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_check.txt)): on wheels that n fits ((c/n) = +1), 164 odd composites below 2,000,000 satisfy (a + √c)^n ≡ a ± √c, and every one is a base-2 pseudoprime. And the wheel was put on every odd number from 3 to 10^10: 455,052,510 came home, and a sieve of Eratosthenes that shares nothing with the wheel ([pcount.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/pcount.c)) counts 455,052,510 odd primes there. The largest a needed by a prime was 23 (n = 2,306,940,767).

The page's code for machine 19, run under node, gives the same start, the same two cells after n turns and the same verdict as [norm2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2.py) on 45,742 numbers (the sampled list lines, 3,000 primes and 3,000 random odd numbers of 19 digits, and every odd n up to 20,001): 0 differ ([norm2_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_js_check.py) → [norm2_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_js_check.txt)).

Every start, not only the first (computed; [norm2_census_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_census_check.py) → [norm2_census.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_census.txt)). The lemma holds for every start of norm ±2, so the list also answers a wider question: does the result depend on the choice of start? Each of the 118,968,378 numbers of the list was put on all 15 starts with a up to 8. On a start it does not fit ((c/n) = −1) home means a − √c; on a start it fits ((c/n) = +1) a prime would leave the cells unchanged, a + √c.

In 689,540,521 tests on a start the number does not fit, 0 came home. In 1,035,819,515 tests on a start it fits, 49,977,429 left the cells unchanged, as a prime would, and 0 swapped them. So here too a number cheats only on a wheel it fits, and the choice of the first start is not what protects machine 19. The sampled results (172,385) were recomputed in plain Python: 0 differ.

What is known. Khashin (2018) states that his own version of the two-cell test (steps −1 and 2 first, then 1 + √c) has no mistake below 2^64; his proof is a long computation on the possible prime factors and does not use the list. We did not find the route above (a start of norm 2, so that the list of base-2 pseudoprimes settles the wheel in one pass) written down; it is a direct consequence of the lemma, so it may well be known. Above 2^64 nothing is proved, and the reasons to expect a mistake of such tests at some very large number apply here too. As a small probe, 12,000 numbers above 2^64 that were built to fool the doubling wheel (Carmichael numbers of Chernick's form and pseudoprimes p(k(p − 1) + 1), up to 6 · 10^60) were put on the wheel: 0 came home ([norm2_beyond.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_beyond.py) → [norm2_beyond.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_beyond.txt)).

Cost ([norm2_bench.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_bench.txt); one run in C, numbers between 2^62 and 2^64, time per number). On 200,000 primes: one strong test to base 2 (the doubling wheel) 498 ns, the norm-2 wheel 1758 ns, machine 11's wheel 1734 ns, the seven strong tests that are known to decide every number below 2^64 3470 ns. On 200,000 random odd numbers, where most are turned away early: 460, 1103, 975 and 593 ns. The program is plain (four multiplications mod n for a squaring of the two cells); it is not tuned.

**Primes before their tick: four machines that do not wait (squaring, the sieve, Legendre's count and the proof steps of Pocklington and Morrison are literature; the runs are computed; the rule log₄(ln n) for the rounds of machine 23 is a fit to the runs, and the reason given for it is a heuristic).** On machines 10 to 19 wheel n decides at tick n: 30 ticks give the 10 primes up to 30. Four machines of the page break that rule, each in another way. The table says below which number every prime is on after 3, 5, 10 and 30 ticks, with as many wheels as the machine can use ([early.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early.py) → [early.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early.txt), [early_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_summary.py) → [early_sum.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_sum.txt); [inherit.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit.py) → [inherit.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit.txt), [inherit_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_summary.py) → [inherit_sum.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_sum.txt)).

| Machine, and what one tick is | Every prime below … is on after tick 3 | after tick 5 | after tick 10 | after tick 30 | All 669 primes below 5,000 on at tick |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 10 to 19: wheel n makes one turn | 4 | 6 | 11 | 31 | 4,999 |
| 21: one new prime gear, taken from the lamps | 49 | 169 | 961 | 16,129 | 19 |
| 20: every wheel squares its cells | 16 | 64 | 2,048 | 2,147,483,648 | 12 |
| 23: a round in which every dark wheel asks the lit ones | 21,767 | 2,223,990,533 | not known | not known | 3 |
| 22: a gear changes counters; it counts and does not find | 25 (counted) | 49 (counted) | 121 (counted) | 961 (counted) | 4,999 for the lamps |

Machine 20, squaring wheels (the verdict is that of machine 19; the count of ticks is proved). The two cells of wheel n hold a + √c, the start of machine 19. Write n in binary as 1 b₁ b₂ … b_k. At tick i the wheel multiplies its two cells with themselves and, when b_i = 1, makes one more plain turn (it multiplies by a + √c).

1. After tick i the cells hold (a + √c)^m with m = (1 b₁ … b_i) in binary (proved: squaring doubles m and the plain turn adds b_i). So after k = ⌊log₂ n⌋ ticks they hold (a + √c)^n, and tick T decides every wheel below 2^(T+1).
2. No wheel that has to make n turns is earlier (proved). Let a tick be one multiplication of two values that the wheel already holds and at most one plain turn. Before the first tick the wheel holds 1 turn, fewer than 2. If the values held after t − 1 ticks stand for fewer than 2^t turns, the new value stands for at most 2(2^t − 1) + 1 < 2^(t+1) turns. So after t ticks fewer than 2^(t+1) turns are made, n turns need t ≥ ⌊log₂ n⌋ ticks, and the wheel is done exactly then. (A test that needs only the power (n ± 1)/2 could be one tick earlier.)
3. Checked ([early.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early.py)): for every n from 2 to 299,999 the verdict equals that of the stepping wheel ([norm2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2.py), which takes the power from the lowest binary digit up) and is right, and the number of ticks is the number of binary digits less one; differences 0 and 0. In C ([norm2.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2.c), [early_dyadic.sh](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_dyadic.sh) → [early_dyadic.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_dyadic.out)) every odd number below 2^32 went on the wheel. The numbers that came home, added up:

| Tick T | 3 | 5 | 10 | 12 | 15 | 20 | 25 | 30 | 31 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lamps (primes below 2^(T+1)) | 6 | 18 | 309 | 1,028 | 6,542 | 155,611 | 3,957,809 | 105,097,565 | 203,280,221 |

   Every one of the 31 totals equals the count of machine 22's recursion for 2^(T+1) − 1, which shares nothing with the wheel.
4. Below 2^64 no number that is not prime comes home, given the list (see the two-cell wheel above). So at tick 63 the 425,656,284,035,217,743 primes below 2^64 would all be on (that count is literature).

The price: every wheel works at every tick. With 5,000 wheels the odd wheels make 25,905 ticks together, 38.7 for each prime found, and a tick is one squaring of two cells and at most one plain turn. Square-and-multiply is as old as the subject; the only thing added here is the clock.

Machine 21, gears from its own lamps (proved; the sieve of Eratosthenes counted in gears). Tick 1 puts in gear 2. At every tick after that the machine takes the smallest lamp that is not yet a gear.

1. Let the gears be the primes p₁, …, p_j and let q be the smallest number above p_j that no gear divides. Then q is prime: a prime factor of q below q is either at most p_j, and then it is a gear, or it lies between p_j and q, and then it is itself a number above p_j that no gear divides, smaller than q. A number m below q² that is not prime has a prime factor at most √m < q, and that factor is a gear. So every number below q² that no gear divides is prime (proved). Tick j lights the primes below p_(j+1)².
2. The run ([early.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early.py); frontier up to 12,000,000, 484 ticks): at every tick each lamp is a prime and every prime below the frontier is lit, by comparison with a separate sieve.

| Tick | Gear put in | Every number decided below | New lamps | Lamps |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2 | 9 | 4 | 4 |
| 2 | 3 | 25 | 5 | 9 |
| 3 | 5 | 49 | 6 | 15 |
| 5 | 11 | 169 | 9 | 39 |
| 10 | 29 | 961 | 16 | 162 |
| 19 | 67 | 5,041 | 66 | 675 |
| 30 | 113 | 16,129 | 354 | 1,877 |
| 100 | 541 | 299,209 | 498 | 25,936 |

3. An open question inside the machine. The new lamps of tick j ≥ 2 are the primes between p_j² and p_(j+1)²: 5, 6, 15, 9, 22, 11, 27, 47, 16, … (OEIS A050216). Brocard's conjecture (1904) says there are always at least four; it is open. In the run the fewest were 5, at tick 2.

The price: it looks at every number below the frontier. With 5,000 wheels that is 8,087 strikes and 4,999 numbers looked at, 19.6 moves for each prime.

Machine 22, counting wheels (proved; Legendre's rule of 1808 in the form known as Lucy's recursion, as in [p9_lucy.c](writeup/code/p9_lucy.c)). Let S(v, p) be the number of m with 2 ≤ m ≤ v that are prime or have no prime factor up to p. Then S(v, 1) = v − 1.

1. For a prime p with p² ≤ v the numbers that leave the count are p · m with p ≤ m ≤ v/p and m without a prime factor below p. There are S(⌊v/p⌋, p − 1) − π(p − 1) of them, and π(p − 1) = S(p − 1, p − 1). So S(v, p) = S(v, p − 1) − (S(⌊v/p⌋, p − 1) − S(p − 1, p − 1)) (proved). For p² > v nothing leaves, so S(v, p) = π(v) as soon as p ≥ √v: a counter is exact once the tick has passed the square root of its number.
2. p is prime exactly when S(p, p − 1) is one more than S(p − 1, p − 1) (proved: among the numbers up to p, only p itself can be without a prime factor below p, so S(p, p − 1) = π(p), and likewise S(p − 1, p − 1) = π(p − 1)). The machine needs no other test.
3. The counters needed for x are those of v = ⌊x/k⌋, because ⌊⌊x/k⌋/p⌋ = ⌊x/(kp)⌋: about 2√x of them. A move is one change of a counter.

| x | Counters | Prime gears | Moves | Primes up to x | Primes per move |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 900 | 59 | 10 | 179 | 154 | 0.86 |
| 10,000 | 199 | 25 | 990 | 1,229 | 1.24 |
| 62,500 | 499 | 53 | 3,597 | 6,275 | 1.74 |
| 1,000,000 | 1,999 | 168 | 24,641 | 78,498 | 3.19 |
| 25,000,000 | 9,999 | 669 | 232,773 | 1,565,927 | 6.73 |
| 100,000,000 | 19,999 | 1,229 | 614,527 | 5,761,455 | 9.38 |
| 1,000,000,000 | 63,244 | 3,401 | 3,092,168 | 50,847,534 | 16.44 |

   For each x the gears whose counter rose are exactly the primes up to √x, and the counters equal π ([early.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early.py), against sympy). With the gears up to N and x = N² the moves stay below the primes counted for every N from 55 on (tried up to 2,999). The box of the page counted the 455,052,511 primes up to 10^10 in 15,697,553 moves ([early_node.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/early_node.js), [early_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_js_check.py) → [early_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_js_check.txt)).

The price: it knows how many primes there are, not which. A machine that finds its primes needs at least one move for each. The fastest counts of this kind need about x^(2/3) steps (Meissel, Lehmer, Lagarias, Miller and Odlyzko, Deléglise and Rivat; fact 2020 of [FACTS.md](FACTS.md)).

Machine 23, wheels that light each other (the proof steps are literature; the count of rounds is computed; its growth is a heuristic). Round 0: only the prime 2 is lit. In every round a dark number N looks at the lit primes that divide N − 1 and at those that divide N + 1. Let F₁ be the part of N − 1 made of lit primes (with their full powers), F₂ the same for N + 1, and L = lcm(F₁, F₂).

1. If L² > N, the lit primes are enough to decide N (proved, from steps 1 and 2 of the chain of wheels above; the combination is that of Brillhart, Lehmer and Selfridge, 1975). Run the one-cell test for the primes of F₁ and the two-cell test, with one step c for all of them, for the primes of F₂. If N fails one of them it is not prime. If it passes, every prime factor r of N is 1 mod F₁ and, with one sign for the whole of F₂, 1 or −1 mod F₂. So r is 1 or x mod L, where x is the number below L that is 1 mod F₁ and −1 mod F₂. A composite N has a prime factor r ≤ √N < L; r cannot be 1 mod L, so r = x. Hence N is not prime when 1 < x < N and x divides N, and prime otherwise: one division finishes the proof.
2. Every prime is lit in the end, and nothing else (proved). For a prime N the witnesses exist: a number that is not an f-th power mod N for the one cell, an element of order N + 1 among the elements of norm 1 for the two cells. By induction the primes below N are lit at some round, and when all prime factors of N − 1 are lit, L ≥ N − 1 > √N.
3. rounds(N) is 1 more than the smallest g such that the primes lit by round g are enough. Round 1 is known without a run (proved from the rule): the primes with (2^m)² > N for the power 2^m in N − 1 or N + 1, that is the Proth primes k · 2^m + 1 with k < 2^m and their mirror images k · 2^m − 1 with k < 2^m: 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 23, 31, 41, 47, 79, 97, …

The runs.

- Python with the real proofs ([inherit.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit.py) → [inherit.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit.txt)): every odd number up to 200,000 was put through the tests with witnesses. The numbers that got a proof are exactly the primes. As a test of step 1, 1,070 odd composites below 3,000 were given every prime factor of n − 1 and n + 1 as helper: 0 passed.
- C by the rule ([inherit.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit.c) → [inherit_c_4e9.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_c_4e9.out); primes by a Miller–Rabin test with the bases 2 to 13, which is exact far beyond this range) up to 4,200,000,000: 198,996,103 primes, the number that the counting recursion of machine 22 gives (the table leaves out the prime 2, which is lit from the start). A second C program, written separately from the description of the rule ([inherit_second.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_second.c) → [inherit_second_4e9.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_second_4e9.out); primes and factors from one sieve, no Miller–Rabin test), gives the same number of primes for every round up to 4,200,000,000 and the same list of 30,789 primes of 5 and 6 rounds. Run on to 8,400,000,000 ([inherit_second_8e9.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_second_8e9.out)), it finds 385,307,831 primes, the number of the counting recursion, and still none of 7 rounds; the primes of 6 rounds are 2,223,990,533, 3,053,740,441, 3,859,111,517, 7,066,121,653. That list was also made without a scan ([inherit_lemma.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_lemma.py) → [inherit_lemma.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_lemma.txt)): by the lemma under "Primes of 7 rounds" below, such a prime is s · q + 1 with q one of the primes of 5 or more rounds below 4.2·10^9, and trying all 104,170 such numbers gives the same 4 primes. The lemma reaches further when it is used on both neighbours ([inherit_lemma_chain.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_lemma_chain.py) → [inherit_lemma_chain_4e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_lemma_chain_4e9.txt), [inherit_lemma_chain_8e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_lemma_chain_8e9.txt)). A prime q up to 4B of g + 1 rounds has a prime factor r of g or more rounds in q − 1 = s · r and one in q + 1 = s′ · r′. If both were above B, then s = s′ = 2 and r′ = r + 1, two primes next to each other. So q = s · r ± 1 with r up to B. From the list up to 4.2·10^9, on which both programs agree: 11 primes of 6 rounds up to 16,800,000,000 (438,896 candidates), and from these no prime of 7 rounds up to 67,200,000,000. From the 73,520 primes of 5 or more rounds up to 8.4·10^9 that the second program lists ([inherit_deep_8e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep_8e9.txt)): 51 primes of 6 rounds up to 33,600,000,000, and no prime of 7 rounds up to 134,400,000,000. A scan confirms the count made without one: the second program with a smaller table ([inherit_third.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_third.c) → [inherit_third_17e9.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_third_17e9.out)) went up to 16,800,000,000, found 746,813,071 primes (the number of the counting recursion), none of 7 rounds, and as primes of 6 rounds exactly the 11 of the lemma. Its list of 172,514 primes of 5 or more rounds ([inherit_deep_17e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep_17e9.txt), [inherit_lemma_chain_17e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_lemma_chain_17e9.txt)) gives 119 primes of 6 rounds up to 67,200,000,000 and no prime of 7 rounds up to 268,800,000,000.

| Rounds | Primes up to 4,200,000,000 | Smallest such prime |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 19,273 | 3 |
| 2 | 62,240,805 | 19 |
| 3 | 127,499,945 | 173 |
| 4 | 9,205,290 | 21,767 |
| 5 | 30,786 | 1,688,173 |
| 6 | 3 | 2,223,990,533 |

- From the top down with PARI ([inherit_big.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big.py) → [inherit_big_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_check.txt)): the same rule, worked out from N downwards, agrees on the 17,983 odd primes up to 200,000 and gives 6 rounds for 2,223,990,533. Its neighbours are 4 × 555,997,633 and 2 × 3 × 370,665,089, and both large primes need 5 rounds.

- A third count ([inherit_hist.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_hist.py) → [inherit_hist.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_hist.txt)): the rule from the top down for every prime up to 20,000,000; the numbers of primes per round equal those of the C program (1,270,606 odd primes). It also confirms the 15 primes of 5 and 6 rounds that the C run printed; the three primes of 6 rounds up to 4.2·10^9 are 2,223,990,533, 3,053,740,441, 3,859,111,517.
- The box of the page proves a typed number of up to 30 digits and lists its helpers round by round. For 2,223,990,533 it shows the helpers 555,997,633, 277,998,817, 138,999,409, 1,263,631, 577, 2; here 555,997,633 = 2 × 277,998,817 − 1 and 277,998,817 = 2 × 138,999,409 − 1. On 1,031 primes ([inherit_node.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/inherit_node.js), [inherit_box_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_box_check.py) → [inherit_box_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_box_check.txt)) its rounds equal those of PARI, except for 5 primes of 30 digits where the page cannot take a neighbour apart; there it says so and gives a longer proof.

With N − 1 alone ([inherit_c_minus_1e9.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_c_minus_1e9.out), up to 1,000,000,000) the same count needs up to 14 rounds; the smallest primes of each round are 3, 7, 23, 47, 283, 719, 1,439, 2,879, 34,549, 138,197, 1,266,767, 14,920,303, 36,449,279, 377,982,107. From 23 on, all 12 terms equal those of OEIS A056637 (the least prime of each class n− in the classification of Erdős and Selfridge, where every prime factor of p − 1 counts, down to 2 and 3). OEIS A082449 (the smallest prime for which taking the greatest prime factor of p − 1 again and again needs n steps) has one other term, 14,619,833 where these lists have 14,920,303. For the primes between 2^28 and 2^29 the mean is 4.30 rounds with N − 1 alone and 2.68 with both neighbours.

How the rounds grow ([inherit_big.sh](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big.sh) → [inherit_big.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big.out): random primes, factored with PARI).

| Size | Primes | Mean rounds | Most | log₄(ln n) + 0.51 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2^10 to 2^11 (every prime) | 137 | 1.99 | – | 1.95 |
| 2^15 to 2^16 (every prime) | 3,030 | 2.20 | – | 2.23 |
| 2^20 to 2^21 (every prime) | 73,586 | 2.39 | – | 2.43 |
| 2^25 to 2^26 (every prime) | 1,894,120 | 2.58 | – | 2.58 |
| 2^30 to 2^31 (every prime) | 50,697,537 | 2.74 | – | 2.71 |
| 12 digits (random) | 3,000 | 2.93 | 5 | 2.89 |
| 15 digits (random) | 3,000 | 3.10 | 5 | 3.05 |
| 20 digits (random) | 3,000 | 3.28 | 5 | 3.26 |
| 25 digits (random) | 2,000 | 3.41 | 5 | 3.43 |
| 30 digits (random) | 1,500 | 3.55 | 5 | 3.56 |
| 35 digits (random) | 800 | 3.67 | 5 | 3.67 |
| 40 digits (random) | 500 | 3.80 | 5 | 3.77 |
| 50 digits (random) | 200 | 3.93 | 5 | 3.93 |
| 60 digits (random) | 80 | 4.08 | 5 | 4.06 |

The rule of thumb (heuristic). Suppose the primes below y are lit after g rounds. The part of a random number that is made of primes below y has, on average, about as many digits as y (the sum of ln p/(p − 1) over p ≤ y is ln y + O(1)). N is lit in the next round when F₁ and F₂ together have more than half the digits of N, and each neighbour supplies about ln y: 2 ln y > ½ ln N, that is N < y⁴. So one more round raises the size that can be reached to about its fourth power, and rounds(N) ≈ log₄(ln N) + constant. With N − 1 alone the same count gives N < y², hence log₂(ln N). The fit (computed): a straight line through 30 size classes (every class from 2^10 to 2^31 and the random sizes from 12 to 60 digits; the table above shows some of them; mean rounds against ln ln N) has slope 0.73, where 1/ln 4 = 0.72; and with the slope fixed at 1/ln 4 the difference rounds − log₄(ln N) lies between 0.47 and 0.56 for every one of these classes. With N − 1 alone and the slope fixed at 1/ln 2 the difference lies between −0.01 and 0.16 for the classes 2^10 to 2^28.

The rule tried on sizes it was not made from (computed; [inherit_runs.sh](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_runs.sh) → [inherit_big_65.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_65.out), [inherit_big_65b.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_65b.out), [inherit_big_70.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_70.out), [inherit_big_70b.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_70b.out), [inherit_big_75.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_75.out), [inherit_big_75b.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_75b.out), [inherit_big_80.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_80.out), [inherit_big_80b.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_80b.out)). The constant 0.51 comes from the classes up to 2^31 and the random sizes up to 60 digits. Afterwards:

| Size | Primes | Mean rounds | Standard error | The rule says | Difference in standard errors |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2^31 to 2^32, every prime | 98,182,656 | 2.767 | – | 2.736 | – |
| 2^32 to 2^33, every prime | 190,335,585 | 2.795 | – | 2.759 | – |
| 65 digits, random, first sample | 60 | 4.083 | 0.055 | 4.120 | −0.7 |
| 65 digits, random, second sample | 60 | 4.100 | 0.066 | 4.120 | −0.3 |
| 65 digits, the samples together | 120 | 4.092 | 0.043 | 4.120 | −0.7 |
| 70 digits, random, first sample | 40 | 4.175 | 0.079 | 4.174 | 0.0 |
| 70 digits, random, second sample | 40 | 4.200 | 0.073 | 4.174 | 0.4 |
| 70 digits, the samples together | 80 | 4.188 | 0.054 | 4.174 | 0.3 |
| 75 digits, random, first sample | 40 | 4.050 | 0.071 | 4.224 | −2.4 |
| 75 digits, random, second sample | 40 | 4.325 | 0.083 | 4.224 | 1.2 |
| 75 digits, the samples together | 80 | 4.188 | 0.057 | 4.224 | −0.6 |
| 80 digits, random, first sample | 24 | 4.208 | 0.104 | 4.270 | −0.6 |
| 80 digits, random, second sample | 24 | 4.292 | 0.112 | 4.270 | 0.2 |
| 80 digits, the samples together | 48 | 4.250 | 0.076 | 4.270 | −0.3 |

None of the random primes needed more than 6 rounds. The second samples need a word. The first sample of 75 digits lay −2.4 standard errors from the rule, so a second one was drawn for that size with another seed; it lies 1.2 standard errors on the other side. A second sample drawn because the first stood out is not a clean test. Afterwards a second sample was drawn for every other size as well, so that no size is treated differently; all are shown. Taken one by one, the samples of random primes differ from the rule by at most 0.17 rounds. The two whole classes come from the later scan ([inherit_third_17e9.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_third_17e9.out)); the lower one was partly, up to 4.2·10^9, in the first version of the fit. They lie 0.03 and 0.04 rounds above the rule, and that is not noise: the difference mean − log₄(ln N) rises slowly through the classes 2^29 to 2^33 (0.529, 0.535, 0.541, 0.546), and is still rising at the top of the scanned range. Over all classes it stays between 0.47 and 0.56; the rule is a rule of thumb with that much play, not a law with a fixed constant.

Because the large sizes allow only small samples, the rule was also tried on 11 sizes between those of the fit, with 27,300 random primes in all ([inherit_big_more.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_big_more.out)):

| Size | Primes | Mean rounds | Standard error | The rule says | Difference | In standard errors |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 11 digits | 3,000 | 2.869 | 0.009 | 2.824 | +0.045 | 4.8 |
| 13 digits | 3,000 | 3.008 | 0.009 | 2.947 | +0.061 | 6.9 |
| 14 digits | 3,000 | 3.045 | 0.009 | 3.002 | +0.043 | 4.9 |
| 16 digits | 3,000 | 3.129 | 0.008 | 3.100 | +0.029 | 3.5 |
| 17 digits | 3,000 | 3.183 | 0.008 | 3.144 | +0.039 | 4.7 |
| 18 digits | 3,000 | 3.225 | 0.008 | 3.186 | +0.039 | 4.6 |
| 19 digits | 3,000 | 3.241 | 0.009 | 3.226 | +0.015 | 1.7 |
| 22 digits | 3,000 | 3.326 | 0.009 | 3.333 | −0.007 | −0.8 |
| 28 digits | 2,000 | 3.473 | 0.012 | 3.509 | −0.036 | −3.0 |
| 32 digits | 1,000 | 3.603 | 0.018 | 3.606 | −0.003 | −0.2 |
| 45 digits | 300 | 3.907 | 0.030 | 3.853 | +0.054 | 1.7 |

Here the answer is sharp. The rule is never off by more than 0.06 rounds, but it is not exact: 6 of the 11 differences are more than three standard errors. The mean moves in a slow wave around log₄(ln N) + 0.51, above it from 11 to 18 digits and below it near 28. So the honest form of the rule is: rounds = log₄(ln N) + 0.51, give or take 0.06. The crests of the wave (13 digits, and again 40 to 45 digits) and its troughs (the class 2^17, and again 28 digits) lie about one round apart on the scale of ln ln N, where a round is ln 4 = 1.39: that is what a count in whole rounds would do. With two crests and two troughs this is an impression, not a result.

The reason tested where it had not been fitted (computed; [inherit_sources.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_sources.py) → [inherit_sources_1.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_sources_1.out), [inherit_sources_2.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_sources_2.out), [inherit_sources_3.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_sources_3.out), [inherit_sources_5.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_sources_5.out)). The same reasoning says more: with k sources of helpers each source has to supply 1/(2k) of the digits, so the rounds should grow like log_(2k)(ln N), with slope 1/ln(2k) against ln ln N. The lit part of N² + 1 is such a source, and for k = 5 also those of N² + N + 1 and N² − N + 1 (the proof steps for these numbers, wheels of four, three and six cells, are literature and were not built here; only the rounds were counted, with helpers below N). The slope 1/ln(2k) contains no fitted number. Every prime in six classes from 2^10 to 2^21 and random primes of 9 to 30 digits:

| Sources of helpers | k | Slope measured | 1/ln(2k) |
|---|---|---|---|
| N − 1 | 1 | 1.38 | 1.44 |
| N − 1 and N + 1 (machine 23) | 2 | 0.71 | 0.72 |
| N − 1, N + 1 and N² + 1 | 3 | 0.59 | 0.56 |
| N − 1, N + 1, N² + 1, N² + N + 1 and N² − N + 1 | 5 | 0.54 | 0.43 |

For k = 2 this fourth program gives the same class means as the C program. For k = 5 the straight line does not fit: slope 0.54 where the reasoning says 0.43. The curve there is a step: almost flat from 2^10 to 2^21 (1.93 to 2.02 rounds) and rising after that. A second measure, chosen after seeing the step and therefore worth less: from the first class to the last the mean rises by 2.94, 1.49, 1.18, 1.06 rounds for k = 1, 2, 3, 5, where the reasoning says 3.22, 1.61, 1.25, 0.97. So the size of the effect is right within about 10 % in all four cases, and the straight line is a fair picture only when one round is short compared with the range of sizes.

Primes of 7 rounds exist (computed; a search by construction, [inherit_deep.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep.py) → [inherit_deep_first.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep_first.out), [inherit_deep2.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep2.out)). Each neighbour of a prime of g + 1 rounds has a prime factor of g rounds or more (proved: otherwise that neighbour is lit completely by round g − 1, and one whole neighbour is larger than √N by itself). So candidates were built on the deep primes already known. From the 30,789 primes r of 5 or 6 rounds below 4.2·10^9 ([inherit_deep_4e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep_4e9.txt)), q = s · r ± 1 with even s up to 2,000 gave 3,336 primes of 6 rounds or more; from these, N = s · q ± 1 below 320,000,000,000,000 gave 4 primes of 7 rounds. The smallest is 76,175,908,868,713 = 24 × 3,173,996,202,863 + 1; the box of the page proves it in 7 rounds as well. A wider first stage ([inherit_runs.sh](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_runs.sh) → [inherit_deep_wide.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep_wide.out), [inherit_deep_wide2.out](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_deep_wide2.out); every even s up to the smaller of r and 3.9·10^13 / r: 1,059,562,414 candidates, 42,468 primes of 6 rounds or more) gave no smaller one below 77,000,000,000,000. This search finds examples. It does not show that this is the smallest prime of 7 rounds: that one lies between 268,800,000,000 (see the lemma used twice, above; 67,200,000,000 if only the list of both programs is used) and 76,175,908,868,713. Whether the number of rounds is unbounded is not proved.

What is known, and what we did not find. The two proof steps are Pocklington's (1914) and Morrison's (1975). Counting levels of such proofs is known in two forms: the classes of Erdős and Selfridge (all prime factors of p + 1, or of p − 1, down to 2 and 3; OEIS A005113 and A056637) and the Pratt tree (all prime factors of p − 1), whose height Ford, Konyagin and Luca (2010) bound for almost all primes and for which their model suggests about e · ln ln p. The count used here differs: both neighbours, and only as many helpers as it takes to pass √N. We did not find it in a search, and the local copy of the OEIS (31 October 2026) contains neither 2, 3, 19, 173, 21767, 1688173, 2223990533 nor the list of round 1 as one sequence (its two halves are there: the Proth primes, A080076, and the primes k · 2^m − 1 with k < 2^m, A112715). It is a small step from known things and may well be known.

What machine 23 is not. It is not a faster way to find primes: a round is heavy, because every dark wheel tries in every round, and a machine that may turn as much as it likes within one tick can let each wheel decide alone, as machine 20 does. For a large N the hard part is taking N − 1 and N + 1 apart. What the rounds measure is how deep a proof by these two steps has to go.

On the page ([page_check_20_23.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/page_check_20_23.txt); the page's own code in headless Chrome with 5,000 wheels): machine 20 has 669 lamps at tick 12, machine 21 at tick 19, machine 23 after round 3, machine 22 at tick 5,000 with 1,565,927 primes counted; no mistake on any of them. The page code of the four machines was compared with Python ([early_node.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/early_node.js), [early_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_js_check.py) → [early_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_js_check.txt): 270 comparisons, 0 differ; [inherit_node.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/inherit_node.js), [inherit_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_js_check.py) → [inherit_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_js_check.txt): rounds, helpers, witnesses and the division for 2,638 odd numbers, 0 differ).

**What this is and is not.** No wheel here is new mathematics: the pair is the test of Baillie, Pomerance, Selfridge and Wagstaff, the two-cell wheel is a Frobenius test (Khashin's, here with odd prime steps only; for his own version, which tries the steps −1 and 2 first, he states that there is no mistake below 2^64), and Pascal's wheel is the congruence of Agrawal, Kayal and Saxena, whose proved test needs many rings and offsets. What the contest adds is the common rule in gear terms (in every case found a number cheats only on a wheel it fits, so take a wheel it does not fit; for the ring this is the exception clause of Popovych's version of the conjecture, carried over here to the wheels of two and three cells), the check of Pascal's wheel up to 2^64 on every ring up to 42 cells, the folding of the ring onto small wheels with its link to the Fibonacci wheel and the prize problem, the remark that the two-cell wheel is the Lucas–Lehmer test on Mersenne numbers, and a small statement on prime powers. A proof that either kept machine never errs would give a prime test of a few lines and would be a major result; so would the first number that fools it.

**The page.** Machines 10 and 11 of [prime3.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime3.html) are Pascal's wheel and the two-cell wheel: at tick n wheel n decides, the lamps stay on, a picture shows every turn of the wheel, and a box tests a number of up to 2,000 digits. Among 250 wheels all 53 prime lamps are on at tick 241, all 12 exponents at tick 127, all 33 twin primes at tick 241, and no other lamp; the two-cell wheel gives the same ([duo_row.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/duo_row.py) → [duo_row.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/duo_row.txt)). Machines 12 to 16 are the three-cell wheel, Wilson's wheel, Lucas's wheel, the two cells with check turns and the curve wheel, built the same way; they give the same three results ([rows_12_13.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/rows_12_13.py) → [rows_12_13.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/rows_12_13.txt), [curve_wheel.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/curve_wheel.txt)), machine 17 is the chain of wheels ([chain_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_summary.py) → [chain.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain.txt), [page_check_17.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/page_check_17.txt)); machine 19 is the two-cell wheel from a start of norm 2 ([norm2_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_summary.py) → [norm2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2.txt); 669 lamps and no mistake with 5,000 wheels, [page_check_19.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/page_check_19.txt)); machine 18 is the two-cell wheel with a receipt, where wheel p decides 2^p − 1 ([receipt_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_summary.py) → [receipt.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt.txt); with 5,000 wheels it lights the 20 Mersenne exponents below 5,000 and nothing else, [page_check_18.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/page_check_18.txt)); and with 5,000 wheels the page's own code lights 669 lamps and makes no mistake on each of machines 10 to 17 ([page_check_10_15.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/page_check_10_15.txt)). The test box of machine 14 proves 3 · 2^2208 + 1 and that of machine 15 proves 2^4423 − 1. The tape and the square jumps, which gave the same results as machine 1, were removed.

## 21. Gaps between Mersenne exponents (computed; the size law is the conjecture of Lenstra, Pomerance and Wagstaff; the remainder law is elementary)

The question: do the gaps between consecutive Mersenne exponents follow a rule, in their size or in their remainders, that random primes would not follow? The test is run on both pages: two layouts on [prime2.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime2.html) and machine 24 on [prime3.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime3.html).

**The data.** The 52 known exponents. GIMPS has checked every exponent below 83,200,483 twice (fact 6301 of [FACTS.md](FACTS.md)), so the first 51, up to 82,589,933, are certainly consecutive; for the gap from 82,589,933 to 136,279,841 that is not yet certain, and it is left out of every count. The 50 certain gaps: 1, 2, 2, 6, 4, 2, 12, 30, 28, 18, 20, 394, 86, 672, 924, 78, 936, 1,036, 170, 5,266, 252, 1,272, 8,724, 1,764, 1,508, 21,288, 41,746, 24,260, 21,546, 84,042, 540,748, 102,594, 398,354, 140,482, 1,577,952, 45,156, 3,951,216, 6,494,324, 7,529,094, 3,040,572, 1,928,368, 4,437,506, 2,180,200, 4,574,010, 5,487,134, 468,808, 14,772,552, 16,322,120, 3,025,636, 5,357,016. All but the first (2 → 3) are even.

**Sizes (computed against a conjecture).** The heuristic of Lenstra, Pomerance and Wagstaff says that log₂ of the exponents behaves like a random scatter (a Poisson process) with e^γ ≈ 1.781 points per doubling. Then log₂(p_(n+1)/p_n) is exponential with mean e^(−γ) = 0.5615. Tested with 20,000 runs of the model for each statistic ([mexp_gaps.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/mexp_gaps.py) → [mexp_gaps.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/mexp_gaps.txt)):

| Exponents | Gaps | Mean log₂ ratio | p | Kolmogorov–Smirnov distance | p | Correlation of one ratio with the next | p |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| from 5 on | 48 | 0.500 | 0.45 | 0.102 | 0.66 | −0.10 | 0.46 |
| from 127 on | 39 | 0.495 | 0.46 | 0.154 | 0.29 | −0.08 | 0.61 |

The largest ratio is 2^2.04 (127 → 521), the smallest 2^0.0158 (9,689 → 9,941). Counted per doubling from 2^2 to 2^26 the exponents are 2, 1, 3, 1, 3, 0, 0, 2, 1, 3, 2, 3, 3, 1, 2, 2, 0, 2, 2, 2, 1, 1, 5, 4: mean 1.92, variance 1.56, so variance/mean 0.81; a Poisson count is this even or more in 28 % of runs. The sizes fit the model; a large jump is not followed by a small one more often than chance.

**Remainders (elementary, then computed).** m divides p_(n+1) − p_n exactly when the two exponents leave the same remainder mod m. For a random large prime the remainder mod m is spread evenly over the φ(m) remainders prime to m, so two independent ones agree with probability 1/φ(m). The values of m were fixed before the run: 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9, 11, 12, 13, 16, 24. Counted over the 42 gaps between the exponents from 61 on, against two models with 5,000 runs each. In model A every exponent is replaced by a random prime of about its size (window ×/÷ 1.5 below 10,000, ±5 % above). Model B uses only random primes for which 2^q − 1 has no factor 2kq + 1 with k ≤ 100, as a Mersenne exponent has none:

| m | Gaps that are multiples of m | 42/φ(m) | Model A | Model B | p (model B, two-sided) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3 | 21 | 21.00 | 20.9 | 21.1 | 1.00 |
| 4 | 26 | 21.00 | 21.0 | 21.0 | 0.17 |
| 5 | 6 | 10.50 | 10.4 | 10.6 | 0.13 |
| 7 | 10 | 7.00 | 7.0 | 7.1 | 0.33 |
| 8 | 12 | 10.50 | 10.5 | 10.6 | 0.73 |
| 9 | 10 | 7.00 | 7.0 | 7.2 | 0.34 |
| 11 | 3 | 4.20 | 4.2 | 4.3 | 0.71 |
| 12 | 14 | 10.50 | 10.4 | 10.7 | 0.32 |
| 13 | 5 | 3.50 | 3.5 | 3.7 | 0.61 |
| 16 | 4 | 5.25 | 5.2 | 5.3 | 0.72 |
| 24 | 8 | 5.25 | 5.3 | 5.4 | 0.33 |

[mexp_gaps_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/mexp_gaps_check.py) → [mexp_gaps_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/mexp_gaps_check.txt) recomputes the gaps, the sizes and every count with sympy and plain integers, and finds model A within 0.6 of 42/φ(m) for each m (0 differ). The smallest p is 0.13; with eleven values of m that is what chance gives. The largest deviation is for m = 4 and 5. For m = 4 the exponents are more often ≡ 1 (mod 4) than random primes (28 of 43 from 61 on; model B gives 51.4 %). This tilt is known: a factor 2p + 1 of 2^p − 1 is possible only for p ≡ 3 (mod 4), and trial division up to k = 100 captures only part of it.

**The gear version (machine 24 on prime3.html).** A tick is one gap; wheel m is home when m divides the gap. Over the wheels 2 to 60 and the same 42 gaps, 4 wheels are off at the 5 % level in model B: 15 (1 home, 5.4 expected), 21 (8 home, 3.6 expected), 30 (1 home, 5.4 expected), 42 (8 home, 3.6 expected). They are two pairs, m and 2m, so really two findings: few gaps are multiples of 15 and many are multiples of 21. A sequence drawn from model B itself gives 1.2 such wheels on average, and 4 or more in 8 % of runs. That is not unusual, and the wheels 15 and 21 were not chosen in advance.

**What the gaps cannot do.** If the model holds and no exponent below 142,351,031 is missing (every exponent below it has been tested once, fact 6301), the next exponent has median 186 million and lies below 349 million with 90 % chance (heuristic). The gaps give no sharper prediction, because they look like those of random primes chosen at the right rate.

**On the pages.**
- prime2.html, "Gaps between Mersenne exponents: one row per gap, on a doubling scale" (`expgaprows`). Each row runs from one exponent to the next, drawn with the primes in between, so the length of a row is log₂ of the ratio.
- prime2.html, "Gaps between Mersenne exponents: remainders mod P" (`expgapmod`). Only the exponents, one row each, in the column of their remainder mod P; with Links: In order, a vertical link is a gap that is a multiple of P. The note counts that over all 51 certain exponents for any P and gives the binomial p against 1/φ(P).
- Both layouts are in [lx.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/lx.js) (added by [edit32.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/edit32.py)).
- On every layout of prime2.html the option Links: "In order, with the gaps as numbers" writes the gap on each link between consecutive exponents, and lists the gaps under the picture. The toggle "Highlight the exponents" dims everything else and rings the exponents. Both were added by [edit33.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/edit33.py); the links are page code, so they need no check beyond the screenshots.
- Screenshots: [shots/eg_rows_1e6.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/eg_rows_1e6.jpg), [shots/eg_mod4.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/eg_mod4.jpg), [shots/eg_mod5.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/eg_mod5.jpg), [shots/eg_mod12.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/eg_mod12.jpg); with the gaps as numbers and the exponents highlighted: [shots/gp_rows_hi.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/gp_rows_hi.jpg), [shots/gp_mod4_hi.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/gp_mod4_hi.jpg), [shots/gp_spiral_hi.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/gp_spiral_hi.jpg), [shots/gp_sacks_hi.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/gp_sacks_hi.jpg), [shots/gp_plain_rows.jpg](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/shots/gp_plain_rows.jpg).
- prime3.html, machine 24: the status line counts the multiples of 3, 4, 5 and 7 among the gaps so far. [page_check_24.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/page_check_24.txt) shows that at tick 50 it gives the counts of the table (21, 26, 6, 10).

What this is and is not: the size test repeats, with today's 51 exponents, a check that Wagstaff and others made with fewer. The remainder test is elementary. Neither finds anything beyond the model.

## 22. Prime beads: drag the numbers yourself ([prime4.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime4.html); a page to play with, nothing claimed)

The numbers 1 to 200 (20 to 500) are beads, the primes lit. A bead is tied with elastic to its neighbours. The neighbours are n − 1 and n + 1 (a string), its prime factors (every number that is not prime hangs on each prime that divides it), both, or n ± 6 (six strings). Dragging a bead pulls its neighbours along; a double-click (a long press on a phone) pins it. The elastic is tied in the shape the beads have when the ties are made, so nothing moves until a bead is dragged. Start shapes: the numbers in order on a circle (rolled up into a spiral when it does not fit), in rows of 10, as a snake, as an Ulam spiral, or at random.

Under the picture a meter asks whether the primes lie closer together than chance. For every prime it takes the distance to the nearest other prime and compares the mean with 150 random groups of the same size: from all beads, from the odd beads (and 2), and from the beads that leave 1 or 5 after division by 6 (and 2, 3). A clump that only the first comparison shows comes from parity or from the remainders mod 6, not from the primes themselves. With the prime factors as ties the primes above 100 hang on nothing and float away from the composite numbers, which pull together around 2 and 3; the meter then reports the primes as further apart than random beads. That is how the ties are made, not a property of the primes.

## 23. Prime balance ([prime5.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime5.html); a page to watch; the checks are computed, the famous numbers are literature)

A balance receives weights one by one, on the left or the right pan by a rule; a weight weighs 1, its own value, or its logarithm. The weights are the primes or, as an option for the five rules on primes, the 52 known Mersenne exponents p themselves (2, 3, 5, 7, 13, …; not 2^p − 1). The rules: the primes in turn (left, right, left, …); each prime on the lighter pan; each prime on a random pan; primes ≡ 1 (mod 4) left and ≡ 3 right (Chebyshev's bias); primes ≡ 1 (mod 3) left and ≡ 2 right; every number by the parity of its number of prime factors (Liouville, Pólya's problem); the square-free numbers by that parity (Mertens). Start adds weights continuously (1 to 200,000 per second); the page counts how often the pans are equal, which pan was heavier how often, the lead changes and the longest leads, and draws left minus right over time (with ±√n for Mertens). A log lists every time the pans are equal as a plain list (the step numbers, the numbers just added, or the weight of each pan; for the square-free rule with weight 1 the numbers just added are the square-free n with M(n) = 0: 2, 39, 58, 65, 93, …), or line by line with all details. It copies the list and downloads everything as CSV (up to 100,000 entries). The numbers come from a segmented sieve in the page (primes up to 10^5 as divisors, so up to 10^10).

Two remarks that follow from the rules (proved, elementary). With the primes in turn and their own values the pans are never equal after the start: after an even number of steps the right pan is heavier by the sum of every second gap. With each prime on the lighter pan the heavier side flips at every step, because each new prime exceeds the difference so far, and the difference is the alternating sum p − p′ + p″ − … again.

What the page finds by itself, checked against a separate numpy sieve ([page_check_5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/page_check_5.txt)): after six primes in turn, left minus right is −5; with the weight 1 the primes ≡ 1 (mod 4) lead for the first time at 26,861 (Leech 1957); the Liouville balance up to 10^6 ends at −530 and the left pan is never heavier from 2 on (Pólya's question; the first exception is 906,150,257, Tanaka 1980); the Mertens balance at the last square-free number below 10^6 is M(999,997) = 212; with mod 3 the left pan never leads up to 2,750,171. In the usual count, which includes 2, the primes ≡ 1 (mod 3) first lead at 608,981,813,029 (Bays and Hudson 1978); the page leaves out 2, so its left pan gets ahead when the usual count is tied, which happens at the latest at the prime before that one. With the Mersenne exponents as weights and the weight 1 each, the exponents ≡ 1 (mod 4) first lead at 17, the right pan leads again several times (for the last time at 4,423), and from 9,941 on the left pan stays ahead (32 against 19 at the end, equal at the steps 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 18, 20, counted without 2): the opposite of the primes. A tilt towards 1 (mod 4) is expected (for p ≡ 3 (mod 4) with 2p + 1 prime, 2p + 1 divides 2^p − 1; Wagstaff's heuristic weighs a prime p ≡ 1 by ln 6p and p ≡ 3 by ln 2p), but it predicts only 27.1 of the 51 odd exponents in class 1 (standard deviation 3.6); the excess of 32 over 27.1 is 1.4 standard deviations, so most of the split is chance. Mod 3: 24 against 26, equal at the steps 2, 4, 36, 46, 48. In turn with their own values the right pan ends 95,157,121 heavier and the pans are never equal. A separate Python loop gives the same lines.

**Twelve more rules and a backtest (computed; the digit-sum explanation is a heuristic with a known history, the rest is literature).** New rules: primes ≡ 1 (mod 8) (weighing 3) against 3, 5, 7 (mod 8); last digit 1 or 9 against 3 or 7; the same last digit as the prime before (weighing 3) against another; even against odd sum of binary digits, and of decimal digits; twins p, p + 2 against cousins p, p + 4; twins (weighing 2) against pairs p, p + 6; 2p + 1 prime against 2p − 1 prime; consecutive primes 2 apart against 4 apart, and 2 apart against 6 apart; a larger against a smaller next gap; every number by the parity of its number of different prime factors. New weight: 1/n. A limit stops the run after the numbers up to 5,000, 100,000, 10^6 or 10^10.

The backtest ([ref.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/ref.py) independent of the page, [pagerun.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/pagerun.js) runs the page's own code; [compare_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/compare_5000.txt) and [compare_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/compare_1e6.txt)): all 104 runs ((18 + 8) × 4: the 18 rules without the random one, three of which use all numbers or the square-free ones; the 8 rules that also take the Mersenne exponents; 4 weights) agree between page and program in every count, total, equal moment, lead change and longest lead, up to 5,000 and up to 10^6. [analyse.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/analyse.py) → [analysis.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/analysis.txt) reads the runs to 5,000 as a forecast (which pan is heavier most of the time) and checks it up to 10^6 (for the exponents: 20 against all 52). With the weight 1 each, 14 of the 18 rules keep the side that is heavier most of the time; two of these are trivial (in turn and on the lighter pan, the left pan is heavier at half the steps and the pans are equal at the other half):
- Chebyshev's bias (mod 4, mod 3, mod 8, last digits 1, 9 against 3, 7): the side of the non-squares leads 99 to 100 % of the time at both sizes.
- Repeated last digits are rarer (Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan); gaps of 6 overtake gaps of 2; Pólya's Liouville balance; the Möbius balance stays a fair race.
- The fair races of Hardy and Littlewood (twins against cousins, gaps 2 against 4, twins counted double against pairs 6 apart, 2p + 1 against 2p − 1) end within 50 of each other among thousands. The first three change the side that leads most between 5,000 and 10^6; 2p + 1 keeps it (98 % and 87 % of the steps) but ends behind. Chance either way.
- The parity of the number of different prime factors turns: the even side is heavier at 99 % of the steps up to 5,000 (though not at n = 3 to 50) and the odd side at 99 % up to 10^6; the last n where the even side is not lighter is 12,100, and at 10^6 the difference is −1,908.
- "A larger next gap" leads 98 % of the time but by 56 in 75,502: chance, not a lean.

The digit sums lean, and the cause is one small prime (computed, [baselines.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/baselines.py) → [baselines.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/balance/baselines.txt)). For even b the parity of the digit sum in base b equals the parity of the alternating digit sum A, and A ≡ n (mod b + 1) (both proved). A number not divisible by b + 1 cannot have A = 0, the most likely even value, so it more often has an odd digit sum (heuristic). Base 2: numbers without the factors 2 and 3 have an odd binary digit sum 57.1 % of the time up to 5,000 and 52.7 % up to 10^6; the primes 58.7 % and 53.0 %. Base 10: numbers without 2, 3, 5 show no lean (50.00 %), adding 11 gives 51.0 %, adding 7 and 13 instead of 11 gives none (49.98 %); the primes 51.2 % (one standard deviation of chance 0.18 %). The primes lean a little more than these numbers (53.03 % against 52.73 %, 1.7 standard deviations; 51.22 % against 50.99 %, 1.3), so the factor b + 1 explains most of the lean, not provably all (empirical). Literature: in base 2 this is Newman's phenomenon (Newman 1969: the multiples of 3 favour an even binary digit sum); Shevelev (arXiv:0707.1761, 2007) found the odd lean of the primes in base 2 up to 10^9 and gave a heuristic for it; Gupta and Sridhar (arXiv:1812.11841) report the base-10 lean for the first 5 · 10^7 primes and know no reason for it. The link of the base-10 lean to the factor 11 we did not find in their abstracts (we did not read the full papers). Mauduit and Rivat's theorem (the share tends to 1/2) holds either way.

## 24. Prime race ([prime6.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime6.html); the bias is literature, the backtest is computed)

A horse for every remainder a prime can leave after division by q (q = 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 10, 11, 12, 24, 30; with 10 the horses are the last digits 1, 3, 7, 9). Every prime moves its own horse one step; the track shows each horse's count minus the average, the squares mod q are marked. Counted: the leader after every prime (a tie has no leader), the share of the time each horse led, the first lead of each horse, every change of the leader (a log of the primes at which it changed). The racers can also be the 52 Mersenne exponents.

Backtest ([ref6.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/race/ref6.py) independent of the page, [pagerun6.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/race/pagerun6.js) runs the page's code; all 40 races, primes and exponents, up to 5,000 and up to 10^6 (exponents: 20 and 52), agree in every count, lead time, first lead and the first 300 changes of the leader; [analysis6.json](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/race/analysis6.json) → [analysis6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/race/analysis6.txt)):
- The squares lag at both sizes. For q = 3, 5, 8, 10, 12, 24, 30 a square horse led 0.0 % of the time up to 10^6 (with 10 a square led for the first time at 72,269); for q = 7 and 11, where half of the horses are squares, they led 7.7 % and 4.5 % of the time, not 50 %. With q = 4 the square 1 leads first at 26,861 (Leech).
- Which of the other horses leads longest is not predictable from the run to 5,000: it changed for q = 5, 11, 12, 24, 30. Among the non-squares the race is close.
- The Mersenne exponents run the other way for q = 4 and 8: the square 1 takes the lead (the known tilt; 52 racers are few).

This is Chebyshev's bias in the form of Rubinstein and Sarnak (1994) and Granville and Martin (Prime number races, 2006); the page and the backtest show it, they add nothing to the theory.

## 25. Prime Galton board ([prime7.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime7.html); the fair coin is Dirichlet's theorem, the backtest is computed)

Numbers fall through 4 to 14 rows of pins; at row i the ball goes right when binary digit i of the number is 1 (digit 0 skipped), or when its remainder after division by the i-th odd prime is in the upper half, or when it is a nonzero square after that division; a coin is the comparison. The balls are the primes, the odd numbers, the numbers without the factors 2 and 3, all numbers, or the Mersenne exponents. The page measures the mean bin against the middle, the share of balls in odd bins, and the shape against the binomial bell (chi-square).

Backtest with 10 rows ([ref7.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/galton/ref7.py) independent of the page, [pagerun7.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/galton/pagerun7.js) runs the page's code; all 30 boards to 5,000 and to 10^6 agree bin by bin; [analysis7.json](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/galton/analysis7.json) → [analysis7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/galton/analysis7.txt)):
- The primes fall like a fair coin on the remainder board (mean 5.002 at 10^6), as Dirichlet's theorem says; every set that contains multiples leans left (mean 4.467 for all numbers, z = −337), because remainder 0 counts as the lower half: the sieve.
- The binary board looked lopsided for every set at 5,000 (mean 4.78), and that was an edge effect: the block from 4,096 to 5,000 is incomplete. At 10^6 all sets are fair. A warning against reading a run that stops inside a block.
- The square-remainder board is fair in the mean for the primes (4.994, z = −1.0) but not in shape at 10^6 (p = 5·10^−8): the last two bins are short (−5.3 and −4.3 standard errors; bin 10 holds 39 primes against 76.6 for a coin). The deficit is not special to primes ([qr_nonsquares.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/galton/qr_nonsquares.py) → [qr_nonsquares.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/galton/qr_nonsquares.txt)): of the 305,709 numbers up to 10^6 without the factors 3 to 31, 475 are nonzero squares modulo all ten primes (a coin gives 298.5), but 319 of them are real squares and only 156 are not (0.52 of the coin, the primes 0.51); bin 9 is short for these numbers too (2,545 against 2,985). Only 3 of the 156 are Lehmer pseudosquares (which must also be ≡ 1 mod 8): 515,761, 878,641, 979,969. So far below the period 3 · 5 · … · 31 ≈ 10^11 the ten tests are not independent for non-squares; we have no proof of the size of the effect (computed, cause open). At 5,000 the shortfall shows already but more weakly (bins 8, 9, 10 hold 15, 0, 0 against 28.9, 6.4, 0.6; p = 0.016).

## 26. Prime turtle ([prime8.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime8.html); computed; the explanation by the small primes is a tested heuristic, the point against Cramér's model is Granville's)

A turtle walks one step for each number 1, 2, 3, … and turns at the numbers a rule picks: left at every prime; left at random with chance 1/ln n (Cramér's fake primes, fixed seed); left at primes ≡ 1 and right at primes ≡ 3 (mod 4) (its heading is the race of section 24 modulo 4); left at last digits 1, 9 and right at 3, 7; left at lower twin primes and right at other primes; at every number by the parity of Ω(n) (the heading is Liouville's sum modulo 4); left at the Mersenne exponents. [ref8.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/ref8.py) and [pagerun8.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/pagerun8.js) agree on the end position, the turns and the farthest distance of all six fixed rules, up to 5,000 and up to 10^6.

Does the prime turtle walk like chance? ([msd.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/msd.py) → [msd_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/msd_5000.txt), [msd_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/msd_1e6.txt); [wheelmodel.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/wheelmodel.py) → [wheelmodel.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/wheelmodel.txt); [gapcorr.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/gapcorr.py) → [gapcorr.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/turtle/gapcorr.txt).) Diffusion, the mean squared distance covered in w steps divided by w, at w = 1,000 up to 10^6: primes 8.67; Cramér's fakes 11.83 (20 runs, spread 0.40); odd fakes with chance 2/ln n 10.93 (spread 0.23). Up to 5,000 the order is the same for short windows (w = 30: 4.55 against 6.84 and 6.06, 5.5 and 4.3 spreads).

Why (heuristic, then tested). Between primes the turtle walks straight, and the heading turns a quarter at each prime, so over many primes it moves like a walk whose steps are the gaps; its diffusion is then about Var(g)/E[g]. Measured: primes 8.30 (diffusion 8.67), odd fakes 10.91 (10.93), Cramér 11.98 (11.83). Prime gaps are more regular than random ones: E[g²]/E[g]² = 1.651 for the primes from 1,000 to 10^6, against 1.86 to 1.94 for the fakes (2 for exponential gaps; Gallagher's theorem, under Hardy–Littlewood, gives Poisson counts in short intervals, which suggests this limit). The gaps of the primes are also negatively correlated at every lag from 1 to 6 (−0.057 at lag 1, −0.025 at lag 2, gaps scaled by ln p), which the odd fakes are not (|r| ≤ 0.002; the other fakes were not measured). For the displacement Σ g_k i^k over K gaps, E|Z|²/K = Var(g) + 2 Σ_l Cov(g_0, g_l) cos(πl/2) (derived; stationary gaps): lag 1 has weight 0 and lag 2 weight −1, so the negative lag-2 correlation raises the diffusion. With the covariances of the raw gaps up to lag 8 the estimate becomes 8.45 instead of 8.30, against 8.67 measured; the remaining 3 % we did not explain.

The prediction written before the run: fakes that avoid the multiples of the small primes come closer to the primes. They do, step by step (diffusion at w = 1,000; 8 runs each): no wheel 11.68, without 2: 10.88, without 2, 3: 9.90, without 2, 3, 5: 9.47, without 2 to 7: 9.23, without 2 to 13: 8.95; the primes 8.67. So the turtle's short walk is mostly the work of the small primes (the wheel up to 13 still walks 0.28 further than the primes, several standard errors); this agrees with Granville's remark (1995) that Cramér's model misses the structure the small primes impose.

## 27. Prime waterfall ([prime9.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime9.html); computed; Gilbreath's conjecture itself is open)

Gilbreath's waterfall: the primes in a row, below it the absolute differences, below that their differences, and so on. Gilbreath (1958) noticed that every row after the first starts with 1; Proth had claimed a proof in 1878 that was wrong, and Odlyzko (1993) checked the first 3.4 × 10^11 primes. A row whose first entry is 1 and whose other entries are all 0 or 2 forces the same shape on every later row (|0 − 1| = |2 − 1| = 1, and the difference of two numbers from {0, 2} is 0 or 2; proved), so for a finite top row the question stops at the first such row, the depth. The page draws the waterfall for the primes, for fakes and for the Mersenne exponents. [ref9.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/ref9.py) and [pagerun9.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/pagerun9.js) agree on the depth, the first column and the number of terms for the primes up to 1,000, 5,000, 10^5 and 10^6 (depths 15, 23, 65, 95; the rule holds) and for the 33 Mersenne exponents up to 10^6 (the first column reads 2, 1, 1, 1, 3: broken in row 4). The reference also reproduces OEIS A036277 for rows 0 to 51.

Is the rule about the primes? Fakes that are random from 3 on break it often ([gilb.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb.py) → [gilb_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb_5000.txt), 200 runs each up to 5,000): all 200 when even numbers may be taken, 70 when only odd numbers are taken, 24, 12 and 0 when also the multiples of 3, of 3 and 5, of 3, 5 and 7 are avoided. Fakes that copy the true primes up to 100 and then take each n > 100 that avoids the multiples of the listed primes with chance ∏ p/(p − 1) / ln n break it rarely. [gilb3.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb3.py) → [gilb3_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb3_5000.txt), [gilb3_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb3_1e6.txt): none of the 1,500 up to 5,000 (300 per model) and none of the 500 up to 10^6 (100 per model). [gilb2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb2.py) → [gilb2_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb2_5000.txt), [gilb2_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb2_1e6.txt), with other seeds: 1 break in 20 odd fakes up to 10^6 (0 with 3 and 5 also avoided); copying only the primes up to 10 gives 76 breaks in 300 odd fakes up to 5,000 and 7 in 20 up to 10^6, copying the primes up to 1,000 gives none. The page's own comparison (other random numbers) found 2 breaks in 100 odd fakes up to 10^5 and none in 100 fakes that avoid 2, 3, 5, 7. This agrees with Odlyzko's view that the property holds for any sequence that starts with 2, continues with odd numbers and has gaps that are small and random enough: the risk lies at the start, where the gaps are of the size of the numbers.

The depth (computed). Up to 5,000 the primes reach depth 23, the odd fakes have median 36.5 (10 % to 90 %: 28 to 49) and only 1 % of them are as shallow; up to 10^6 the primes reach 95, the odd fakes median 115.5 (103 to 143), with 1 run in 100 at or below. Against fakes sieved more deeply the primes are not special ([gilb4.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb4.py) → [gilb4_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb4_5000.txt), [gilb4_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/waterfall/gilb4_1e6.txt); 100 runs per model up to 10^6): fakes without prime factors up to B = 7, 13, 47, 100 have median depth 107.5, 104, 98, 97 and are at or below the primes in 16 %, 23 %, 42 %, 46 % of the runs. The depth follows the largest gap: the primes' largest gap below 10^6 is 114, the odd fakes' median 141 (none of 100 as small), and among the sieved fakes whose largest gap is at most 114, 33 % (B = 7), 38 % (13), 72 % (47) and 67 % (100) are at or below the primes' depth. (Up to 5,000 the model B = 100 is almost the primes themselves: its chance is 1 below about e^8.3 ≈ 4,000.) So the shallow waterfall is a sign of the sieve, which keeps the largest gaps small, not a property of the primes beyond it (computed).

## 28. Goldbach comet ([prime10.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime10.html); the bands are Hardy–Littlewood's prediction (literature, conjecture), the width is computed)

The page counts for every even n up to 200,000 the sums n = p + q of two primes and plots the counts: Goldbach's comet. Colours mark the multiples of 3 and 5 (or 7); the view can divide by the factor of the small primes S(n) = ∏ (p − 1)/(p − 2) over the odd primes p | n, or by the whole prediction of Hardy and Littlewood (1923) for the ordered count, r(n) ≈ 2 C₂ S(n) ∫₂^{n−2} dt / (ln t · ln(n − t)). Clicking a point lists its sums. [ref10.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/ref10.py) (direct counting) and [pagerun10.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/pagerun10.js) (the page's own code) agree on the sum of r(n), the n with a single sum (4, 6, 8, 12), the fewest sums from X/2 on, the five band ratios and the width, for X = 5,000, 20,000 and 200,000 ([compare10.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/compare10.txt)).

The bands ([comet.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/comet.py) → [comet_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/comet_5000.txt), [comet_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/comet_1e6.txt); counts by a Fourier product, checked by direct counting at 33 n). Prediction: a factor (p − 1)/(p − 2) between multiples of p and the rest. Measured for even n in (X/2, X], mean of r(n) / (n / ln² n): for p = 3, 5, 7, 11, 13 the ratios are 1.985, 1.326, 1.198, 1.106, 1.084 up to 5,000 and 2.000, 1.333, 1.200, 1.111, 1.091 up to 10^6 (predicted 2, 1.333, 1.2, 1.111, 1.091). The whole prediction at single points: r(10^5) = 1,620 against 1,620.7 (0.9996) and r(10^6) = 10,804 against 10,834.2 (0.9972). The integral is taken by the trapezoid rule in ln t on [ln 2, ln(n/2)], doubled; the plain trapezoid in t with 4,001 points overestimates it by 0.3 % (it gave 1,624.0 and 10,871.1), checked against scipy's quad in [hl_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/hl_check.py) → [hl_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/hl_check.txt). Every even n from 4 to 10^6 has a sum (checked to 4 · 10^18 by Oliveira e Silva, Herzog and Pardi 2014).

The width (computed; [subpoisson4.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subpoisson4.py) → [subp4_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subp4_1e6.txt), [subp4_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subp4_5000.txt)). Statistic: the standard deviation over all even n in [X/2, X] of r(n)/E(n), divided by a least-squares quadratic in n, with E = the Hardy–Littlewood prediction for the primes and the exact expectation of the model for fakes (true primes up to max(B, 100), then numbers without prime factors up to B, each with chance ∏_{p ≤ B} p/(p − 1) / ln n). Up to 10^6: primes 0.00801; fakes with B = 2: 0.01236, B = 13: 0.01178, B = 31: 0.01193, B = 100: 0.00978 (4 runs each, spreads 0.00004 to 0.0005). So the comet of the primes is about 35 % narrower than that of Cramér-type fakes (B = 2), and sieving the fakes closes part of the gap (ratio 0.82 at B = 100). Random sets also fill the classes mod small q unevenly, which moves r(n) for whole classes of n. Removing the class means of the residual mod every odd prime up to 97 gives: primes 0.00748; fakes B = 2: 0.01233, B = 13: 0.01060, B = 31: 0.01039, B = 100: 0.00859 (ratio 0.87). With that removal the ratio rises steadily with B, as predicted before the first run; without it the model B = 13 varies from run to run (0.0118 here, 0.0129 in an earlier set of 4 runs with another seed, [subpoisson3.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subpoisson3.py) → [subp3_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subp3_1e6.txt), which used 4,000 sampled n). Up to 5,000 (30 runs): primes 0.0571, B = 2: 0.0865, B = 13: 0.0725; larger B are nearly deterministic below 5,000. The rest, primes about 13 % narrower than fakes sieved to 100 once the classes are evened out, we have not explained. Background (literature): the primes spread over residue classes more evenly than random weights (Montgomery and Hooley: the variance over the classes mod q is about x log q, against x log x for random weights), and their counts in short intervals have the smaller variance h log(N/h) instead of Cramér's h log N (Montgomery and Soundararajan 2004); for Goldbach sums see Granville (2007) and Goldston and Suriajaya (arXiv:2110.14250). We found no source that measures the width of the comet against random models.
