A side lesson ([subpoisson2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subpoisson2.py) → [subp2_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subp2_5000.txt), [subp2_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/comet/subp2_1e6.txt)): the spread over n of one realization is 0.78 to 0.94 of the model's variance formula, because that formula includes the fluctuation of the total number of fakes, which is common to all n; so fakes must be compared by measurement, not by formula.

## 29. Prime radio ([prime11.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime11.html); the stations are classical (Ramanujan sums, Vinogradov's major arcs), the noise levels are computed)

The page adds a wave e(pθ) = e^{2πipθ} for every prime p ≤ X and shows the power |F(θ)|² / N (N = π(X)) for θ from 0 to 1/2, by a fast Fourier transform of length L, the least power of 2 ≥ 2(X + 1). At θ = a/q the sum depends only on how the primes fall into the remainders mod q: F(a/q) = Σ_r π(X; q, r) e(ar/q) + (primes dividing q), so by Dirichlet's theorem |F(a/q)| ≈ N |μ(q)| / φ(q) (the Ramanujan sum c_q(a) = μ(q) for gcd(a, q) = 1). The table on the page gives this exactly from the residue counts. At q with a square factor the station is silent, apart from the prime race: at q = 4, |F(1/4)| = √((π(X; 4, 3) − π(X; 4, 1))² + 1) (the 1 is the prime 2), at 10^6 that is √(147² + 1), strength 0.001873 (147 is the lead of 39,322 over 39,175 that prime6 and the turtle of section 26 show). The strength of a station is the mean over a coprime to q of |F(a/q)| / N. Other signals: odd fake primes, fakes avoiding 2 to 13, the numbers without 2, 3, 5. [ref11.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/ref11.py) and [pagerun11.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/pagerun11.js) agree exactly (to 10^−6) on the twelve station strengths and the two noise levels up to 5,000, 10^5 and 10^6 ([compare11.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/compare11.txt)).

The noise between the stations (computed). Noise level: the mean of |F_k|² / N over the bins farther than w from every a/q with q ≤ Q (w = 20 bins at X = 10^6, 3 at X = 5,000). Parseval: Σ_k |F_k|² = L N, so power in the stations is missing from the noise. [spectrum.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/spectrum.py) → [spec_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/spec_1e6.txt), [spec_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/spec_5000.txt): up to 10^6 with Q = 50: primes 0.597, odd fakes 0.844, fakes avoiding the primes up to 13: 0.629, up to 100: 0.594 (random signals of density δ give about 1 − δ). [spectrum2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/spectrum2.py) → [spec2_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/spec2_1e6.txt) removes the stations up to Q = 400 (w = 2 beyond q = 50): the primes drop to 0.470, the odd fakes stay at 0.844, the fakes avoiding 2 to 13 reach only 0.603 and those avoiding the primes up to 100 0.486. Before this run we predicted that the primes would drop below the fakes avoiding 2 to 13 as Q grows; they do (ratio 1.001, 0.947, 0.858, 0.780 for Q = 12, 50, 150, 400). The primes have stations at every square-free q up to large q, because their sieve uses every prime up to √X; fakes have stations only at q built from the primes they avoid. Up to 5,000 (Q = 12): primes 0.568, odd fakes 0.764.

The noise levels follow from the classical size of the stations: the stations a/q (gcd(a, q) = 1) carry about (μ(q)²/φ(q)) Σ_{n ≤ X} 1/ln² n of the total power N, so the noise is about (1 − Σ_{q ≤ Q} μ(q)²/φ(q) · Σ 1/ln² n / N) divided by the share of bins kept: 0.592, 0.511, 0.480 for Q = 50, 150, 400 against 0.597, 0.523, 0.470 measured ([noise_formula.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/noise_formula.py) → [noise_formula.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/noise_formula.txt); heuristic).

The link with section 28 (heuristic, partly failed). Hardy and Littlewood's singular series is the sum over all stations (for even n, 2 C₂ S(n) = Σ_q μ(q)² c_q(n) / φ(q)², a classical identity, checked for six n with q up to 2 · 10^5 in [singular_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/singular_check.py) → [singular_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/singular_check.txt)), so the prediction removes the stations from the Goldbach count and leaves the noise, which for the primes is lower than for odd fakes. The prediction written in spectrum.py, that the width ratio of section 28 follows the noise ratio for the sieved fakes too, failed: at Q = 50 the noise ratios primes/fakes are 0.949 (B = 13) and 1.005 (B = 100), the width ratios 0.68 and 0.82; at Q = 400 the fakes sieved to 100 have nearly the primes' noise (ratio 0.969) but a comet about 20 % wider. So the noise level explains the gap to odd fakes in part, not the rest; the uneven filling of residue classes by random sets (section 28) is another part.

## 30. Prime rain ([prime12.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime12.html); Mertens and Buchstab are literature, the finite-x behaviour is computed)

Every prime p is a cloud that wets its multiples; after the clouds up to y the dry numbers in [2, x] are those without a prime factor ≤ y, Φ(x, y) − 1 of them. The forecast of independence is x ∏_{p ≤ y} (1 − 1/p). The page draws the numbers (x ≤ 2,000) or a bar, adds clouds one by one, and plots dry / forecast against y together with e^γ ω(u), u = ln x / ln y, where ω is Buchstab's function (ω(u) = 1/u on [1, 2], (u ω(u))′ = ω(u − 1) beyond; computed by the trapezoid rule with step 10^−3 on the page, 10^−4 in the program). Since Φ(x, y) ≈ x ω(u) / ln y for y → ∞ with u > 1 fixed (Buchstab 1937; de Bruijn 1950 made it uniform for large y; for small fixed y, Φ(x, y) ~ x ∏ (1 − 1/p) instead) and ∏ (1 − 1/p) ≈ e^{−γ} / ln y (Mertens' third theorem), the ratio tends to e^γ ω(u): 1 for small clouds, e^γ/2 = 0.8905 at y = √x, a first maximum above 1 near u = 2.76 (ω = 0.56714 against e^{−γ} = 0.56146). [ref12.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/ref12.py) and [pagerun12.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/pagerun12.js) agree on every cloud, dry count and forecast up to 5,000 (669 clouds) and 10^6 (78,498 clouds) ([compare12.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/compare12.txt)).

Backtest ([rain.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/rain.py) → [rain_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/rain_5000.txt), [rain_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/rain_1e6.txt)). Up to 10^6: for y ≤ 31 the forecast is right to 10^−4 (152,839 dry against 152,852 at y = 31); at y = 101 (u = 2.99) there are more dry numbers than forecast, 119,563 against 119,126 (1.0037; e^γ ω(u) = 1.0055), as predicted before the run; the dip is deepest at y = 653, 0.9441 (81,175 dry against 85,982.1, and within 0.0002 of that for y from 619 to 677; found by a scan of every cloud in ref12_1e6.json, not only the tabled ones), and at y = 997, the largest prime ≤ √x, 78,330 dry (the primes in (997, 10^6]) against 80,965 forecast (0.9675, against the limit 0.8905). The integral form of Buchstab's count, ∫_y^x ω(ln t / ln y) / ln y dt, gives 0.9689 there and 0.9481 at y = 701 (measured 0.9447). For u ≤ 2 this integral is exactly li(x) − li(y), so near √x the gap to the limit is the prime number theorem's second-order term (li(x) against x / ln x), not a sieve effect. Away from √x the integral is no better than the limit (y = 101: 1.0266 against 1.0037 measured and 1.0055 for the limit; y = 251: 0.9938 against 0.9807 and 1.0013), because it uses e^{−γ}/ln y in place of the true product (∏ (1 − 1/p) · e^γ ln y = 0.617, 0.979, 0.988, 0.996 at y = 2, 101, 251, 997). Up to 5,000 the effects are blurred: the overshoot does not show (0.9971 at y = 17), the dip is deepest at y = 43 (0.9766; 0.9776 at y = 47), and at y = 67 ≤ √5,000 the ratio is 1.0029 (Buchstab integral 1.0221). Lesson: the famous constant e^γ/2 (the forecast at √x is 2e^{−γ} = 1.123 times too high in the limit) is far from visible at 10^6, where the excess is 3.4 %; near √x most of the difference is li(x) against x / ln x (computed; the limit statements are literature).

## 31. Prime dance ([prime13.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime13.html); the bias is literature, the fake-prime test is computed)

A dancer steps to the remainder of each prime in turn (mod 10: the last digit; also mod 3, 4, 5, 8, 12); the page draws the steps between the spots with a width that grows with their share, and a 4 × 4 table. Sources: the primes or fakes (true primes up to 100, then numbers avoiding the multiples of W with chance ∏_{p ∈ W} p/(p − 1) / ln n, fixed seed) for W = {2, 5}, {2, 3, 5}, {2, …, 13}. [ref13.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/ref13.py) and [pagerun13.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/pagerun13.js) agree on every transition count of the primes for q = 3, 4, 5, 8, 10, 12 (consecutive primes both > q) up to 5,000, 10^6 and 10^7 ([compare13.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/compare13.txt)). (Mod 5 and mod 10 give the same transitions apart from the step 7 → 11, which only mod 5 counts: an odd number's last digit is fixed by its remainder mod 5.)

Backtest ([dance.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/dance.py) → [dance_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/dance_5000.txt), [dance_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/dance_1e6.txt), [dance_1e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/dance_1e7.txt); last digits, primes above 100). Repeats would be 1/4 for independent digits. Primes: 0.1135 up to 5,000, 0.1536 up to 10^6, 0.1630 up to 10^7: the dislike of repeats of Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan (2016), fading slowly. Prediction written before the run: fakes avoiding 2 and 5 show most of the deficit, and avoiding 3 brings them closer. Up to 10^6 (10 runs each): W = {2, 5}: 0.1744 (spread 0.0012), 78 % of the deficit (0.0756 of 0.0964); W = {2, 3, 5}: 0.1544 (0.0014), about 2 standard errors of the mean above the primes; W = {2, …, 7}, {2, …, 13}, {2, …, 97}: 0.1508, 0.1508, 0.1510, about 5 to 11 standard errors below; the largest cell differences are 0.0027 for {2, 3, 5} and 0.0014 to 0.0020 for the deeper wheels. Up to 10^7 (3 runs each): {2, 5}: 0.1861, {2, 3, 5}: 0.1668, {2, …, 7}: 0.1627, {2, …, 13}: 0.1628, {2, …, 97}: 0.1626 (largest cell differences 0.0131, 0.0022, 0.0008, 0.0006, 0.0004). So the wheel that matches moves with x: {2, 3, 5} crosses the primes near 10^6, the deeper wheels match at 10^7. Between 10^6 and 10^7 the deficit of the primes shrinks by a factor 0.90 (0.0964 to 0.0870), that of the wheel {2, …, 13} by 0.88 (0.0992 to 0.0872): the bias of the primes fades more slowly than that of a random model with a fixed wheel (Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's leading term is of order (ln ln x)/ln x, a fixed wheel gives about 1/ln x; heuristic). Up to 5,000 (200 runs) the spread is about 0.011 and the random models agree with the primes; the model {2, …, 97} is nearly the primes themselves there (its chance is at least 0.98 below 5,000). Conclusion (computed): at these sizes most of the deficit is reproduced by random numbers that respect the sieve of the small primes, 2, 3, 5 and 7 and more, but no fixed wheel follows how slowly the bias fades. Literature: Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan (PNAS 113, 2016) proposed a conjectural explanation from a modified Hardy–Littlewood conjecture; their leading term comes from an average of singular series over all primes, and they note that the effect of small gaps alone is of smaller order. Holt (arXiv:2405.03540, 2024) attributes the biases to the evolution of the gaps in Eratosthenes' sieve (small gaps appear early and are spread unevenly over the residue classes) and expects them to be transient.

## 32. Prime census ([prime14.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime14.html); Erdős–Kac, Landau, Sathe–Selberg and the second-order variance are literature; the counts are computed)

The page counts ω(n), the number of different primes dividing n, for every n ≤ x (x up to 10^7), as bars that grow during the count, and draws three predictions: Erdős–Kac (a normal law with mean and variance ln ln x, counts from [k − ½, k + ½]); Sathe–Selberg, (x / ln x) (ln ln x)^{k−1} / (k − 1)! · G((k − 1) / ln ln x) with G(z) = ∏_p (1 + z/(p − 1)) (1 − 1/p)^z / Γ(z + 1) (product over the primes up to max(x, 10^6)); and a normal law with the mean of the census and a variance with a pair term (below). [ref14.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/ref14.py) and [pagerun14.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/pagerun14.js) agree on every bar, the mean, the variance, the pair sum and the four distances up to 5,000, 10^6 and 10^7 ([compare14.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/compare14.txt)).

The mean is exact: Σ_{n ≤ x} ω(n) = Σ_{p ≤ x} ⌊x/p⌋ (proved: count the pairs p | n). The variance: Σ_{n ≤ x} ω(n)² = Σ_{p ≤ x} ⌊x/p⌋ + Σ_{p ≠ q, pq ≤ x} ⌊x/(pq)⌋ exactly (the second-moment identity behind Turán's proof, Hardy and Wright §22.11; it reproduces the measured variances exactly). Dropping the floors gives Var ω ≈ Σ_{p ≤ x} (1/p)(1 − 1/p) − Σ_{p ≠ q, pq > x} 1/(pq): two different primes with pq > x cannot both divide n ≤ x. The pair sum tends to ∫₀¹ −ln(1 − u)/u du = π²/6 (proved for the limit: with p = x^u, q = x^v the sum becomes ∫∫_{u + v > 1} du dv /(uv)); it is 1.285 at x = 5,000, 1.435 at 10^6, 1.467 at 10^7. This is known: Var ω = ln ln x + B₁ − Σ_p 1/p² − ζ(2) + o(1) = ln ln x − 1.836 + o(1) (Diaconis, Mosteller and Onishi 1977 treat these second-order terms; the pair sum's limit ζ(2) follows from Saidak 2002, as quoted by Das, Kuo and Liu, arXiv:2409.10430). The page shows how far that constant is from negligible at computable x.

Backtest ([census.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census.py) → [census_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census_5000.txt), [census_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census_1e6.txt), [census_1e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census_1e7.txt); [census2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census2.py) → [census2_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census2_5000.txt), [census2_1000000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census2_1000000.txt), [census2_10000000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census2_10000000.txt)). Total variation distance (half the sum of |measured − predicted| over k = 0..11, divided by x; taken from the reference ref14_*.json, census.py sums only over the k that occur and gives slightly smaller values): up to 5,000 / 10^6 / 10^7, Erdős–Kac 0.278 / 0.230 / 0.217, Poisson (Landau, (x / ln x)(ln ln x)^{k−1}/(k − 1)!) 0.293 / 0.235 / 0.227, Sathe–Selberg 0.165 / 0.120 / 0.115, the normal law with the pair term 0.022 / 0.015 / 0.017. Measured variance 0.655 / 0.981 / 1.103 against the heuristic 0.667 / 1.000 / 1.122 and ln ln x = 2.142 / 2.626 / 2.780. Prediction written before the run: the normal law with the pair term beats Erdős–Kac by far; it does, by a factor of 10 to 15 in distance. Both corrections are needed ([census_parts.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/census_parts.txt)): the right variance with the mean ln ln x gives distances 0.090 / 0.082 / 0.075, the right mean with the variance ln ln x 0.273 / 0.223 / 0.216, both together 0.022 / 0.015 / 0.017. At 10^7 the variance falls short of ln ln x by 1.68: the pair term 1.47 and 0.19 from Σ (1/p)(1 − 1/p) against ln ln x; the pair term is a constant against a slowly growing ln ln x, but at computable x it is about half of it. Sathe–Selberg is better than Erdős–Kac but still at a distance of 0.115: its total mass is only 0.89 to 0.91 of x, and bar by bar it is off by 7 to 23 % for k = 1 to 4 at 10^7 (its relative error is of order k/(ln ln x)²). The smallest n with ω(n) = k are the primorials 2, 6, 30, 210, 2310, 30030, 510510, 9699690.

## 33. Prime carousel ([prime15.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime15.html); Artin's conjecture with Heilbronn's correction is literature, proved by Hooley under GRH; the counts are computed)

For a prime p the page puts the seats 1 … p − 1 on a ring and rides x → a x mod p from seat 1, drawing a chord for every hop; the ride visits every seat exactly when a is a primitive root of p (base 10: the period of the decimals of 1/p). It counts the primes p ≤ X (p ∤ a) with a full ride and plots the running share against the prediction: Artin's constant A = ∏_q (1 − 1/(q(q − 1))) = 0.3739558… for a square-free base a ≠ −1 with a ≢ 1 (mod 4), and A (1 − μ(|a|) ∏_{q | a} 1/(q² − q − 1)) for a ≡ 1 (mod 4) (Heilbronn's correction; Hooley 1967 proved these densities under the generalised Riemann hypothesis); a square base (4) never gives a full ride (for p = 3, 4 ≡ 1). The product over the primes below 10^7, 0.3739558158, agrees with A = 0.3739558136… to 8 digits (the truncation error is about A/(Q ln Q) ≈ 2 · 10^−9; [artin.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/artin.py)). [ref15.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/ref15.py) (sympy's is_primitive_root) and [pagerun15.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/pagerun15.js) agree on every count for the bases 2, 3, 5, 6, 7, 10, 13, 21, −3, 4 up to 5,000, 10^6 and 10^7 ([compare15.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/compare15.txt)).

Backtest ([artin.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/artin.py) → [artin_5000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/artin_5000.txt), [artin_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/artin_1e6.txt); [ref15.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/ref15.py) → [ref15_1e7.json](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/ref15_1e7.json)). Predictions written before the run: 5 near 0.3936, −3 near 0.4488, 21 near 0.3721 (21 ≡ 1 mod 4 with μ(21) = +1, so slightly below A), the others near 0.3740. Measured up to 10^6 (78,497 primes; 78,496 for the bases 6, 10, 21, which exclude a second prime) / 10^7 (664,578; 664,577): 2: 0.37378 / 0.37391; 3: 0.37445 / 0.37411; 5: 0.39345 / 0.39394; 6: 0.37388 / 0.37391; 7: 0.37497 / 0.37427; 10: 0.37582 / 0.37450; 13: 0.37674 / 0.37647 (predicted 0.37637); 21: 0.37193 / 0.37213 (predicted 0.37213); −3: 0.45000 / 0.44894 (predicted 0.44875). Every difference is within 1.1 binomial standard deviations at 10^6 and within 1 at 10^7 (one standard deviation is about 0.0017 and 0.0006; the counts are deterministic, so the binomial spread is a yardstick, not a test). Up to 5,000 (668 primes, one standard deviation 0.019) the shares scatter by up to 1.4 standard deviations. So the corrections for 5 and −3 are visible at every size (against plain A: +11 and +44 standard deviations at 10^6); those for 13 and 21 only at 10^7 (+4.2 and −3.1 against plain A; at 10^6 +1.6 and −1.2). −3 is a primitive root for 45 % of the primes (computed; the densities are conditional theorems).

## 34. Prime baskets ([prime16.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime16.html); the variance formula is Montgomery and Soundararajan's, conditional on a uniform Hardy–Littlewood conjecture; the measurements are computed)

The page drops baskets (n, n + h] at random n in [N/2, N] (N = 10^5 or 10^6, h from 10 to 10,000), draws the last basket with its primes and the histogram of the counts against a Poisson count with the same mean (Cramér's model) and against a bell with the measured spread. For every n in [N/2, N] it also computes exactly: the variance of π(n + h) − π(n) − (li(n + h) − li(n)) divided by the mean (li(m) = Σ_{2 ≤ t ≤ m} 1/ln t), and the variance of ψ(n + h) − ψ(n) − h with von Mangoldt weights. Sources: the primes or fakes (true primes up to max(W, 100), then numbers without prime factors in W, chance ∏ p/(p − 1) / ln n) for W = {2}, {2, …, 13}, the primes up to 100. [ref16.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/ref16.py) and [pagerun16.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/pagerun16.js) agree on all four quantities for 7 widths at N = 10^5 and 10^6 ([compare16.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/compare16.txt)).

The prediction (literature, and a heuristic reading). Montgomery and Soundararajan (Comm. Math. Phys. 252, 2004, Theorem 3, eq. (21) with K = 2) show, assuming the Hardy–Littlewood conjecture with a square-root error term uniformly (hypothesis (20), which includes the Riemann hypothesis), that Σ_{n ≤ N} (ψ(n + h) − ψ(n) − h)² = h ∫_1^N (ln(x/h) + B) dx + error, B = 1 − γ − ln 2π = −1.4151, for ln N ≤ h ≤ √N; they conjecture it up to N^{1 − δ}. Read locally (a heuristic), the variance at x is h (ln(x/h) + B), against h (ln x − 1) for Cramér's model (Bernoulli with chance 1/ln n, weight ln n). The error term of the theorem is not small at these sizes, so the comparison below is an empirical test of the asymptotic formula, not of the theorem. For unweighted counts the Hardy–Littlewood singular-series average R₂(h) = −h ln h + (B + 1) h gives variance / mean ≈ 1 − (ln h − B) / ln x (heuristic); Cramér gives 1 − 1/ln x.

Backtest ([baskets.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/baskets.py) → [baskets_1e5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/baskets_1e5.txt), [baskets_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/baskets_1e6.txt), [baskets_1e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/baskets_1e7.txt); windows n in [N/2, N]). Weighted variance / formula for h = 10, 30, 100, 300, 1,000: 1.003, 0.981, 1.016, 1.037, 0.999 at N = 10^6 and 1.004, 0.990, 1.002, 0.990, 0.963 at N = 10^7 (h = 10 is below the theorem's lower limit ln N); at N = 10^7 the measured variance falls below the formula from h = 1,000 on, inside the proved range (0.963, 0.924 at h = 3,000, 0.879 at 10^4), a shortfall we have not explained. Against Cramér's h ln n the variances are 0.727 (h = 10) down to 0.383 (h = 1,000) at N = 10^6. Unweighted variance / mean at N = 10^7: 0.768, 0.689, 0.620, 0.544, 0.456, 0.373 for h = 10 … 3,000, against the heuristic 0.765, 0.695, 0.619, 0.550, 0.474, 0.404.

Fakes (true primes up to max(W, 100), then numbers without prime factors in W, chance c_W / ln n, c_W = ∏ p/(p − 1); one or two runs each). Prediction written before the run: W = {2} stays near 1 and the ratio comes down as W grows. Detrended by li like the primes (the fair comparison), at N = 10^7 for h = 10, 30, 100, 300, 1,000, 3,000: W = {2}: 0.876, 0.881, 0.884, 0.883, 0.890, 0.905 (that is 1 − 2/ln n, not 1; the prediction "near 1" failed); W = {2, …, 13}: 0.778, 0.719, 0.695, 0.677, 0.659, 0.634; W = primes ≤ 100: 0.769, 0.692, 0.634, 0.585, 0.544, 0.518; primes 0.768, 0.689, 0.620, 0.544, 0.456, 0.373. So a fixed sieve also gives a falling curve, and follows the primes up to widths of about the size of its largest prime; beyond that it flattens, while the primes keep falling: they behave like a sieve whose limit grows with the numbers, which is what the singular-series average (a product of local factors over all primes) expresses (computed; the explanation is heuristic). Detrended by their own exact expectation instead, the fakes give 1 − c_W / ln n for every h (0.852, 0.613, 0.385 at 10^6), because that removes the sieve's own regular pattern; that flat curve cannot be compared with the primes.

## 35. Prime bricks ([prime17.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime17.html); Dickman, de Bruijn and Saias are literature; the comparison is computed)

Every number is a tower of prime bricks; with bricks up to y only the y-smooth numbers can be built. The page colours the numbers up to 600 (buildable or not), shows the tower of a clicked number, counts Ψ(x, y) for x = 10^5, 10^6 or 10^7 and every y, and plots three predictions divided by the count: Dickman's x ρ(u) (u = ln x / ln y; ρ = 1 on [0, 1], u ρ′(u) = −ρ(u − 1), trapezoid rule with step 10^−4, which gives ρ(2) = 1 − ln 2, ρ(3) = 0.048608, ρ(4) = 0.0049109), the same plus de Bruijn's second-order term (1 − γ) x ρ(u − 1) / ln x (de Bruijn: Ψ − x ρ(u) is asymptotic to this term for (ln x)^{5/8} < ln y < (ln x)/2), and de Bruijn's Λ(x, y) = x ∫ ρ(u − v) d(⌊y^v⌋ / y^v) = x [Σ_{m ≤ x} ρ(u − log_y m)/m − ∫_1^x ρ(u − log_y t) ⌊t⌋/t² dt], which Saias (1989) showed to equal Ψ(x, y) up to a factor 1 + O(exp(−(ln y)^{3/5 − ε})) for y ≥ exp((ln ln x)^{5/3 + ε}). [ref17.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/ref17.py) (it builds every smooth number by multiplying primes, no sieve) and [pagerun17.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/pagerun17.js) agree on Ψ, x ρ(u) and Λ for six (x, y) pairs up to 10^7 ([compare17.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/compare17.txt)).

A lesson on the integral: the plain Simpson rule on each [m, m + 1] gives Λ = 0.995 x instead of x when ρ is replaced by 1 (the error sits at small t, where 1/t² bends fast); Gauss–Legendre with 6 nodes gives x to 10^−6, and a separate Stieltjes sum on a grid of 2 · 10^7 points in v agrees ([lambda_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/lambda_check.py) → [lambda_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/lambda_check.txt): x = 10^6, 64,169.9, 340,466.1, 627,614.8 for y = 100, 1,000, 10,000, against 64,169.9, 340,466.0, 627,614.8).

Backtest ([smooth.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/smooth.py) → [smooth_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/smooth_1e6.txt), [smooth_1e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/smooth_1e7.txt)). Prediction written before the run: Λ within 0.1 %, de Bruijn's term halves the error of ρ, ρ off by 10 % or more at u = 3 to 5. Prediction / count at x = 10^7: u = 3.5 (y = 100): ρ 0.601, with the term 0.728, Λ 0.837; u = 3.0 (y = 215): 0.729, 0.850, 0.915; u = 2.0 (y = 3,162): 0.913, 0.991, 0.997; u = 1.5: 0.954, 0.996, 1.000. At x = 10^6, u = 3 (y = 100): 0.673, 0.803, 0.888. So in every tabled row the order is ρ < term < Λ < count. Of the prediction: Λ is within 0.1 % only at x = 10^7, u = 1.5 (0.9996; at x = 10^6, u = 1.5 it is 0.18 % low), and at u = 3 it is still 9 to 11 % low; de Bruijn's term halves the error of ρ for u ≤ 2.5 but not beyond (it removes 40 to 45 % of it at u = 3 and 32 % at u = 3.5, x = 10^7); ρ is off by 27 to 40 % at u = 3 to 3.5. These are asymptotic results, and most tabled rows lie outside their stated ranges at these x: de Bruijn's term is stated for (ln x)^{5/8} < ln y < (ln x)/2, that is 174 < y < 1,000 at x = 10^6 and 294 < y < 3,162 at x = 10^7, and Saias' estimate for y ≥ exp((ln ln x)^{5/3}), y ≥ 148 and y ≥ 244; so the table is an empirical comparison, not a test of those error terms. For very small y (10 to 30) none of the three is close (Λ / Ψ = 0.11 to 0.54). On the page, for the largest y (u near 1), de Bruijn's term exceeds the count; the saved tables stop at u = 1.5 (computed).

## 36. Spot the primes ([prime18.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime18.html); a game; the detective accuracies are computed)

Two strips show the same stretch [n, n + L) (L = 10^4 or 10^5, n random in [N₀/2, N₀], N₀ = 10^6 or 10^7); one marks the true primes, the other fakes from a forger: odd numbers (clumsy), numbers avoiding 2, 3, 5 (careful), 2 to 13 (skilled) or all primes up to 100 (master), each with chance ∏ p/(p − 1) / ln n. The player guesses; five detectives guess too, each with one statistic and one rule fixed in advance (the primes are the more regular set, so the smaller value is called real): sieve (elements with a prime factor ≤ 31), gaps (E[g²]/E[g]²), repeats (share of repeated last digits), baskets of 100 and of 1,000 (variance of the counts around h / ln n, divided by the mean). [ref18.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/ref18.py) and [pagerun18.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/pagerun18.js) agree on the five statistics of the true primes in six fixed strips ([compare18.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/compare18.txt)).

Backtest ([spot.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot.py) → [spot_1e6_1e4.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot_1e6_1e4.txt), [spot_1e6_1e5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot_1e6_1e5.txt), [spot_1e7_1e5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot_1e7_1e5.txt), [spot_1e7_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot_1e7_1e6.txt) with 200, 100, 100 and 40 rounds; the master forger again with 1,000, 1,000 and 400 rounds: [spot_master_1e6_1e4.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot_master_1e6_1e4.txt), [spot_master_1e6_1e5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot_master_1e6_1e5.txt), [spot_master_1e7_1e5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/spot_master_1e7_1e5.txt)). Accuracy (sieve / gaps / repeats / baskets 100 / baskets 1,000), strips of 10^4 near 10^6: clumsy 1.00 / 0.99 / 0.93 / 0.96 / 0.87; careful 1.00 / 0.90 / 0.51 / 0.91 / 0.79; skilled 1.00 / 0.70 / 0.41 / 0.71 / 0.79. Master forger (1,000 or 400 rounds, standard error about 0.016 or 0.025): strips of 10^4 near 10^6: 0.50 / 0.56 / 0.46 / 0.51 / 0.59; 10^5 near 10^6: 0.50 / 0.58 / 0.40 / 0.55 / 0.84; 10^5 near 10^7: 0.50 / 0.57 / 0.51 / 0.64 / 0.82; and strips of 10^6 near 10^7 (40 rounds): 0.50 / 0.85 / 0.30 / 0.97 / 1.00. (A first set of 100 rounds gave 0.91 for the wide baskets at 10^5 near 10^6, a high draw.) Prediction written before the run: the sieve catches the clumsy and careful forgers at once, gaps and baskets of 100 catch the deeper ones, nothing is perfect against the master at L = 10^4. The first part held; gaps and baskets of 100 catch the skilled forger in about 70 % of the rounds but the master in only 51 to 64 %. The wide-basket detective was added after the prediction, following section 34: it is the best by far against the master (0.82 to 0.84 in strips of 10^5), the others stay below 0.65 though some are above chance. The reason is section 34: a fixed sieve imitates the primes' small spread in baskets only up to widths near its largest prime, while the primes' sieve grows with the numbers; a sieved fake cannot spread less than about 1 − c/ln n (c = ∏_{p ≤ 100} p/(p − 1) = 8.31). The repeat detective's rule points the wrong way against deep sieves near 10^6 (they repeat slightly less than the primes, section 31). The basket statistic is the variance of the residuals c − h/ln n around their own mean, divided by the mean of h/ln n. A first rule, "closer to the value of the primes in [N₀/2, 2N₀]", scored below chance because that value drifts with n; the direction rule replaced it before the runs reported here.

## 37. Prime trains ([prime19.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime19.html); Moser's limit is a theorem; the counts are computed; the smooth model is a heuristic tested against counts)

A train is a run of consecutive primes, the sum of its primes is the number it reaches; f(n) is the number of trains of length n (OEIS A054845; f(41) = 3: 41, 11 + 13 + 17, 2 + 3 + 5 + 7 + 11 + 13). Moser (1963) proved (1/x) Σ_{n ≤ x} f(n) → ln 2. The page draws the trains of a chosen n, finds record numbers, counts the trains up to 10^5, 10^6 or 10^7 (two pointers over the prefix sums) and plots the average against a smooth model. [trains_count.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_count.py) (prefix sums and binary search, primes from a segmented sieve, up to 10^9) and [pagerun19.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/pagerun19.js) agree on the counts at 10^3 to 10^7 ([compare19.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/compare19.txt)).

The average falls ([trains_count.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_count.py) → [trains_count.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_count.txt)): 0.684000 at 10^3, 0.666700, 0.657160, 0.650999, 0.647581, 0.645815, and 0.645089 at 10^9, away from ln 2 = 0.693147. Sorenson and Waiss (Integers 26A (2026) #24, arXiv:2509.00236, 2025) counted f(n) for every n up to 10^14; the means from their Table 1 ([sw_table.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/sw_table.py) → [sw_table.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/sw_table.txt), read from the arXiv HTML, which is not stored here; our reader recovers each column to within 2 · 10^−9 of its total; their columns up to 10^9 give exactly our means) are 0.6450335 at 10^10, 0.6454066 at 10^11, 0.6460456 at 10^12, 0.6468444 at 10^13 and 0.6477353 at 10^14 + 145,300. So the average of the primes' own counts has its lowest decade value at 10^10 and then rises slowly.

Why (heuristic, two ways). (1) A smooth model: replace the m-th prime by q_m = Li^{−1}(m), with Li(T) = ∫_2^T dt/ln t, and count the runs of q's with sum ≤ x ([trains_smooth.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_smooth.py) → [trains_smooth.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_smooth.txt)). Prediction written before the run: within 0.5 % of the primes from 10^4 to 10^7. It is 5.8 %, 2.7 %, 1.25 % and 0.58 % high (with li instead of Li, 6.9 %, 3.0 %, 1.34 %, 0.60 %), so the prediction failed at small x, but the excess halves each decade and the q's show the same fall. A continuous version ([trains_far.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_far.py) → [trains_far.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_far.txt); k up to 2,000 summed, larger k by the trapezoid rule) gives 0.64590 at 10^9 and 0.64542, 0.64560, 0.64614, 0.64689, 0.64776 at 10^10 … 10^14: above the primes' counts by 0.13 % at 10^9 and by 0.061 %, 0.029 %, 0.014 %, 0.0072 %, 0.0037 % at 10^10 … 10^14, so the slow approach is a property of the density of the primes. Beyond 10^14 its numbers lose accuracy (it subtracts sums of order 10^30 in double precision), so we quote none. (2) An asymptotic form (derived by an independent review and consistent with the model): a train of k primes starting near t has sum about k t, the k-th length cuts off where li(x/K) ≈ K/2, and counting N_k ≈ li(x/k) − k/2 with Euler–Maclaurin gives mean ≈ ln 2 − (ln ln x − 2 ln 2 − γ) / ln x + O((ln ln x)² / ln² x), 2 ln 2 + γ = 1.9635. The correction is of order ln ln x / ln x; (ln ln x − 1.96)/ln x peaks near 10^8.4 at first order, which together with second-order terms puts the minimum near 10^10. Our model's gap times ln x minus ln ln x is −2.04, −2.02, −2.01 at 10^10, 10^12, 10^14, approaching −1.96 slowly. At 10^30 the gap would still be about 0.03.

Other measurements ([trains.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains.py) → [trains_1e5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_1e5.txt), [trains_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_1e6.txt)): f(n) on [x/2, x] is close to Poisson (variance / mean 0.98 for even and 0.97 for odd n at 10^6); odd n have more trains than even n (0.70 against 0.60) only because primes are trains of one (without the one-prime trains odd n have fewer: 0.55 against 0.60); and multiples of 3 have fewer trains (0.596 against 0.676 for the other numbers), which fake primes without the factor-3 structure do not show (0.64 to 0.66 in every class, two runs). The most trains up to 10^6 is 7, first at 218,918.

## 38. Prime compass ([prime20.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime20.html); Hecke's theorem and Rudnick–Waxman's conjecture are literature; the measurements are computed)

Every prime p ≡ 1 (mod 4) is a² + b²; the prime ideals of ℤ[i] with norm ≤ X give needles at the angles of a + bi and b + ai in [0, π/2), the ramified 1 + i at π/4 and the inert p ≡ 3 (mod 4) with p² ≤ X at 0 (Rudnick and Waxman's normalisation). Hecke (1918–1920) proved that the angles are equidistributed. Rudnick and Waxman (Israel J. Math. 2019, arXiv:1705.07498, Conjecture 1.2) conjecture that the number of angles in a sector of a fraction 1/K of [0, π/2) has variance ~ (N/K) min(1, 2 log K / log N) for 1 ≪ K ≪ N^{1 − o(1)}, against N/K for random angles. The page draws the needles of the primes up to 3,000, a rose of the counts in K sectors, and the variance / mean for every K = 2^j against log K / log N, for norms up to 10^5, 10^6 or 10^7, with fake needles (primitive lattice points (a, b), gcd(a, b) = 1, a² + b² odd, each kept with chance c / ln n so that their expected number is N). Statistic: the variance over 64 positions of the sector grid, averaged, divided by N/K. [pagerun20.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/pagerun20.js) and [rw.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw.py) agree on N and on the primes' variance / mean for every K at 10^6 and 10^7 (to the 3 printed decimals; [compare20.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/compare20.txt)).

Backtest ([rw.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw.py) → [rw_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw_1e6.txt), [rw_1e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw_1e7.txt), [rw_1e8.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw_1e8.txt); without the inert ideals: [rw_noinert_1000000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw_noinert_1000000.txt), [rw_noinert_10000000.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw_noinert_10000000.txt)). Prediction written before the run: the primes follow min(1, 2 log K / log N) roughly; the fakes stay near 1 minus the thinning. At norm 10^8 (N = 5,761,628) for K = 256, 1,024, 4,096, 65,536, 2^20 (log K / log N = 0.356, 0.445, 0.534, 0.712, 0.891): 0.363, 0.541, 0.668, 0.909, 0.930, against 0.712, 0.891, 1, 1, 1. The curve rises roughly linearly with slope 1.7 to 2.1 in log K / log N (from K = 64 to 1,024: 2.06; 256 to 2,048: 1.72), but in log K / log X the same data give 2.4, so the data cannot fix the slope; it keeps rising well past log K / log N = 1/2 and lies below the line 2 log K / log N by about 0.35 at 10^8 and 0.41 to 0.47 at 10^7 (0.24 at 10^8 if measured against 2 log K / ln X). Part of this is the 619 inert ideals at angle 0 (225 at 10^7, 87 at 10^6): they add (number of inert)² / N = 0.0665 to variance / mean at fine K and lower it at coarse K by filling the empty stretch near 0; without them the level for the finest sectors is 0.79 at 10^6, 0.84 at 10^7 and 0.86 at 10^8 instead of 0.88, 0.91 and 0.93 (the closeness of 0.93 to 1 − 1/ln N was a coincidence). Lower-order terms are expected: Rudnick and Waxman's function-field analogue (their Theorem 1.3) has 2β − 2/log_q N and 1 − (1 − η)/log_q N, and Chen, Kim, Lichtman, Miller, Shubina, Sweitzer, Waxman, Winsor and Yang (Exp. Math. 2020, arXiv:1901.07386) refine the conjecture with a negative constant term for the log-weighted count; we did not compare with their formula. So the prediction held for the rough shape and failed for the level.

Fakes ([rw_fakes.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw_fakes.py) → [rw_fakes_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw_fakes_1e6.txt), 10 sets at 10^6): their floor from the coin flips is 1 − Σp²/Σp = 0.751, but at K = 64, 256, 1,024, 4,096, 16,384 their mean is 0.765, 0.829, 0.900, 0.946, 0.931 (spreads 0.10, 0.06, 0.03, 0.017, 0.007), so "near the floor" held only for coarse sectors; the primes give 0.256, 0.459, 0.650, 0.825, 0.877, well below the fakes at every K from 64 on. So lattice points with independent coin flips do not reproduce the primes' evenness; this rules out that one null model. Rudnick and Waxman explain part of the rigidity by the lattice itself (their §2: two angles of norms N₁, N₂ differ by at least 1/√(N₁N₂)), and derive the variance from the zeros of Hecke L-functions. For the primes the 64-offset bin average equals the exact integral over θ to within 0.003 even at K = 16; only a single set of fakes scatters at small K (by 0.1 to 0.2).

## 39. Prime doorman ([prime21.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime21.html); Erdős's law is a theorem; the counts are computed; the explanation of the slow mean is a heuristic)

For an odd prime p the least quadratic non-residue n(p) is the first number "turned away" when 1, 2, 3, … knock and the squares mod p are let in; it is always a prime. The page shows the queue at the door of a chosen p (squares green, the first refused framed, the least primitive root g(p) framed in gold), finds the next record, and counts n(p) and g(p) for all odd primes up to 10^5, 10^6 or 10^7. Erdős (1961): the share of p with n(p) equal to the k-th prime tends to 2^{−k} (each condition "q is a square mod p" is, by quadratic reciprocity, a condition on p in residue classes, and the primes fill the classes evenly by Dirichlet), and with a bound on the rare large n(p) (the real content of Erdős's proof) the mean of n(p) tends to Σ_k p_k / 2^k = 3.674644 (OEIS A098990). [ref21.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/ref21.py) (sympy's legendre_symbol and primitive_root) and [pagerun21.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/pagerun21.js) agree on the count of every value of n(p) and on the sums of n(p) and g(p) up to 10^5 and 10^6 ([compare21.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/compare21.txt)).

Backtest ([door.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/door.py) → [door_1e5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/door_1e5.txt), [door_1e6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/door_1e6.txt)). Prediction written before the run: the shares are within 1 % of 2^{−k} for the first values, the mean is near 3.67 but approached from below. Shares up to 10^6 (78,497 primes) for n(p) = 2, 3, 5, 7, 11: 0.50035, 0.25025, 0.12520, 0.06265, 0.03142 (2^{−k}: 0.5, 0.25, 0.125, 0.0625, 0.03125). Mean n(p): 3.60035 up to 10^5 and 3.64614 up to 10^6, below 3.674644 and rising. The largest n(p) is 31 (first at 31,391) up to 10^5 and 43 (at 366,791) up to 10^6. Why from below (heuristic, from an independent review, checked here on our counts): a Chebyshev-type bias. For each non-trivial real character χ the sum Σ_{p ≤ x} χ(p) is about −½ π(√x) (the prime squares in the explicit formula), so each set {p : n(p) > q_k} is short by about ½ π(√x) (1 − 2^{−k}) primes. At 10^6 (½ π(10^3) = 84) the sets n(p) > 3 and n(p) > 7 hold 19,577 and 4,831 primes against 19,624.25 and 4,906.06 for 2^{−k}: short by 47 and 75, against 63 and 79 from the bias; the set n(p) > 7 is short although its period 840 is filled about 1,190 times below 10^6. A model with only this bias for every tail set ([door_bias.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/door_bias.py) → [door_bias.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/door_bias.txt): tail_k = M/2^k − ½ π(√x)(1 − 2^{−k}), clipped at 0) predicts shortfalls 42, 63, 74, 79, 81, 83, 83, 84 for n(p) > 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19 at 10^6 against the measured 27, 47, 63, 75, 88, 82, 78, 75, and a mean of 3.5907 at 10^5 and 3.6409 at 10^6 against the measured 3.6004 and 3.6461: it accounts for the whole gap to 3.674644 and slightly more (113 % and 119 %). The largest values of n(p) are pseudosquare-like primes (9,257,329, with n = 53, is a Lehmer pseudosquare); the same bias may be behind the short last bins of the square-remainder board in section 25 (not tested). Mean least primitive root: 4.72693 up to 10^5, 4.88184 up to 10^6, also rising; Elliott and Murata (J. London Math. Soc. 1997) conjectured that it tends to a constant (we have not checked their value).

## 40. Prime window ([prime22.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime22.html); Ramanujan's theorem and the limit 1/2 are literature; the identity is elementary; the counts are computed)

The window (x/2, x] slides along the numbers; s(x) = π(x) − π(x/2) primes are inside. Ramanujan (1919) proved s(x) → ∞; the n-th Ramanujan prime R_n is the least number with s(x) ≥ n for every x ≥ R_n (Sondow 2009; OEIS A104272: 2, 11, 17, 29, 41, …). The page draws the window with its primes, s(x) and its running minimum from x onward, and marks the R_n. From the definition, R_n ≤ x exactly when s(y) ≥ n for all y ≥ x, so, the R_n being strictly increasing and s → ∞ (Ramanujan), the number of Ramanujan primes up to x is π_R(x) = min_{y ≥ x} s(y) (elementary; s is constant between integers, so real or integer x does not matter; checked by counting at 10^3 to 10^6). The programs take the minimum over y ≤ 2 · 10^7, which suffices by Sondow's bound R_n < 4n ln 4n (for n = 316,067 it is below 1.78 · 10^7). The counts 72, 559, 4,459, 36,960, 316,066 at 10^3 … 10^7 are OEIS A181671. [ram2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/ram2.py) and [pagerun22.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/pagerun22.js) agree on the 316,066 Ramanujan primes up to 10^7 (the largest 9,999,973), on π_R at 10^3 to 10^6, and on the first 20 against A104272 ([compare22.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/compare22.txt)).

The share of the primes that are Ramanujan primes ([ram2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/ram2.py) → [ram2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/ram2.txt); [ram.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/ram.py) → [ram.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/ram.txt)): 0.4286, 0.4548, 0.4649, 0.4708 at 10^3, 10^4, 10^5, 10^6, below the limit 1/2 (Amersi, Beckwith, Miller, Ronan and Sondow, Springer PMS 101 (2014); noted by Sondow, Nicholson and Noe, J. Integer Seq. 14 (2011)). The fluctuations of s(y) are tiny next to its growth (at 10^5, 10^6, 10^7 even π_R(x) = s(x) exactly), so the share is close to (Li(x) − Li(x/2)) / Li(x), Li(x) = ∫_2^x dt/ln t: 0.4294, 0.4513, 0.4635, 0.4708 at the same x. With the prime number theorem with error term, the identity gives π_R(x) = Li(x) − Li(x/2) + O(x e^{−c√(ln x)}) (upper bound: π_R(x) ≤ s(x); lower bound: Li(y) − Li(y/2) increases with y), hence the limit 1/2 at once, and share = 1/2 − ln 2/(2 ln x) − (ln² 2 + ln 2)/(2 ln² x) + … (expanding Li). So (1/2 − share) · ln x ≈ 0.347 + 0.587/ln x = 0.432, 0.411, 0.398, 0.389 at 10^3 … 10^6, against the measured 0.49, 0.42, 0.40, 0.40. R_n / p_{2n} for n = 10, 100, 1,000, 10,000: 1.366, 1.177, 1.116, 1.077, tending to 1 (R_n ~ p_{2n}, Sondow).

## 41. Prime high jump ([prime23.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime23.html); the record list is OEIS data; Cramér's conjecture and Wolf's formula are literature; the fake-prime comparison is computed)

Every gap between consecutive primes longer than all earlier gaps is a record (OEIS A005250, with the primes before them in A002386; the b-files list 85 records, the last a gap of 1,854 after 101,412,319,996,363,309,069). The page plots the records, as gap / ln² p or as the gap, against p, with Cramér's ln² p (1936), the largest of π(x) exponential gaps of mean ln x, ln x · ln π(x) (Cramér's random model), and Wolf's formula (x / π(x)) (2 ln π(x) − ln x + ln C₂), C₂ = 0.66016 (π replaced by Li on the page). The page only draws published data and formulas; there is no page computation to compare.

Records against the formulas ([jump2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/highjump/jump2.py) → [jump2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/highjump/jump2.txt); the 74 records with p ≥ 10^4): record / Wolf has mean 1.060 (0.972 to 1.422), record / (ln p ln π(p)) mean 0.841 (0.606 to 1.024), record / ln² p mean 0.736 (0.471 to 0.921); the largest gap / ln² p is 0.9206 at p = 1,693,182,318,746,371 (gap 1,132). So the records stay below Cramér's ln² p and below the maximum of random gaps, and follow Wolf's formula, which they exceed slightly as new maxima should.

Fakes ([jump.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/highjump/jump.py) → [jump.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/highjump/jump.txt); 6 runs each up to 10^9). Prediction written before the run: the primes' largest gaps lie below those of Cramér-model fakes and close to Wolf's formula; fakes with a wheel come closer. Largest gap below x = 10^6, 10^7, 10^8, 10^9: primes 114, 154, 220, 282; Wolf 105.9, 154.7, 213.4, 282.2; odd Cramér fakes 145, 207, 262, 347 (spreads 10 to 19); fakes avoiding 2 to 13: 123, 207, 245, 310; avoiding the primes up to 100: 118, 171, 236, 305 (spreads 10 to 19). The prediction held: the primes' record gaps are the smallest, the deeper wheels come closer but stay above the primes at 10^8 and 10^9 by 1 to 2 spreads. This is the extreme-value face of the regular gaps of sections 26 and 34; Granville's argument that the true limsup of gap / ln² p is at least 2e^{−γ} ≈ 1.12 concerns sizes far beyond these (computed; literature).

## 42. Prime impostors ([prime24.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime24.html); the counts are computed and agree with published tables; Pomerance's heuristic is literature)

Fermat's check: a prime p has 2^{p−1} ≡ 1 (mod p). Composite numbers that pass are the base-2 pseudoprimes (OEIS A001567: 341, 561, 645, 1105, …); Carmichael numbers (A002997: 561, 1105, 1729, …) pass for every base coprime to them (Korselt: square-free, p − 1 | n − 1 for all p | n, at least three prime factors). The page runs the check for a chosen n and the bases 2 to 37, lists the impostors up to 10^7 with the Carmichael numbers marked, and plots the published counts below 10^n (A055550 to 10^19, A055553 to 10^24) against the primes. [impostors.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/impostors/impostors.py) (square-and-multiply on numpy arrays, Korselt's criterion) counts up to 10^9: 3, 22, 78, 245, 750, 2,057, 5,597 pseudoprimes and 1, 7, 16, 43, 105, 255, 646 Carmichael numbers below 10^3 … 10^9 ([imp_1e9.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/impostors/imp_1e9.txt)), equal to the OEIS values; [pagerun24.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/impostors/pagerun24.js) (the page's own code) agrees up to 10^7 ([compare24.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/impostors/compare24.txt); note that A055550 and A055553 start at n = 1, a(n) counting below 10^n).

How rare ([pomerance.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/impostors/pomerance.py) → [pomerance.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/impostors/pomerance.txt)). Pomerance's heuristic (1981) gives x^{1 − (1 + o(1)) ln ln ln x / ln ln x} Carmichael numbers below x; with the o(1) left out it overshoots the true count by a factor 145 at 10^6, 789 at 10^9, 3 · 10^5 at 10^18 and 1.6 · 10^7 at 10^24, a gap that keeps growing over the whole published range. The count of Carmichael numbers grows by a factor 2.29 to 2.69 per decade (lowest 2.29 from 10^11 to 10^12, then rising slowly to 2.50 at 10^24), the base-2 pseudoprimes by 2.60 to 3.14 (lowest 2.60 near 10^13, then rising to 2.70 at 10^19): far from the factor 10 that an exponent near 1 would need (computed from the published counts).

## 43. Euclid's orchard ([prime25.html](writeup/code/mersenne/prime25.html); 6/π² and Mertens' bound are classical; the error statistics are computed)

Trees stand at the lattice points (a, b), 1 ≤ a, b ≤ N; from the origin a tree is visible exactly when gcd(a, b) = 1. The page draws the orchard with a few lines of sight and plots D(N)/N, where V(N) = 2 Σ_{n ≤ N} φ(n) − 1 is the number of visible trees and D(N) = V(N) − (6/π²) N². Each prime p hides a share 1/p² of the pairs, and the visible share tends to ∏_p (1 − 1/p²) = 6/π²; Mertens (1874) proved D(N) = O(N log N), Walfisz sharpened it to O(N (log N)^{2/3} (log log N)^{4/3}). [ref25.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/orchard/ref25.py) (the φ sieve, checked against direct gcd counting for N ≤ 100) and [pagerun25.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/orchard/pagerun25.js) agree on V(24) = 359, V(10^6) = 607,927,104,783 and on the statistics of D(N)/N over [10^4, 10^6] ([compare25.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/orchard/compare25.txt)).

Measured ([orchard.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/orchard/orchard.py) → [orchard_1e7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/orchard/orchard_1e7.txt), N up to 10^7): V(10^7) = 60,792,712,854,483, so V/N² = 0.60792713 against 6/π² = 0.60792710. D(N)/N behaves like a stationary wobble: over [10, 10^3], [10^3, 10^5] and [10^5, 10^7] its mean is 0.6050, 0.6079, 0.6079, its spread 0.2814, 0.2819, 0.2819, it lies between −0.183 and 1.390, and it is positive for 99.8 % of the N; the largest |D|/(N ln N) for N ≥ 100 is 0.25. Why the mean is 6/π² (heuristic derivation, exchanging the average over N with the sum over d): V(N) = Σ_{d ≤ N} μ(d) ⌊N/d⌋², and with ⌊N/d⌋ = N/d − {N/d} this is N² Σ μ(d)/d² − 2N Σ μ(d) {N/d}/d + Σ μ(d) {N/d}². For whole numbers N the fractional part {N/d} runs through 0, 1/d, …, (d − 1)/d, with mean 1/2 − 1/(2d), so the middle term averages to −N Σ μ(d)/d + N Σ μ(d)/d², that is about 0 + (6/π²) N; the first term is (6/π²) N² + O(N) with a mean correction that vanishes (Σ_{d > N} μ(d)/d² averages to 0), and the last is o(N) on average (Σ μ(d) = o(N)). So D(N)/N averages to 6/π², a consequence of N being a whole number. We did not find this remark in one search; the error term of Σ φ(n) has a large literature.

An overview page, [primes.html](writeup/code/mersenne/primes.html), links the pages prime.html to prime25.html with one line each.

## Validation

- [ll_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/ll_check.py) → [ll_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/ll_check.txt): Lucas–Lehmer (gmpy2) on all 1,229 primes below 10⁴ gives exactly the 22 exponents.
- [engine/page_check.js](writeup/code/mersenne/engine/page_check.js) and [page_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/engine/page_check.py) → [page_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/engine/page_check.txt), on the functions cut out of the page itself: the chain of 142 from 1 is exactly the 23 exponents up to 11,213 and every start 2 … 11,212 gives the next one; the test on every prime below 12,000 finds exactly them, and all 759 factors it reports divide 2^q − 1; the FFT residues of 18 primes from 4,201 to 30,011 equal those of gmpy2 (largest rounding error 0.0215); the zero test of the verifier is right in 46 of 46 cases.
- [engine/test_best.js](writeup/code/mersenne/engine/test_best.js) → [test_best.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/engine/test_best.txt): the readable engine, the same checks plus 36 FFT residues against BigInt.
- [structure/check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/structure/check.py) rechecks section 3; [touch/verify.py](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/verify.py) → [verify.out](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/verify.out): Lucas–Lehmer confirms the 28 exponents below 10⁵, and below 20,000 every prime is either removed by a proved factor or tested (761 tests), leaving exactly the listed exponents. [tfcheck.py](writeup/code/mersenne/touch/tfcheck.py) checks the factor tables against a plain search.
- Section 6: [numbers/page143_check.js](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/page143_check.js) → [page143_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/page143_check.txt) checks transformation 143 on the page's own functions; [numbers/verify.py](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/verify.py) → [verify.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/numbers/verify.txt) recomputes the best rules in plain integers on the real and the 500 null sequences; [relations/check_real.py](writeup/code/mersenne/relations/check_real.py) → [check_real.out](writeup/code/mersenne/relations/check_real.out) recomputes every statistic of the real sequence with other algorithms (exact ranks, a plain Lempel–Ziv, Myhill–Nerode for the automata), and [check_base23.py](writeup/code/mersenne/relations/check_base23.py) → [check_base23.out](writeup/code/mersenne/relations/check_base23.out) the base-23 p-values.
- Section 7: every round has an independent recheck script in its folder ([r3_early/theory.py](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_early/theory.py), [r3_arith/recheck.py](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_arith/recheck.py), [r3_cross/recheck.py](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_cross/recheck.py), [r4_lines/recheck.py](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r4_lines/recheck.py), [r4_index/check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r4_index/check.py), [r5_transfer/recheck_tests.py](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r5_transfer/recheck_tests.py)) and its outputs beside it; the pre-registration files of rounds 3b and 3c are [r3_arith/prereg.json](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_arith/prereg.json) and [r3_cross/prereg.json](writeup/code/mersenne/rounds/r3_cross/prereg.json).
- Section 10: a second recheck, [theories/check2/](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/check2/) ([RESULTS.md](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/check2/RESULTS.md)), recomputes theories 3, 5, 7, 9b, 10 and 11 and the mod-8 Goldbach means: the byte counts, χ² values, entropies and shared information by its own sieve and Hardy–Littlewood code, the Mertens predictor, A = 0.538241 as the product (7/12)·Π_(r≥3) r(r² − r − 1)/((r − 1)²(r + 1)) and the share 1/5 as a proof, the 14 lowest zeros by PARI's lfunzeros and the variances V three ways (Hurwitz zeta, PARI, a sum over the zeros below 400), and all 23 numbers of theory 9b (both moduli, its own FFT and counts), and on the range to 10⁹ the exact bin means of Δ and −S in four bins, the four correlations (pred sampled, 20,000 n per bin) and the sampled predictions ([g_bins.py](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/check2/g_bins.py)): all agree (one value in the request had been copied from the neighbouring bin; the output file is right). - Section 10: [theories/check/](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/check/) recomputes the numbers of theories 1, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9 and 10 with its own scripts and methods (a plain pow test for every k, two independent septuplet searches, a full sieve to 10⁹, a smallest-prime-factor sieve with sympy's n_order up to 10⁵, exact Goldbach means without FFT; [RESULTS.md](writeup/code/mersenne/theories/check/RESULTS.md)), and the lane weights of theory 1 with its own model code: all agree.
- Section 11: [a_analysis.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/a_analysis.py), [a_analysis2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/a_analysis2.py), [p6_mirror.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/p6_mirror.py), [p7_classcov.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/p7_classcov.py), [p4.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/p4.py) and [a_surv_rhythm.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/a_surv_rhythm.py) recompute every number from scratch with numpy, sympy and mpmath, independently of the page's JavaScript; outputs in [logs/](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/).
- Section 12: the five layouts without a sieve by [lean_profile.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/lean_profile.py), [lean_cutoff.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/lean_cutoff.py), [curve.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/curve.py), [tree.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/tree.py), [misc.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/misc.py), and again from scratch by [round3_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/round3_check.py) (sympy factorisations, Euler's criterion, PARI, plain Python); the ten puzzle layouts by [exp_scan.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/exp_scan.py), [exp_class.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/exp_class.py) and [round4_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/round4_check.py) (gmpy2 with 25 rounds for the tables, so their primes above 2^64 are PRP). Every count agrees with the page's own. Outputs in [logs/](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/logs/). The numbers of the ten shape layouts: [shapes_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/shapes_check.py).
- Section 13: [score.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/score.js) and [gensearch.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/gensearch.js) run in headless Chrome against the page; [analyse.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/analyse.py) and [analyse_gen.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/backtest/analyse_gen.py) print the tables. The square effect is counted independently in numpy by [quad_deep.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_deep.py), [quad_probe.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_probe.py) (with two block sizes for the decoy density) and [quad_surv.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_surv.py); the exponent backtest by [exp_backtest.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/exp_backtest.py). The cause and scope of the square effect: [quad_fermat.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_fermat.py), [quad_scope.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/check/quad_scope.py).
- Section 14: [check5.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/check5.py) → [check5.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/check5.txt) recomputes the primes column with a plain Python sieve and other code (the scatter in windows with li removed instead of a fitted curve: 0.345) and two draws of the class model with another generator; [check5b.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/check5b.py) → [check5b.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/model/check5b.txt) counts Goldbach sums directly for 1,500 random n (0.274 for the primes, 0.326 and 0.296 for two draws) and finds the first factors of every 8th prime with a primality test in place of the remainder filter (737 primes, no difference).
- Section 15: [check6.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/check6.py) → [check6.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/layers/check6.txt) recomputes every number with plain loops and sympy: the pair counts and the counts of p + 2q by direct lookup instead of a transform (93.5 %, 98.4 %, 66.8 % for the page setting, and 66.4 % with steps 2.10, 1.37, 1.22, 1.13 on 16 random blocks), the layer shares, the pair information, three biases by direct parity counts (0.0542, 0.0335, 0.0393) and the correlation on 150 of the 1,000 strongest sets (0.962), the fold information by plain remainders, the depths with sympy's order function, and the table (each entry prime and of order w; 2^w − 1 fully split by the rows d | w for every w up to 200).
- Section 16: [check7.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/check7.py) → [check7.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carry/check7.txt) multiplies the patterns as coefficient lists, counts the primitive roots with plain powers, decides "the strip divides n without carries" by a fold (n xor 2n, folded in w layers, is 0) instead of a division, tests the 20-bit patterns with Rabin's irreducibility test, rotates and counts runs on strings, and lists the all-ones numbers below 10¹² in another order.
- Section 18: [check_machines.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/check_machines.py) → [check_machines.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/check_machines.txt) and [check_machines2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/check_machines2.py) → [check_machines2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/check_machines2.txt) move every wheel one tick at a time for 100,000 ticks (no closed forms) and give the same quiet shares, prime shares, records and first ticks for all eight rules with 30, 100 and 250 gears; [check_dial.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/check_dial.py) does the dial and [check_pairs.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/check_pairs.py) the pairs and the doubling sieve.
- Section 19: [bt_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt_check.py) → [bt_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/bt_check.txt) redoes the dial for every number of hands and every denominator, the Fibonacci wheels with sympy up to 2,000, the powers of two on the accelerating wheels and the slipping wheels up to 300.
- Section 20: [tour_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check.py) → [tour_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check.txt) redoes the contest in plain Python with lists of integers ([ref.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ref.py)): the list below 10^7, Pascal's wheel on every candidate below 10^10, on every 4,000th line of the published list up to 2^64 and on 3,001 primes of 8 to 64 bits, each ring on the candidates that do not fit it, the pair of wheels, the single wheels below 10^6 and the two lemmas. [tour_check2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check2.py) → [tour_check2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check2.txt) does the same for the two-cell wheel with sympy's Jacobi symbol, [pascal_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/pascal_check.py) → [pascal_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/pascal_check.txt) checks what the page shows, and [big_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/big_check.py) compares the C program with Python on 65 numbers near 2^64, and [beyond64.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/beyond64.py) → [beyond64.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/beyond64.txt) tries 10,000 built numbers above 2^64. [count_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/count_check.py) → [count_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/count_check.txt) confirms with PARI's prime count that each range run tested exactly the odd numbers that are not prime. [tour_check3.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check3.py) → [tour_check3.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour_check3.txt) puts both wheels on 320,000 random odd numbers, primes and others, and compares with sympy. The runs up to 2^64 were made once by [tour.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/tour.c) (logs big_*.err in the same folder; [ring2.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring2.c) repeated the rings whose big_ring2_*.err log is present); they need the published list (883 MB, address and checksum in [list_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/list_check.txt)). Second round: [ring3_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring3_check.py) → [ring3_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring3_check.txt) checks the faster ring program against the known mistakes and against plain Python; [fold_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_check.py) → [fold_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_check.txt) does the same for the folded wheels and shows that folding throws no mistake away; [fold_units.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_units.py) → [fold_units.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold_units.txt) checks the folding facts exactly; [agrawal_sign.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/agrawal_sign.py) → [agrawal_sign.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/agrawal_sign.txt) checks the sign lemma; [three_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_check.py) → [three_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/three_check.txt) the three-cell wheel; [duo_mersenne.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/duo_mersenne.py) → [duo_mersenne.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/duo_mersenne.txt) the Lucas–Lehmer statement; [proved_wheels.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/proved_wheels.py) → [proved_wheels.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/proved_wheels.txt) the proved wheels. The runs over the list by [ring3.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/ring3.c) and [fold.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/fold.c) were made once (logs big3_*.err, big4_*.err, three_list_*.err). For the chain of wheels, [chain_verify.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_verify.py) checks every certificate built by [chain_wheel.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_wheel.py) and by the page's code ([chain.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/chain.js), run by [chain_node.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/chain_node.js)) and shares no code with either; [chain_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_summary.py) → [chain.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain.txt) collects the results of [chain_test.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_test.py), [chain_test_big.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_test_big.py), [chain_adversary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_adversary.py) and [chain_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/chain_js_check.py). For the test with a receipt, [receipt_test.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_test.py) → [receipt_test.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_test.txt) compares both forms with the plain Lucas–Lehmer test, tampers with results and receipts and forges receipts; [receipt_js_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_js_check.py) → [receipt_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_js_check.txt) checks the page's code ([receipt.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/receipt.js)) against [receipt.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt.py) in both directions; [receipt_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt_summary.py) → [receipt.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/receipt.txt) collects the results. For the wheel from a start of norm 2, [norm2_list_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_list_check.py) → [norm2_list_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_list_check.txt) recomputes every 4,000th line of the run over the list in plain Python and compares the line count, the last number and the checksum with the earlier runs; [norm2_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_check.py) → [norm2_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_check.txt) tests the norm lemma; [pcount.c](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/pcount.c) counts the primes below 10^10 for the comparison with the direct run; [norm2_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2_summary.py) → [norm2.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/norm2.txt) collects the results. The four machines that find primes before their tick: [early.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early.py) → [early.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early.txt) (squaring wheel against the stepping wheel, the sieve against a separate sieve, the counters against sympy), [early_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_summary.py) → [early_sum.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_sum.txt) (the counts of the C run per power of two against the counting recursion), [inherit.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit.py) → [inherit.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit.txt) (real proofs for every odd number up to 200,000), [inherit_summary.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_summary.py) → [inherit_sum.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_sum.txt) (the C run up to 4.2·10^9, the run from the top down with PARI, the fit), and the page code against Python in [early_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/early_js_check.txt) and [inherit_js_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/gears/tour/inherit_js_check.txt).
- Section 21: [mexp_gaps_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/mexp_gaps_check.py) → [mexp_gaps_check.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/mexp_gaps_check.txt) recomputes the gaps, the size statistics and every remainder count of [mexp_gaps.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/mexp_gaps.py) with sympy and plain integers and compares model A with n/φ(m); [page_check_24.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/expgaps/page_check_24.txt) checks the counts of machine 24 on the page.

- Section 29: [ref11.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/ref11.py), [pagerun11.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/pagerun11.js), [compare11.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/compare11.txt), [singular_check.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/radio/singular_check.py) the radio: station strengths and noise levels agree to 10^−6; the singular-series identity checked for six n.
- Section 30: [ref12.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/ref12.py), [pagerun12.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/pagerun12.js), [compare12.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/rain/compare12.txt) the rain: every cloud, dry count and forecast agree up to 5,000 and 10^6.
- Section 31: [ref13.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/ref13.py), [pagerun13.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/pagerun13.js), [compare13.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/dance/compare13.txt) the dance: all transition counts agree for six moduli up to 5,000, 10^6, 10^7.
- Section 32: [ref14.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/ref14.py), [pagerun14.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/pagerun14.js), [compare14.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/census/compare14.txt) the census: bars, mean, variance, pair sum and distances agree up to 5,000, 10^6, 10^7.
- Section 33: [ref15.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/ref15.py), [pagerun15.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/pagerun15.js), [compare15.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/carousel/compare15.txt) the carousel: primitive-root counts (sympy) agree for ten bases up to 5,000, 10^6, 10^7.
- Section 34: [ref16.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/ref16.py), [pagerun16.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/pagerun16.js), [compare16.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/baskets/compare16.txt) the baskets: all spreads agree for 7 widths at 10^5 and 10^6.
- Section 35: [ref17.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/ref17.py), [pagerun17.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/pagerun17.js), [compare17.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/bricks/compare17.txt) the bricks: Ψ (built by products, not by a sieve), x ρ(u) and Λ agree for six (x, y) pairs up to 10^7.
- Section 36: [ref18.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/ref18.py), [pagerun18.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/pagerun18.js), [compare18.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/spot/compare18.txt) the game: the five statistics of the true primes agree in six fixed strips.
- Section 37: [trains_count.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/trains_count.py), [pagerun19.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/pagerun19.js), [compare19.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/trains/compare19.txt) the trains: exact counts at 10^3 to 10^7 agree; the counts to 10^9 and the smooth model are in the section.
- Section 38: [rw.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/rw.py), [pagerun20.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/pagerun20.js), [compare20.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/compass/compare20.txt) the compass: the number of needles and the variance of the primes agree for every K at 10^6 and 10^7.
- Section 39: [ref21.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/ref21.py), [pagerun21.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/pagerun21.js), [compare21.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/doorman/compare21.txt) the doorman: counts of every least non-residue and the sums of n(p) and g(p) agree up to 10^5 and 10^6.
- Section 40: [ram2.py](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/ram2.py), [pagerun22.js](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/pagerun22.js), [compare22.txt](writeup/code/mersenne/strategies/ten/window/compare22.txt) the window: the Ramanujan primes up to 10^7, the counts and the first 20 against OEIS A104272 agree.
